# Sūrah Yā Sīn (Chapter 36) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Heart and Its Place in the Revelation**

Sūrah Yā Sīn takes its name from the two isolated Arabic letters — *yāʾ* and *sīn* — that open its first verse. It is a Makkan sūrah from the early part of the middle Makkan period, revealed when the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and the small band of believers were surrounded by rejection in Makkah yet before the persecution reached its most intense phase. All scholars agree on its Makkan character, though some commentators hold that verse 12, with its reference to recording footsteps to the mosque, may belong to the Madinan period when the community in Madinah was established around the Prophet's mosque. In the ordering of the muṣḥaf it stands as the thirty-sixth chapter, with eighty-three verses, placed among the series of Makkan sūrahs that build the foundations of faith: the oneness and power of God, the truth of prophethood, and the certainty of the Resurrection and Judgment.

The sūrah is known throughout the Muslim world by a title of honour: *Qalb al-Qurʾān*, "The Heart of the Qur'an." This name comes from a well-known report:

> *"Everything has a heart, and the heart of the Qur'an is Yā Sīn. Whosoever recites Yā Sīn, God records for him the recitation of the Qur'an ten times for his recitation of it."* (al-Tirmidhī; also cited by al-Qurṭubī)

Scholars have reflected deeply on why this particular chapter earned such a title. The heart in the human body is small in size yet central in function: it pumps life to every limb, and if it stops, the whole body dies. Yā Sīn is likewise compact — it can be recited in some fifteen to twenty minutes — yet it pumps the central lifeblood of the Qur'anic message to every part of belief. As several commentators observe, it addresses the three cardinal doctrines around which the entire Qur'an revolves: *tawḥīd* (the oneness, power, and mercy of God), *nubuwwah* (the truth of the messengers and of Muhammad ﷺ in particular), and *maʿād* (the Return, Resurrection, and recompense). Whoever understands Yā Sīn understands, in concentrated form, what the whole Qur'an teaches.

**A Sūrah Woven Into Muslim Life and Death**

Few chapters of the Qur'an are as present in the daily and critical moments of Muslim life as Yā Sīn. Many Muslims recite it regularly as part of supererogatory devotion; for large numbers of believers it is the longest sūrah they have memorized in full, learned in childhood and carried for a lifetime. It is recited morning and evening, in sickness and in health, and above all at the threshold of death. A famous ḥadīth instructs:

> *"Recite Yā Sīn over your dead."* (Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah; cited by al-Qurṭubī and al-Suyūṭī)

On the basis of this report, Yā Sīn is recited for those close to death, for those who have just died, and at the graves of loved ones. Another report promises intercession and forgiveness connected with it:

> *"Verily in the Qur'an there is a sūrah that intercedes through its recitation and forgives through its being heard — indeed, that is Sūrat Yā Sīn."* (Cited by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī)

And concerning the night recitation:

> *"Whosoever recites Sūrat Yā Sīn at night, desiring the Face of God, is forgiven during that night."* (Cited by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī)

The morning recitation, though often repeated as a ḥadīth, most likely derives from a saying attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās:

> *"Whosoever recites Yā Sīn when he awakens is given ease for his day until the evening comes. And whosoever reads it in the midst of the night is given ease for his night until he awakens."* (Cited by al-Qurṭubī)

Whether every one of these reports reaches the highest grade of authenticity or not — the scholars of ḥadīth have graded them variously — their collective weight explains fourteen centuries of practice. The logic is luminous: a sūrah whose climax is the ease with which God revives dead bones (verses 77–83) is the natural companion of the dying, the dead, and the grieving. It speaks resurrection into the room where death seems final.

**How the Chapter Is Built**

The sūrah moves in six great waves, each flowing into the next. Verses 1–12 open with an address to the Prophet ﷺ that establishes both his mission and the nature of the revelation he carries, closing with the register in which every deed and trace is recorded. Verses 13–30 then set forth the parable of the town to which messengers were sent — the narrative heart of the sūrah — with the lone believing man who runs from the outskirts of the city, his martyrdom, and the instant destruction of the rejecters. Verses 31–44 widen from history to cosmology: the destroyed generations, the dead earth revived by rain, the pairs in creation, the night stripped of day, the sun and moon in their orbits, and the ships that carry mankind — a gallery of signs proving that the God who creates and sustains can surely resurrect. Verses 45–52 answer the stock objections of the disbelievers — their refusal to spend, their mocking question "When will this promise come?" — and describe the single Cry that will seize them mid-dispute and the trumpet blast that will raise them. Verses 53–68 contrast the two final abodes: the rejoicing inhabitants of the Garden, greeted with "Peace!" from a Merciful Lord, and the guilty ones driven toward Hell, their own limbs testifying against them. The sūrah then returns to the Prophet ﷺ (verses 69–70), defending the revelation against the charge of poetry, and closes (verses 71–83) with cattle and provision as signs, a final consolation to the Prophet, the parable of the man with rotted bones, and the majestic affirmation of the creative Command — *"Be!" and it is* — ending with dominion returned to its Owner.

**Reading What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the eighty-three verses as an independent discourse in strict order. Verses that the source commentary treats in groups (such as 3–4, 10–11, 13–14, 33–34, 60–61, 63–64, 71–73, and 78–79) are here separated and given full individual treatment, exactly as required. Nothing has been compressed because of the chapter's length. Each verse is quoted, then expanded through Arabic language, Qur'anic cross-references with their actual text, authenticated Prophetic reports with collections identified, the views of the classical commentators, the logic of its teaching, and its bearing on the inner life and the modern world. The pace is unhurried, for haste is the opposite of what this sūrah teaches: from *Yā. Sīn.* to *"unto Whom you shall be returned,"* the reader walks the whole path of the heart.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:1

> **Yā. Sīn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Letters at the Door of the Heart**

The sūrah known as the heart of the Qur'an opens not with a sentence but with two isolated letters of the Arabic alphabet, recited by their names: *Yā. Sīn.* These belong to the *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*, "the disconnected letters," found at the beginning of twenty-nine sūrahs — from the single letters *Qāf*, *Nūn*, and *Ṣād* to the five-letter opening of Sūrah al-Shūrā, *Ḥā Mīm ʿAyn Sīn Qāf*. The majority of scholars, from the Companions onward, held that the definitive meaning of these letters is known only to God, and they are recited as an act of worshipful submission before understanding begins. It is transmitted from Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq (d. 13/634):

> *"Every book has a mystery, and the mystery of the Qur'an is in the openings of the sūrahs."*

That a chapter called the "heart" should begin with a mystery is itself instructive. The physical heart is hidden within the chest; its workings are felt rather than seen. So too Yā Sīn opens with sounds whose full sense is veiled, inviting the listener into humility before the revelation unfolds its clearest teachings about God, prophethood, and the Last Day. Before the mind grasps, the tongue recites and the heart listens.

**"O Little Human Being": A Vocative of Tenderness**

One beautiful interpretation, recorded by al-Qurṭubī, reads the two letters as an Arabic address: the *yāʾ* is the vocative particle "O," used in countless Qur'anic verses (*yā ayyuhā al-nās*, "O mankind"; *yā ʿibādī*, "O My servants"), and the *sīn* is an abbreviation of *unsayn*, the diminutive of *insān* ("human being"). On this reading, *Yā Sīn* means "O little human being" — a diminutive of endearment, God's tender address to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. The diminutive in Arabic often expresses affection rather than belittlement, as a father might call his son "my little man." If this is the sense, then the sūrah opens the way a compassionate speaker begins difficult news: with gentleness toward the bearer of the message before describing the heaviness of the people's rejection. The Prophet ﷺ, who would be told within a few verses that most of his people will not believe (36:7) and counseled not to let their speech grieve him (36:76), is first gathered up in divine affection. Every subsequent command to warn, to persist, and to trust is thus framed inside love.

**A Proper Name Given by God to His Prophet**

Others say that *Yā Sīn* is a name by which God Himself named the Prophet ﷺ, its exact meaning unknown — and for this reason it is used in the Muslim world as a boy's name to this day. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib is reported to have said:

> *"I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say, 'Verily God has named me by seven names in the Qur'an: Muḥammad [3:144; 33:40; 47:2; 48:29], Aḥmad [61:6], Ṭā Hā [20:1], Yā Sīn [36:1], thou enwrapped [al-Muzzammil; 73:1], thou who art covered [al-Mudaththir; 74:1], and servant of God [ʿAbd Allāh; 72:19].'"* (Cited by Ibn ʿAṭiyyah, al-Qurṭubī)

This report, whether taken as naming in the strict sense or as designations of honour, binds the opening of the sūrah directly to verse 3 — *"truly thou art among the message bearers"* — so that the chapter begins by addressing the Messenger and immediately confirms his messengership. The oath of verse 2 stands between the name and the confirmation like a seal between the envelope and the letter. Other commentators take *Yā Sīn* to be a name of the Qur'an itself, while al-Qushayrī (d. 465/1072) identifies it with the Day of the Covenant (*yawm al-mīthāq*), when God made a covenant with all the children of Adam:

> *"And when thy Lord took from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny and made them bear witness concerning themselves, 'Am I not your Lord?' They said, 'Yea, indeed, we bear witness.'"* (Qur'an 7:172)

On this mystical reading, the sūrah opens by recalling the primordial encounter between every soul and its Lord — fitting for a chapter that will close with every soul's return to Him (36:83).

**Why the Letters Silence the Objection Before It Is Spoken**

Ibn Kathīr and the majority note a pattern: in most sūrahs opened by disconnected letters, mention of the Book or revelation follows immediately. Here, *"Yā. Sīn. By the Wise Qur'an"* — the letters herald the revelation as a trumpet heralds a proclamation. The classical explanation associated with Ibn ʿAbbās treats the letters as a standing demonstration of the Qur'an's inimitability (*iʿjāz*): they are the Arabs' own alphabet, the very material from which their poets wove their celebrated odes, yet from these same letters has come a speech they cannot match. The challenge issued explicitly elsewhere — *"bring a sūrah like it"* (2:23; 10:38) — is thus implicit in the opening sound. There is also a psychological wisdom: the unexpected letters arrest attention. A listener in Makkah expecting news, poetry, or argument hears instead two bare letter-names, and attention sharpens. What follows — an oath upon the Qur'an and a confirmation of the Prophet — then falls upon an awakened ear. Modern teachers of rhetoric call this a "pattern interrupt"; the Qur'an employed it fourteen centuries ago.

**What Two Letters Ask of the Reader Today**

For the contemporary believer, *Yā. Sīn.* is a discipline in miniature. First, it teaches reverence for what exceeds us: not every verse yields its meaning to immediate analysis, and the mature reader does not discard what he cannot decode. Second, it personalizes the revelation: if the opening is God's tender address to His Prophet, then every reciter who loves the Prophet hears, at the threshold of the heart of the Qur'an, how God speaks to the one he loves — and hopes to be addressed with mercy himself. Third, it sets the key of affection for a sūrah that will speak of shackles, barriers, cries, and Hell: judgment is real, but the first sound of the chapter is gentleness. The believer who recites *Yā. Sīn.* over the sick or the dead (in accordance with the practice discussed in the Introduction) thus begins with the softest of openings before proceeding to the mightiest of warnings and hopes. Begin with love; end with return.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:2

> **By the Wise Qur'an,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath Sworn Upon Speech Itself**

*"By the Wise Qur'an"* is an oath — *wa al-Qurʾān al-ḥakīm* — in which God swears by the revelation itself. The Qur'an contains many divine oaths: by the sun and its morning brightness (91:1), by time (103:1), by the fig and the olive (95:1). The scholars explain that when God swears by a created thing, He thereby honours it and draws attention to its testimony; when He swears by His own speech, the honour is absolute, for the Qur'an is the uncreated word expressing His knowledge and will. The oath here has agrammatical partner: oaths in Arabic require an answer (*jawāb al-qasam*), and it comes in the next verse — *"truly thou art among the message bearers."* The structure is therefore: God calls the Qur'an as witness that Muhammad ﷺ is His messenger. The Makkans said the Prophet was a poet, a soothsayer, a liar; God answers by summoning the mightiest witness available — the Book itself. No human court could stage a scene more dramatic: the evidence takes the stand and speaks.

**Ḥakīm: Wise, Firm, and Wisdom-Giving All at Once**

The adjective *ḥakīm*, here translated "Wise," is one of the richest words in the Qur'anic vocabulary, and the commentators unpack three interlocking senses, all active at once. First, *ḥakīm* means wise in itself: the Qur'an embodies perfect wisdom in its doctrines, laws, narratives, and promises. Second, it means *muḥkam* — determined, made firm, secured against corruption — as in the description of the Book *"whose signs have been determined"* (*uḥkimat*; 11:1). Ibn Kathīr connects this firmness to the verse:

> *"Falsehood comes not upon it from before it or from behind it; a revelation from One Wise, Praised."* (Qur'an 41:42)

Fourteen centuries of transmission, memorization by millions, and manuscript attestation have fulfilled this description before the eyes of history: the text sworn by here is the text recited today, letter for letter. Third, *ḥakīm* can mean *muḥkim* — that which makes wise: the Qur'an teaches truth and imparts judgment to whoever receives it. It does not merely contain wisdom as a vessel contains water; it transforms the drinker into a wise person. Al-Qurṭubī notes all three senses, and Ibn Kathīr adds that the use of *ḥakīm* here alludes to the Qur'an specifically as a *revealed* book — its wisdom is not human philosophy refined by trial and error but guidance sent down complete. The Qur'an is elsewhere also described as wise in 10:1, 31:2, and 43:3–4, each time at the threshold of a chapter, as though every major discourse must first acknowledge the wisdom of its source.

**Why Wisdom Is the First Attribute Named**

Of all the Qur'an's attributes — glorious (*majīd*), noble (*karīm*), clear (*mubīn*), blessed (*mubārak*) — why does this sūrah choose *wise*? The answer lies in what the sūrah must accomplish. Yā Sīn will argue: from nature to resurrection, from the town of the messengers to the fate of Makkah, from dead bones to living judgment. Every one of these arguments requires the reader to trust that the reasoning is sound — that the connections drawn are not poetry's associations but wisdom's demonstrations. By naming the Qur'an *ḥakīm* at the outset, God certifies the logic of everything to follow. There is a further subtlety: the Makkans prided themselves on judgment and counsel — their assembly was called *Dār al-Nadwah*, the House of Counsel. The sūrah tells them that true *ḥikmah* is not in their deliberations but in this recitation. Human wisdom assembles opinions; divine wisdom descends as certainty. The contrast would not have been lost on its first hearers.

**The Oath as Comfort to the Messenger**

Read in its biographical setting, the oath is also consolation. The Prophet ﷺ was denied, mocked, and called every name except his own. In the preceding interpretation of *Yā Sīn* as his name (see 36:1), God first addressed him with tenderness; now God vindicates him with an oath. Consider the psychology: when every voice in one's city says "you are false," and the Lord of the worlds swears "this Qur'an is wise and you are its bearer," the slander collapses like mist. The Prophet's wife Khadījah and his uncle Abū Ṭālib could offer protection; only God could offer attestation. This is why the sūrah will later counsel, *"So let not their speech grieve thee"* (36:76): the grief is real, but the oath stands above it. Classical scholars note that God's oaths in the Qur'an are not for God's sake — He needs no confirmation — but for the sake of the hearers, to settle hearts. Here the first heart to be settled is the Prophet's own.

**Living With a Wise Book in an Age of Information**

The modern reader drowns not in scarcity of words but in their flood: feeds, headlines, opinions, and counter-opinions without end. To such a reader, *"the Wise Qur'an"* proposes a hierarchy of speech. Information tells what happened; knowledge explains how; only wisdom tells what it means and what to do. The Qur'an claims the third station. Practically, this means the believer treats recitation as consultation: before deciding, he asks what the Wise Book says about wealth, anger, marriage, grief, and death — and finds that its answers, though fourteen centuries old, diagnose his situation with unnerving precision. It also means a discipline of protection: just as the Qur'an is *muḥkam* (secured against falsehood), the believer secures a portion of his day against the flood — even a few verses of Yā Sīn in the morning, as the pious practice holds — so that wisdom, not noise, sets the tone of his judgments. The oath that vindicated the Prophet now invites his followers: swear your allegiance to no chatter; swear it to the Wise Book.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:3

> **Truly thou art among the message bearers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Oath: A Verdict on the Prophet**

This short verse is the answer (*jawāb*) to the oath sworn in verse 2: *"By the Wise Qur'an — truly thou art among the message bearers."* The Arabic, *innaka la-mina al-mursalīn*, layers two particles of emphasis — *inna* ("truly") and *la-* ("indeed") — so that the sentence carries the force of a sworn verdict: "You are, beyond all doubt, one of those who have been sent." The Makkans' central charge against Muhammad ﷺ was precisely that he had *not* been sent — that he was a poet inventing verses, a soothsayer retailing whispers, an ambitious man claiming heaven's authority. God answers not with argument first but with attestation: the matter is settled in heaven before it is debated on earth. Al-Ṭabarī notes the legal gravity of the construction: when the Qur'an combines *inna* with the emphatic *lām*, it closes the door to doubt. The court of Makkan opinion might deliberate; the court of heaven has ruled.

**Mursalīn: Sent, Not Self-Appointed**

The word *mursalīn* ("message bearers," those who are sent) is passive in form: a *mursal* is one *sent* by another. This grammar is theology. Every claimant to religious authority in history falls into one of two classes: the self-sent, who appoint themselves, and the heaven-sent, who are commissioned. The verse places Muhammad ﷺ firmly in the second company — *"among"* them, *mina al-mursalīn*, one link in the chain that runs from Noah through Abraham, Moses, and Jesus. The Qur'an elsewhere uses the same term for other prophets in over a dozen verses, and for Muhammad ﷺ himself in 2:252. The preposition *"among"* (*min*) does quiet work: it denies both extremes — that he is no messenger at all (the Makkan claim) and that he is something other than a messenger, an angel or a god (the exaggeration into which other communities fell regarding their prophets). He is a man among men, a messenger among messengers. His honour is membership, not exception: he stands in the noblest caravan ever to cross human history, and his distinction within it — the finality and universality of his mission — will be established elsewhere, as in:

> *"Muḥammad is not the father of any man among you, but the Messenger of God and the Seal of the Prophets."* (Qur'an 33:40)

**Why the Confirmation Comes Before the Arguments**

A student of persuasion might expect the sūrah to argue first and conclude last: here are the proofs, therefore he is a messenger. The Qur'an reverses the order: the verdict first, the proofs across the next eighty verses. The wisdom is pastoral and logical at once. Pastorally, the Prophet ﷺ needed confirmation before combat; the sūrah arms him with certainty before sending him into rejection. Logically, the order mirrors how trust actually works: we first learn *who* speaks, then weigh *what* is said. A prescription is trusted because a physician wrote it; the patient's verification of each drug comes after, not before, the trust. So here: God establishes the credentials of the speaker, then spends the chapter displaying the signs — the parable of the town, the revived earth, the ordered heavens, the ships, the Garden and the Fire, the rotted bones — each of which double-confirms both message and messenger. Ibn Kathīr observes that this verse, standing where it does, "supports the interpretations that see *Yā Sīn* as a reference to the Prophet himself": the named one (v. 1) is sworn by the Book (v. 2) to be the sent one (v. 3). Name, witness, verdict — three strokes, one portrait.

**The Company of the Sent and the Loneliness of Truth**

There is consolation hidden in the plural. *"Among the message bearers"* tells the Prophet ﷺ — and every truth-teller after him — that rejection is not evidence of falsehood but the standard reception of messengers. Noah was mocked, Abraham was cast into fire, Moses was called a sorcerer, Jesus was plotted against; the parable later in this very sūrah (vv. 13–30) will show messengers beaten, imprisoned, and threatened with stoning. To be "among" them is therefore to be among the rejected — and among the vindicated. The psychology is profound: loneliness magnifies doubt, while company sustains conviction. A believer who stands alone in his family, his campus, or his workplace for prayer, honesty, or modesty, and feels the loneliness as proof that he must be wrong, is answered by this verse: truth has always been a minority caravan, and its members recognize one another across centuries. Al-Qurṭubī draws the practical moral: whoever invites to God in any age shares in the prophetic inheritance precisely to the degree that he shares in prophetic patience.

**What Membership Requires of the Followers**

Finally, the verse quietly obligates those who accept it. If Muhammad ﷺ is truly among the sent, then his message carries the sender's authority, and neutrality is impossible: one either receives a heaven-sent messenger or rejects one. The sūrah will develop the two responses across its whole length — the believers who "follow the Reminder" (v. 11) and the deniers upon whom "the Word has come due" (v. 7). For the modern Muslim, the verse renews the second testimony of faith (*Muḥammadun rasūlu Llāh*) as a living decision rather than a memorized formula: to affirm that he was sent is to accept his guidance on what to love and leave, how to marry and trade, when to speak and be silent. And for the non-Muslim reader proceeding honestly through Yā Sīn, the verse frames the chapter's challenge fairly: weigh the Wise Book (v. 2), examine the signs to come, and then judge whether the man at the centre of it all could be anything other than sent.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:4

> **Upon a straight path,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Words That Describe an Entire Life**

*"Upon a straight path"* — *ʿalā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm* — is among the shortest verses in the sūrah, yet it describes the whole of the Prophet's ﷺ standing: his doctrine, his character, and his mission. Grammatically it completes verse 3 ("truly thou art among the message bearers, upon a straight path"), so that messengership and straightness are bound together: he is sent, and the way on which he travels — and to which he calls — is straight. The preposition *"upon"* (*ʿalā*) pictures him as already walking the road, not merely pointing at it from afar. The Qur'an's critics in Makkah claimed he had gone astray or led astray; God testifies that his feet are on the straight line. The brevity is eloquence: a lifetime of truthfulness (*al-ṣādiq al-amīn*, "the truthful, the trustworthy," as even his enemies called him before revelation), of worship, mercy, courage, and patience, is gathered into three words.

**Ṣirāṭ Mustaqīm: The Line That Does Not Bend**

The phrase is the same one every Muslim recites in every unit of prayer:

> *"Guide us upon the straight path."* (Qur'an 1:6)

Its repetition here is deliberate. The *ṣirāṭ* in Arabic is a broad, clear road — not a hidden track but the highway — and *mustaqīm* is that which stands straight, neither curving nor tilting. Ibn ʿAbbās glossed the straight path as the religion of Islam itself; al-Ṭabarī records that it is "the clear road without crookedness." The image contains its own logic: between any two points, the straight line is the shortest distance. Between man and God, between this world and Paradise, the prophetic way is the shortest route — not the shortest in ease, for it passes through patience and sacrifice, but the shortest in truth, without the detours of idolatry, superstition, desire-worship, and philosophy's endless circling. Every other path bends: indulgence bends toward the animal, severity bends toward despair, associating partners with God bends toward contradiction. The prophetic path alone runs straight because it alone aligns fully with reality — with who God is, who man is, and where both meet.

**The Prophet as Proof That the Path Can Be Walked**

There is a mercy in attaching the straight path to a person rather than leaving it as an abstraction. Had God commanded straightness without showing a straight man, despair could plead impossibility: "Who can live like that?" But the verse says the messenger is *upon* it — walking it in a human body, with hunger, grief, wounds, and fatigue. His Companions watched him pray until his feet swelled, forgive the people of Ṭāʾif who stoned him, weep for his son Ibrāhīm, and stand hungry in the trench of Madinah; they saw straightness with eyes, not merely heard it with ears. The Qur'an generalizes the point:

> *"Indeed, in the Messenger of God you have a beautiful example for whomsoever hopes in God and the Last Day and remembers God much."* (Qur'an 33:21)

For the modern believer overwhelmed by the gap between ideal and reality, the verse reframes the ideal as a *road* rather than a *cliff*: one is not asked to leap to perfection but to stay upon the path — to return to it after every stumble, as the traveller returns to the highway after every detour. Straightness in Islamic ethics is thus direction maintained, not sinlessness claimed.

**Straightness Tested by the Sūrah's Own Story**

The sūrah will soon test this claim narratively. If the Prophet is upon a straight path, why do most reject him? The parable of the town (vv. 13–30) answers: messengers before him walked the same straight road and were likewise denied, threatened, and mocked — yet the lone man who "came running" recognized straightness when he saw it and spent his life for it. The sūrah's later verses also show what leaving the path looks like: necks in shackles with chins forced up (v. 8), barriers before and behind (v. 9), mouths sealed while limbs testify (v. 65). Straightness and crookedness are thus not abstract labels but two fully drawn destinies. Al-Rāzī notes thē correspondence: the path is straight, so whoever walks it arrives; whoever abandons it does not merely slow down but arrives *elsewhere* — and the sūrah names the elsewhere with terrifying precision. The three words of this verse are therefore a promise and a warning in one: upon this road, arrival is certain; off it, arrival is certain too — at the other destination.

**Walking Straight in a Crooked Age**

To be "upon a straight path" today means, practically, alignment across life's domains: straight in belief (one God, without partners or intermediaries), straight in worship (prayer that punctuates the day like milestones), straight in dealings (measures, contracts, and promises without tilt), and straight in character (truthful speech, chaste conduct, merciful judgment). The image of the road helps with consistency: a driver who is straight on highways but reckless in side streets is not a straight driver. Likewise the believer examines side streets — private browsing, quiet intentions, treatment of the weak — because crookedness hides in corners. The verse also offers a diagnostic for confusion: when modern voices insist that every lifestyle is equally valid and no road is straighter than another, the Muslim recalls that geometry is not oppression — to say the straight line is shortest is not arrogance but mathematics. He invites others to the road without apology and without compulsion, walking it himself first, as his Prophet did: upon it, not merely toward it.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:5

> **A revelation of the Mighty, the Merciful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tanzīl: A Book Sent Down, Not Worked Up**

*"A revelation"* — *tanzīl* — is the verbal noun of *nazzala*, "to send down," and its grammar carries the sūrah's central claim about the Qur'an's origin. The Book was not composed upward from human genius but sent downward from divine knowledge. The classical grammarians note two possible readings of the case-ending: as object of an implied verb ("We revealed it *as a revelation*," referring back to the Wise Qur'an of verse 2) or as nominative predicate ("It *is* a revelation"). Most commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, take the referent to be the Qur'an in either reading, though some allow that it refers to the Prophet ﷺ himself — "he is a revelation," i.e., a sent mercy — citing the verse:

> *"God has certainly sent down unto you a reminder: a Messenger reciting unto you the clear signs of God."* (Qur'an 65:10–11)

Here the Messenger himself is described as "sent down," so both readings converge: whether Book or bearer, heaven's gift descends; earth does not manufacture it. The direction matters. Water flows downward and gives life to valleys; revelation flows downward and gives life to hearts. The Makkans wanted a revelation that confirmed their customs; *tanzīl* announces one that judges them.

**Al-ʿAzīz al-Raḥīm: Might That Protects Mercy**

The revelation is ascribed to *"the Mighty, the Merciful"* — *al-ʿAzīz al-Raḥīm* — two names whose pairing recurs throughout the Qur'an, most strikingly as the refrain of Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ (chapter 26), where it closes eight successive narratives of prophets and their peoples. The commentators see in the juxtaposition the perfect balance of the divine nature as it touches creation: *might* toward those who oppose Him, *mercy* toward those who obey Him; justice that none can resist, compassion that none need despair of. *Al-ʿAzīz* (from *ʿizzah*, invincible honour) is the One none can overpower, whose decree none can repel, whose friends none can humiliate. *Al-Raḥīm* is the One whose mercy is constant, intimate, and particular toward the believers. Logically, each name completes the other: might without mercy would be terror, and mercy without might would be wishful sentiment unable to save. Together they describe a Lord who *can* punish the arrogant of Makkah — and *will* forgive the repentant among them. The sūrah will display both faces: the single Cry that extinguishes a town (v. 29) and the greeting *"Peace!"* from a Merciful Lord (v. 58).

**Why These Two Names Open This Sūrah's Argument**

The pairing is not decorative; it is the engine of everything to follow. Every subsequent section of Yā Sīn runs on these two rails. The parable of the town displays might (instant destruction) and mercy (the martyr welcomed into the Garden). The cosmic signs display might (the sun's decreed course, the drowning sea) and mercy (grain, gardens, ships, cattle). The Judgment displays might (mouths sealed, Hell unveiled) and mercy (fruit, spouses, *"whatsoever they call for"*). Even the closing verses pair them: *"Be! and it is"* (irresistible might) with *"unto Whom you shall be returned"* (the mercy of homecoming). Ibn Kathīr notes that the juxtaposition also answers the two errors of the Makkans: those who thought their gods could protect them against God (refuted by *al-ʿAzīz* — none can resist Him) and those who thought their sins were too great for God to forgive (refuted by *al-Raḥīm* — His mercy receives all who turn). The verse is thus a complete theology in five words.

**Revelation as the Meeting Point of Power and Compassion**

Considered deeply, revelation itself is where might and mercy meet. That God *speaks* to man is mercy — He need not guide rebels. That His speech *binds* man is might — the revelation is not advice but authority. The Qur'an elsewhere binds the two aspects in a single image:

> *"And We have sent down the Book unto thee as a clarification of all things, and as a guidance and a mercy and glad tidings for those who submit."* (Qur'an 16:89)

Guidance clarifies (might of truth over falsehood); mercy heals (tenderness toward the lost). The Prophet ﷺ embodied the same pairing: unshakeable against the threats of Quraysh, weeping in night prayer for his people's forgiveness. His Companions learned both lessons: ʿUmar's sternness and Abū Bakr's gentleness were two reflections of one revelation. Al-Qurṭubī draws the devotional conclusion: the believer should receive the Qur'an with both awe and hope — awe, because it is the speech of the Mighty, before which mountains would crumble (59:21); hope, because it is the gift of the Merciful, sent to save and not to destroy.

**Receiving a Mighty and Merciful Word Today**

For the contemporary reader, the verse corrects two opposite diseases. Against the modern temptation to treat the Qur'an as merely inspiring literature — beautiful, optional, revisable — *al-ʿAzīz* stands guard: this is the speech of the Invincible, and its commands on justice, chastity, honesty, and worship are not suggestions. Against the opposite temptation — to read the Book as a record of wrath, all threat and no tenderness — *al-Raḥīm* stands guard: the same voice that warns of Hell greets the Garden's people with *"Peace!"* and promises that good deeds erase bad ones. Practically, the believer lets both names shape his recitation: he reads with the attentiveness one gives to a decisive decree, and with the trust one gives to a loving letter. And when he invites others to the Qur'an, he presents both faces honestly — the Might that gives the message weight and the Mercy that gives it warmth — for a revelation of the Mighty alone would repel, and of the Merciful alone might not awe; together, they draw hearts the way the sūrah's own martyr was drawn: running.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:6

> **That thou mayest warn a people whose fathers were not warned; so they were heedless.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose Clause of Prophethood: Warning as Mercy**

*"That thou mayest warn"* — *li-tundhira* — states the immediate purpose of the revelation just described: the Mighty, Merciful Lord sends down a Wise Book to a straight-walking messenger *so that* he may warn. Warning (*indhār*) in the Qur'an is never cruelty; it is mercy's first act toward those in danger. A sign that says "bridge collapsed ahead" is not hostility to travellers but love for them. The Qur'an pairs warning with glad tidings throughout (*bashīr wa nadhīr*, "a bearer of glad tidings and a warner," as in 34:28), but names warning first here because the audience — the Makkans steeped in idolatry — stood in mortal spiritual danger. Al-Rāzī notes the tenderness beneath the severity: God warns before He punishes, sends the messenger before the Cry, and gives the town with its messengers (vv. 13–30) as a parable precisely so that Makkah need not become its repetition. To be warned is itself a privilege; the destroyed peoples of the past perished only after warners had come, as the Qur'an insists:

> *"Thy Lord never destroys towns until He sends a messenger to their mother city to recite unto them Our signs."* (Qur'an 28:59)

> *"Never did We destroy a town save that it had warners."* (Qur'an 26:208; see also 6:131; 10:13)

**"Whose Fathers Were Not Warned": The Long Sleep of Arabia**

The people to be warned are described as those *"whose fathers were not warned"* — a phrase closely paralleled elsewhere:

> *"So that thou mayest warn a people to whom no warner has come before thee."* (Qur'an 32:3)

And concerning previous Arabic scripture:

> *"We have not given them any books that they study; nor have We sent them a warner before thee."* (Qur'an 34:44)

The historians explain: the Arabs are the progeny of Ishmael, son of Abraham — themselves heirs of pure monotheism — but across the intervening centuries that monotheism had been buried under idolatry. The Kaʿbah built for one God housed three hundred and sixty idols; the rites of ḥajj survived as form without faith. Though messengers may have passed among Arab tribes in the long interval, no abiding revelation or established warner remained in living memory; from the Arabs' own point of view, "the religion of their fathers was pagan." Some commentators propose an alternative reading of the Arabic — "of that of which their fathers were warned" — but the majority retain the plain sense: generations had passed without a warner, and the night had grown deep. The verse is thus also a quiet theodicy: God does not punish unwarned peoples, so He sends the warner now — and the ominous corollary hangs over the sūrah: with the warner's arrival, the excuse departs.

**Ghaflah: Heedlessness as the Master Disease**

*"So they were heedless"* — *fa-hum ghāfilūn* — diagnoses the result of unwarned generations: *ghaflah*, heedlessness. The Qur'an treats heedlessness not as innocent ignorance but as the master disease from which idolatry, ingratitude, and injustice all flow. The heedless man is not one who has reasoned and rejected; he is one who has never truly attended — who eats, trades, marries, and buries without once asking *why* or *whither*. The Qur'an describes such hearts elsewhere:

> *"They have hearts wherewith they understand not, eyes wherewith they see not, and ears wherewith they hear not. They are like cattle; nay, they are further astray. It is they who are the heedless."* (Qur'an 7:179)

The comparison to cattle is precise, not insulting: cattle consume and rest without reflection, which is perfection for cattle and ruin for man, who was created to know. Ghaflah is therefore the opposite of *dhikr* (remembrance), the Qur'an's constant prescription; and the sūrah itself is a *dhikr* (a Reminder, v. 11; v. 69) sent to shatter it. Ibn Taymiyyah observed that sins committed in heedlessness harden fastest, because the sinner never pauses to repent; the first step of every return to God is simply waking up.

**The Ominous Tone and Its Logic of Justice**

Read with the following verse — *"The Word has indeed come due for most of them"* (v. 7) — and against the whole Qur'an, this verse carries what the source commentary rightly calls "an ominous tone." The logic is judicial: warning creates responsibility, and persistent rejection after warning activates judgment. This is not divine harshness but the structure of all justice. A city that posts no speed limit cannot fairly fine drivers; once signs are posted, enforcement is fairness. God, who *"never destroys towns until He sends a messenger,"* has now sent the messenger — indeed, the final and greatest of them — to the mother of cities, Makkah. The parable of the town (vv. 13–30) will show the pattern completed elsewhere; the single Cry (vv. 29, 49) will show how swiftly the sentence, once due, is executed. Yet the ominousness is edged with hope: the warning has *come*, which means the door is still open. The sūrah's later promise — *"give such a one glad tidings of forgiveness and a generous reward"* (v. 11) — is addressed to whoever wakes from heedlessness now.

**Waking the Heedless in an Age of Distraction**

No verse of Yā Sīn lands more directly on modern life than this one. If the Makkans were heedless for lack of warners, our age is heedless under a flood of them — and drowns in distraction instead. The *ghaflah* of ancient Arabia was emptiness (no reminder); the *ghaflah* of today is fullness (a thousand reminders of nothing). The mechanism differs; the disease is one: days pass without remembrance, without prayer attended by the heart, without a glance at the Book. The cure the verse implies is threefold. First, *receive warning gratefully*: the sermon that unsettles, the verse that stings, the friend who counsels — these are mercies, not attacks. Second, *break the inheritance of heedlessness*: the Makkans excused themselves with "our fathers"; the modern soul excuses itself with "my schedule," "my feed," "everyone." Excuses are the lullabies of ghaflah. Third, *become a warner in turn*: the Prophet's mission continues in every parent who wakes a child for prayer, every friend who says "fear God" in love, every reciter of Yā Sīn whose voice reaches a heedless heart. For as the sūrah will show, one man who heeds the warning and "comes running" (v. 20) outweighs a whole city asleep.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:7

> **The Word has indeed come due for most of them, for they do not believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥaqqa al-Qawl: When the Sentence Falls Due**

*"The Word has indeed come due"* — *laqad ḥaqqa al-qawlu ʿalā aktharihim* — is judicial language. *Ḥaqqa* means to become established, binding, and due, as a debt falls due or a verdict becomes final. The verse could equally be rendered, "the Word has indeed proved true against most of them." The *"Word"* (*al-qawl*) here, the majority hold, is the Word of punishment — God's decreed sentence upon persistent disbelief — as clarified elsewhere:

> *"Likewise did the Word of thy Lord come due for those who disbelieve, that they are the inhabitants of the Fire."* (Qur'an 40:6)

It is the same Word that *"comes due"* in the vision of Hell's gates:

> *"But the Word from Me comes due: 'I shall surely fill Hell with jinn and men all together!'"* (Qur'an 32:13; cf. 11:119; 38:84–85)

And it echoes the cosmic scope of divine decree:

> *"And the Word of thy Lord has been fulfilled in truth and justice."* (Qur'an 6:115)

The past tense (*ḥaqqa*, "has come due") for a future punishment is the Qur'an's way of expressing certainty: the sentence is so sure it is spoken of as already fallen. Some commentators add a second sense: the "Word" is the Qur'an itself, which "proves true" as a witness — for those who receive it, it testifies to their faith; for those who turn away, it testifies to their rejection. This matches the famous ḥadīth:

> *"The Qur'an is a proof, either for you or against you."* (Muslim 223, from Abū Mālik al-Ashʿarī)

**"For Most of Them": The Sorrowful Arithmetic of Rejection**

The verse does not say "for all of them" but *"for most of them"* (*ʿalā aktharihim*) — a precision that preserves both truth and hope. Truth, because the Makkan reality was mass rejection: the chiefs of Quraysh, the wealthy, the custodians of idols, and the crowds that followed them refused the Prophet ﷺ year after year. Hope, because "most" is not "all": Khadījah believed, Abū Bakr believed, ʿAlī and Zayd believed, Bilāl under torture believed, and a growing company — the "most" left room for the best. The Qur'an elsewhere confirms the pattern that majorities often stray:

> *"And most of mankind, though thou desirest it, will not believe."* (Qur'an 12:103)

Ibn Kathīr notes that this verse describes those whose persistence in disbelief has sealed their outcome — not arbitrarily, but *"for they do not believe"* (*fa-hum lā yuʾminūn*): the sentence follows the sin, the Word falls due because belief was refused. The cause is stated plainly to refute any whisper of injustice: God did not decree punishment and then prevent belief; they refused belief, and the decree of punishment — announced in advance, warned against repeatedly — became due.

**Disbelief in What, Exactly?**

The verse's closing clause — *"for they do not believe"* — is absolute in form but specific in context. They do not believe in the warning of verse 6, in the Wise Qur'an of verse 2, or that the Prophet is among the message bearers (v. 3). Unbelief here is thus threefold rejection: of the message, the Book, and the man. Al-Rāzī observes the cumulative structure: each preceding verse offered a gift (a Book, a messenger, a path, a revelation, a warning), and each gift refused added weight to the scale until the Word fell due. This is the Qur'anic psychology of sealing: no heart is sealed at the first refusal, but persistent refusal becomes a state, and the state becomes a destiny. As another verse warns:

> *"Truly those for whom the Word of thy Lord has come due will not believe, though every sign should come unto them, till they see the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 10:96–97)

The signs of nature, the parable of the town, the resurrection of bones — all will be displayed in this sūrah, yet for those upon whom the Word has fallen due, display itself cannot compel sight. The eye must open from within.

**Divine Decree and Human Responsibility Held Together**

This verse is a locus of the Qur'an's teaching on decree (*qadar*) and responsibility, and the classical scholars handled it with care. The * Word coming due* is God's act — His just sentence. *Not believing* is man's act — his persistent choice. The verse joins them with *fa-* ("for"), making human unbelief the ground of the divine sentence. Ibn Taymiyyah stressed this order against both extremes: against those who blame decree for their sins ("God sealed me, so I cannot believe"), and against those who deny decree to protect freedom. The Qur'an's way is to affirm both: God knows and decrees, and man chooses and earns. The Prophet ﷺ, when asked about acting in light of decree, replied:

> *"Act, for each is facilitated toward that for which he was created."* (al-Bukhārī 4949; Muslim 2647, from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib)

Practically, the verse forbids two opposite errors in the believer: despair over others ("the Word has fallen, so why invite?") — for no one knows upon whom it has fallen, and today's denier may be tomorrow's believer (as ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb and Khālid ibn al-Walīd proved); and complacency over oneself ("I believe, so I am safe") — for faith must be guarded until death. The Word falls due upon persistence; persistence in faith, likewise, has its own promised Word:

> *"God confirms those who believe with the firm word, in the life of this world and in the Hereafter."* (Qur'an 14:27)

**The Verse as a Mirror for the Living Heart**

For the reader today, the verse is a checkpoint, not a verdict on others. Its question is personal: am I among those who *believe* — truly, obediently, increasingly — or among those drifting toward the state where warning no longer penetrates? The symptoms of the dangerous drift are named across the sūrah: hearing without heeding (v. 10), mocking messengers (v. 30), turning from signs (v. 46), scoffing at charity (v. 47), demanding the Hour as a joke (v. 48). The symptoms of safety are likewise named: following the Reminder, fearing the Merciful unseen (v. 11), running to support truth (v. 20), giving thanks (vv. 35, 73). The wise reader does not use this verse to sort his neighbours into Hell — that knowledge belongs to God — but to sort his own habits into life or death. So long as the heart still trembles at a verse like this, the Word has not fallen due upon it; trembling is itself a sign of life. The door stands open until the last breath; the sūrah's task is to hurry the heedless through it before the Cry (v. 49) closes it forever.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:8

> **Truly We have put shackles upon their necks, and they are up to their chins, so that they are forced up.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Aghlāl: The Iron That Binds Hand to Neck**

The verse opens with *"Truly We have put"* — *innā jaʿalnā* — divine agency stated without apology: the condition described is God's doing, His just sealing of those who refused belief (v. 7). The instrument is *aghlāl*, the plural of *ghull*: iron shackles that bind the hands in cuffs attached to an iron ring around the neck, forcing the prisoner's hands up to his throat so he can neither extend them nor lower his head. The pronoun *"they"* (*fa-hiya*, literally "they [feminine]") is taken by most commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) to refer to the hands — bound up to the necks — though the sentence's grammar lets the shackles themselves loom largest. The image is completed by *"up to their chins"* (*ilā al-adhqān*): the collar rises so high that the chin is propped upward, and *"so that they are forced up"* (*fa-hum muqmaḥūn*) — *iqmāḥ* being the posture of one whose head is thrust back and fixed, unable to bow. The Qur'an uses the same root-image to warn against miserliness:

> *"And let not thine hand be shackled to thy neck."* (Qur'an 17:29)

Here, however, the shackling is not a habit the sinner chose but a state God has set — the miserliness of the soul toward truth, now frozen into its punishment.

**This World or the Next? Both, Say the Scholars**

The commentators divide — and then unite — on whether these shackles are worn in this life or the next. Some (al-Ṭabarsī) take the verse as a depiction of the Hereafter, parallel to:

> *"They will be dragged, with shackles and chains around their necks."* (Qur'an 40:71; cf. 13:5; 34:33; 69:30)

On this reading, the verse unveils the Resurrection bodies of the rejecters: the arrogance that would not bow to God in prayer is answered by a neck that *cannot* bow, fixed forever in the posture of its pride. Others (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī) read the verse as a parable of the present: those who turn from the Prophet's call *are like* men shackled hand-to-neck — unable to extend their hands toward any good, unable to give charity, unable to reach for the Book, unable to lower their heads in humility. Their chins "forced up" allude, as al-Qurṭubī notes, to their arrogance before truth: the proud man's chin is always up; God merely makes the metaphor permanent. The two readings do not compete; together they state the sūrah's law of continuity: the soul's posture in this world becomes the body's posture in the next. What you refuse to bend toward willingly, you will be fixed away from eternally.

**Why the Neck? The Anatomy of Arrogance**

The Qur'an's choice of the neck is precise. In human expression, the neck and chin are the instruments of pride and submission: the humble lower the chin (in prayer, in greeting, in grief), while the arrogant thrust it up. When the Qur'an describes Pharaoh's drowning belief, it is belief at the moment the neck can no longer lift. When it describes the believers' prayer, the forehead — reached by bowing the neck — touches dust. To shackle the neck is therefore to lock the soul's master-joint: the pathway between the head (that knows) and the heart (that submits) is barred. Psychologically, the image diagnoses exactly how persistent disbelief feels from within: the arguments are heard, the signs are seen, yet something at the throat will not let the man say "I was wrong." Every caller to truth knows this rigidity — the listener who follows the reasoning to the threshold and then, chin up, refuses the step. The verse reveals that this is not mere stubbornness but a developing judgment: each refusal adds a link, until God Himself fastens the collar.

**The Chain of Causation: From Heedlessness to Shackles**

Placed in sequence, verses 6–8 trace a pathology: unwarned generations grow *heedless* (v. 6); heedlessness hardens into persistent *disbelief*, upon which the Word falls due (v. 7); and the due Word manifests as *shackles* (v. 8). The progression answers the objection "why would God shackle anyone?" with the record of the shackled: they were warned (v. 6), they would not believe (v. 7), and now they cannot reach (v. 8). Ibn Taymiyyah formulated the principle: divine sealing is never initial but responsive — God seals hearts that have first sealed themselves. The Qur'an states the pattern explicitly elsewhere:

> *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

First the deviation (theirs), then the sealing (His). This preserves both God's justice and man's agency, and it issues the urgent corollary: so long as the neck can still bow — so long as a man can still pray, apologize, weep, give — the shackles have not been fastened. Every bowed forehead in prayer is proof of a neck still free.

**Unshackling the Neck in Daily Life**

The verse prescribes its own cure by inversion. If shackled hands cannot extend to good, the free man extends his deliberately: charity given by the hand unbinds the neck, as the Prophet ﷺ taught:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand; the upper hand is the one that gives."* (al-Bukhārī 1427; Muslim 1033, from Ibn ʿUmar)

If the forced chin cannot bow, the believer bows it repeatedly — in the five prayers, the forehead touches ground seventeen times a day at minimum, each prostration a declaration that the neck belongs to God, not pride. If arrogance lifts the chin before truth, humility lowers it: admitting error to a spouse, accepting correction from a junior, saying "I do not know" — these small bowings keep the joint supple. Parents and teachers can use the verse's vivid image with the young: "Do not let your neck stiffen against advice; the boy who cannot bear to be told grows into the man who cannot bear to be guided." And for the hardened heart that fears it may already be shackled, the sūrah holds the door open: Pharaoh believed too late, but the thief, the usurer, and the idolater who turn *now* find the collar was never locked — for God does not shackle the returning, but runs to meet them, as the celebrated ḥadīth qudsī promises:

> *"Whoever comes to Me walking, I come to him running."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675, from Abū Hurayrah)

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:9

> **And We placed a barrier before them and a barrier behind them and veiled them; so they see not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sadd: The Barricade on Both Sides**

*"And We placed"* — *wa jaʿalnā* — continues the divine agency of verse 8: as God set shackles on necks, so He sets *"a barrier before them and a barrier behind them"* (*saddan min bayni aydīhim wa saddan min khalfihim*). *Sadd* is a massive obstruction — a dam, a wall, a mountain ridge — not a curtain one might brush aside. The disbeliever is thus walled front and back: he cannot advance toward guidance nor retreat to reconsider; movement in either direction of truth is blocked. Then comes the third sealing: *"and veiled them"* (*fa-aghshaynāhum*) — from *ghishāwah*, a covering drawn over the eyes or the whole head, the same root as the "covering" over hearts and ears in 2:7. Shackles bound the limbs (v. 8); walls block the road; the veil blinds the eyes. The sentence concludes: *"so they see not"* (*fa-hum lā yubṣirūn*) — not "they see not the Prophet" or "they see not the signs" specifically, but absolutely: sight itself, in the spiritual sense, is gone. Ibn Kathīr summarizes: "We have placed a barrier between them and the truth; We have blinded them to it." The verse is the sūrah's counterpart to:

> *"Truly those for whom the Word of thy Lord has come due will not believe, though every sign should come unto them, till they see the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 10:96–97)

**Before and Behind: Delusion About This World, Denial of the Next**

Al-Qurṭubī records a penetrating gloss: the barrier *before* them is their delusion concerning the life of this world (*dunyā*) — its pleasures, rivalries, and urgencies wall off the future — while the barrier *behind* them is their denial of the Hereafter (*ākhirah*) — the past's warnings (destroyed peoples, sent messengers) are walled off from instructing them. Hemmed between a present that dazzles and a past they dismiss, they cannot see. Others read "before" as the visible and "behind" as the hidden: neither outward signs (the revived earth, the ordered heavens of vv. 33–40) nor inward reminders (conscience, dreams, mortality) can reach them. The completeness of the enclosure is the point: guidance normally enters through many doors — reason, nature, scripture, suffering, counsel — and here every door is barred. This is why the sūrah will soon counsel the Prophet, *"Let not their speech grieve thee"* (v. 76): when God has walled every entrance, the caller is not at fault for the silence within. His task is delivery (v. 17); opening is God's alone.

**The Night of the Hijrah and the Rock at the Prayer**

Several commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) connect verses 8–9 to concrete events in the Prophet's ﷺ life, reading the cosmic barriers as manifested on specific nights. One account tells of a Quraysh chief who saw the Prophet praying and went to smash his head with a rock; as he raised his hand to cast it, his hand jerked back to his neck and the rock stuck fast in it. A second man seized the rock for the same purpose but could hear the Prophet's recitation without being able to see him. A third approached and beheld before him a tremendous camel about to swallow him. Each assailant met one of the verse's barriers: the shackled hand, the blinding veil, the wall of terror. The most famous related occasion is the Hijrah itself: on the night the Prophet ﷺ left Makkah, the assassin-party of Quraysh youths surrounded his house, and he emerged reciting the opening of Yā Sīn — tradition holds, these very verses — sprinkling dust upon their heads as he passed unseen:

> *"And We placed a barrier before them and a barrier behind them and veiled them; so they see not."*

He walked through armed hatred the way light passes through glass — present, untouched. The commentators stress that these particular manifestations do not contradict the verse's general meaning but exemplify it: the same God who seals hearts against truth can seal eyes against His prophet's body. Disbelief's inner blindness and the plotters' outer blindness are one power's two works.

**The Reflexive Justice of Being Veiled**

As with the shackles (v. 8), the barriers are *responsive* — God's act answering man's prior refusal. The Qur'an states the principle of veiling plainly:

> *"I shall turn away from My signs those who wax arrogant upon the earth without right. Even if they were to see every sign, they would not believe in them."* (Qur'an 7:146)

First the arrogance (theirs), then the turning (His). The psychology is observable without revelation: the man who repeatedly dismisses evidence trains himself out of perceiving it — the scholar who mocks a field never learns it, the addict who denies his addiction cannot see its signs, the partisan who jeers at every correction becomes unable to hear any. Habit becomes character, character becomes blindness. What revelation adds is the metaphysical depth: behind the psychological process stands a divine sentence, so that persistence in dismissal is not merely unwise but *dangerous* — each "I will not see" is answered, at some unknown threshold, by "then thou shalt not." This is why the Qur'an's constant command is to *look* — *"Have they not considered...?"* (vv. 31, 71) — while the capacity to look remains.

**Keeping the Road Open and the Eyes Unveiled**

The believer's response is maintenance of openness. First, *guard the approaches*: the barriers of dunyā-delusion are lowered by regular remembrance — the five prayers puncture the day's dazzle, fasting punctures appetite's wall, charity punctures wealth's wall, funerals and graveyards puncture the wall of imagined permanence. Second, *guard the past*: study the destroyed peoples (v. 31) and the town of the messengers (vv. 13–30) so that history instructs rather than being walled off. Third, *beg for sight*: the Prophet ﷺ himself prayed:

> *"O God, show me truth as truth and grant me adherence to it, and show me falsehood as falsehood and grant me avoidance of it."* (Cited by Ibn Kathīr in his commentary)

Fourth, *never weaponize this verse against others*: no one may declare a living soul walled and veiled — today's veiled scoffer may be tomorrow's running believer (v. 20), as the conversions of ʿUmar, Hamzah, and the whole city of Madinah prove. The verse describes a destination of persistence, not a label for persons; its proper use is self-examination ("are my walls rising?") and prophetic comfort ("their blindness is not thy failure"). And for protection on dangerous paths, the pious have long recited this verse following the Prophet's Hijrah-night example — trusting the God who veils enemies from the righteous as surely as He veils truth from the arrogant.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:10

> **It is the same for them whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they do not believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sawāʾ: When Warning and Silence Weigh Equal**

*"It is the same for them"* — *sawāʾun ʿalayhim* — pronounces the terrible equality: for those described in verses 7–9, the Prophet's ﷺ warning and his silence produce the identical result. The Arabic *sawāʾ* means level, equal, flat — a plain with no rise: speech falls upon it and silence falls upon it, and the ground changes for neither. The verse is nearly identical to the opening description of the disbelievers in Sūrah al-Baqarah:

> *"Truly those who disbelieve — it is the same for them whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they do not believe."* (Qur'an 2:6)

And the Qur'an repeats the verdict in several forms:

> *"It is the same for them whether thou askest forgiveness for them or askest not forgiveness for them."* (Qur'an 63:6; cf. 7:193; 26:136)

The last phrase, *"they do not believe"* (*lā yuʾminūn*), can also be read as future — "they will not believe" — sealing both present and prospect. The commentators stress the scope: this concerns those upon whom the Word has fallen due (v. 7), whose necks are shackled (v. 8) and whose road is walled (v. 9) — not every sinner, not every doubter, but the persistently, finally resistant. For them, the noblest speech ever addressed to man and complete silence weigh exactly the same: nothing.

**Why God Tells His Prophet to Stop Grieving, Not to Stop Calling**

The verse's first audience is the Prophet ﷺ himself, and its purpose is consolation, not cancellation of his mission. A caller who measures himself by results will break against such hearts: he warned by day and they mocked; he warned by night and they plotted; he wept for them and they laughed. God therefore separates *duty* from *outcome*: thy duty is proclamation (v. 17); their belief is not thy burden. The counsel recurs across the revelation:

> *"Truly God leads astray whomsoever He will and guides whomsoever He will; so let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them. Truly God knows that which they do."* (Qur'an 35:8; cf. 3:176; 5:41; 6:33; 10:65; 15:88; 16:127; 18:6; 26:3; 27:70; 31:23)

Al-Rāzī notes the mercy in this theology of results: had the Prophet's ﷺ success been measured by Makkah's conversion, his Makkan years would read as failure; measured by fidelity, they are the most successful preaching in history — for from that "failed" soil grew a community that girdles the earth. Every teacher, parent, and inviter inherits the principle: plant and water faithfully; the harvest belongs to God. The verse does not say "so do not warn them" — the warning continues, for among the hearers are always those of verse 11 — but "so do not *grieve* as though their refusal were thy fault."

**The Logic of Useless Warning and Its Limits**

A thoughtful reader asks: if warning is "the same" as silence for such people, why warn at all? The scholars give four answers, each load-bearing. First, *to establish the proof* (*iqāmat al-ḥujjah*): on Judgment Day none can plead "we were never told," for the warning was delivered — and their own limbs will confirm it (v. 65). Second, *to distinguish the hearers*: the same sermon that hardens one heart softens another; the caller cannot know which hearts are verse-10 hearts and which are verse-11 hearts, so he addresses all. Third, *to fulfill the caller's own duty*: the warner is judged by his warning, and silence would be his sin even if speech is their loss. Fourth, *because "most" is not "all"* (v. 7): the verse describes a class, not named individuals, and no living man wears its label on his forehead — today's mocker may be tomorrow's martyr, as the conversions of bitter enemies (ʿUmar, Khālid, Hind, Abū Sufyān) proved within the Prophet's own lifetime. The verse thus forbids *grief*, not *effort*; it diagnoses a spiritual state while leaving every individual soul's door open until death.

**Equality of Response as Evidence of the Heart's Death**

The phrase "whether thou warnest them or warnest them not" also functions diagnostically: *response-indifference* is the signature of spiritual death. A living limb flinches from fire and warms to shelter; a dead limb does neither. Likewise the living heart trembles at threats and softens at promises, while the dead heart meets both with the same shrug. The Qur'an contrasts the two states sharply — the believers:

> *"The believers are only those who, when God is mentioned, their hearts tremble."* (Qur'an 8:2)

against those for whom mention and silence are one. Psychologists of habit confirm the mechanism: repeated dismissal of a stimulus produces habituation so complete that the stimulus ceases to register — the alarm heard a hundred times without response becomes inaudible. Revelation adds the eternal dimension: the heart that trains itself not to hear warnings in this world will hear, in the next, only the verdict it trained for. The terrifying symmetry is exact: as warning and silence were "the same" to them, so mercy and wrath will be distinguished *for* them — they chose sameness, and sameness in this world purchases separation in the next (v. 59).

**What This Verse Asks of the Living Believer**

Practically, the verse assigns three tasks. First, *examine thyself*: when a sermon, a verse, or a friend's counsel reaches thee, does anything move? If yes — even guilt, even tears — thank God: thou art not of this verse's people, for the dead feel nothing. If nothing has moved for long, treat it as spiritual emergency: increase remembrance, sit with the righteous, visit the sick and the graves, and beg God for a living heart — for indifference is curable while breath remains. Second, *persevere in calling*: never abandon inviting because results are invisible; Noah called for nine and a half centuries with few believers, yet his fidelity, not his numbers, made him among the greatest messengers. Third, *release outcomes to God*: the parent whose child strays, the teacher whose student rebels, the friend whose counsel is mocked — all are addressed by this verse's hidden tenderness: thou warnest because He commanded it; they believe only if He wills it; thy reward was secured the moment thou spokest sincerely. And the very next verse turns the page from darkness to light: for though *these* do not believe, there are *those* who follow the Reminder — and to them belongs forgiveness and a generous reward.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:11

> **Thou only warnest whomsoever follows the Reminder and fears the Compassionate unseen. So give such a one glad tidings of forgiveness and a generous reward.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Innamā: The Warning That Finds Its True Audience**

*"Thou only warnest"* — *innamā tundhiru* — uses the particle of restriction (*innamā*, "only, indeed only") to draw the sūrah's dividing line. Verse 10 described those for whom warning is void; this verse describes those for whom it is fruitful. The warning is *broadcast* to all but *received* by some: only the one who "follows the Reminder and fears the Compassionate unseen" is truly *warned* — that is, the warning takes effect in him, enters his heart, alters his conduct. Others hear sound; he hears summons. Al-Ṭabarī explains that *innamā* here means the benefit of warning is confined to such people, though its delivery extends to all. The verse thus completes the theology of calling begun in verse 6: the Prophet ﷺ was sent to warn a heedless people (v. 6); most persist until the Word falls due (v. 7); for them warning equals silence (v. 10); but there exists another company — defined here by two marks — for whom every warning is treasure. The caller must never confuse the two audiences: he speaks to all, but he *expects* fruit only from the receptive, and among a thousand rejecters, one receptive heart justifies the sermon.

**"Follows the Reminder": The Qur'an as Dhikr**

*"Whomsoever follows the Reminder"* — *man ittabaʿa al-dhikr* — identifies the first mark of receptivity: adherence to *al-dhikr*, "the Reminder." The commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī) agree this is the Qur'an itself, so named because it reminds man of what he already knows in his primordial nature (*fiṭrah*) yet forgets: his Lord, his covenant (7:172), his purpose, his end. The Qur'an claims the title repeatedly:

> *"And this is a blessed Reminder that We have sent down."* (Qur'an 21:50)

> *"Truly We have sent down the Reminder, and truly We shall preserve it."* (Qur'an 15:9)

To "follow" it (*ittabaʿa*) is more than to recite or admire it: *ittibāʿ* means walking behind a guide, step for step — obeying its commands, avoiding its prohibitions, trusting its promises, trembling at its threats. The sūrah will later define the Qur'an's whole function this way: *"It is but a reminder and a clear Qur'an, to warn whomsoever is alive"* (vv. 69–70). The spiritually alive follow; the spiritually dead (v. 10) do not. Psychologically, the mark is observable: place the Reminder before a man — a verse on charity, on prayer, on forgiveness — and watch whether his conduct adjusts. Adjustment is life; indifference is the symptom of verse 10. The Reminder is thus both medicine and diagnosis in one.

**"Fears the Compassionate Unseen": Piety in Secret**

The second mark — *"and fears the Compassionate unseen"* (*wa khashiya al-Raḥmāna bi-al-ghayb*) — reaches the heart's hidden chamber. *Khashyah* is fear mixed with awe and reverence, deeper than fright; its object here is *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate — for true piety fears displeasing the Most Merciful precisely *because* He is merciful, as a loving child fears wounding a tender father more than a tyrant's whip. *"Unseen"* (*bi-al-ghayb*) carries the double sense the commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) draw out: though God remains unseen to him in this world — he worships a Lord he has never beheld, as praised in:

> *"Those who fear their Lord unseen."* (Qur'an 50:33; cf. 67:12)

— and though he himself is unseen by men — worshipping in seclusion, where no eye applauds and no reputation is earned. Both senses describe faith's authenticity: the first, belief without seeing (the very definition of *īmān bi-al-ghayb*, "belief in the Unseen," from 2:3); the second, sincerity without spectators (the soul of *ikhlāṣ*). The Prophet ﷺ gave the famous image of such hidden piety:

> *"Seven shall God shade with His shade on the Day when there is no shade but His... and a man who remembers God in solitude and his eyes overflow with tears... and a man whom a woman of beauty and rank invites, and he says, 'I fear God.'"* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031, from Abū Hurayrah)

These are the unseen-fearers: God sees what none sees, and rewards what none praised.

**"Glad Tidings of Forgiveness and a Generous Reward"**

*"So give such a one glad tidings"* (*fa-bashshirhu*) — the command turns the Prophet ﷺ from warner to herald of joy for this company. The tidings are twofold: *"forgiveness"* (*maghfirah*) — covering of all past sins, for the one who follows the Reminder and fears unseen inevitably stumbles, and his fear itself becomes the ground of pardon — and *"a generous reward"* (*ajrin karīm*) — *karīm* meaning noble, abundant, honourable: Paradise (the Garden), as the commentators agree (cf. 67:12, where the same two rewards follow the same two marks). The pairing is complete salvation: forgiveness erases the debt, reward bestows the wealth; the first removes Hell, the second grants Heaven. Note the order: forgiveness *before* reward, for none enters the Garden on wages alone — all enter by mercy covering sin, then by generosity exceeding measure. The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the economy of grace:

> *"None shall enter Paradise by his deeds." They said, "Not even thou, O Messenger of God?" He said, "Not even I, unless God enfolds me in mercy from Him and bounty."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816, from Abū Hurayrah)

The generous reward is thus doubly generous: given to the forgiven, and beyond all earning.

**Becoming One of the Warned-Effectively**

The verse is the sūrah's first mirror of hope, and its application is direct. To join the effectively-warned, cultivate the two marks deliberately. *Follow the Reminder*: bind thyself to the Qur'an with a daily portion — recitation with reflection, even a few verses — and let each sitting end with one concrete obedience ("this verse commands charity; I will give today"). The mark is following, not merely finishing; better one verse followed than a chapter skimmed. *Fear unseen*: build a secret life with God — night prayer no one knows, charity no one sees, tears no one witnesses, resistance to a private sin no one would detect. As the hidden fear grows, so does the hidden joy, for God repays secrecy with intimacy. And *receive the glad tidings personally*: when guilt whispers that thy sins are too many, answer with this verse's grammar — forgiveness is *promised* (*bashshir*, an accomplished announcement), not auctioned. Finally, *become a bearer of such tidings*: the parent who promises the struggling child that repentance wipes clean, the friend who tells the despairing that God's mercy exceeds his sins — each echoes the Prophet's commission. For every verse-10 heart that breaks the caller, verse 11 sets a verse-11 heart that mends him; and one forgiven, generously rewarded soul outweighs a city of the heedless.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:12

> **Truly We give life to the dead and record that which they have sent forth and that which they have left behind. And We have counted all things in a clear registry.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Nuḥyī al-Mawtā: The God Who Revives the Dead**

*"Truly We give life to the dead"* — *innā naḥnu nuḥyī al-mawtā* — opens the verse with resurrection stated as present-tense divine activity, not merely future promise. The commentators draw out four layered senses, all true. First, the eschatological: God will raise the dead bodily on the Last Day — the sūrah's climactic theme (vv. 51–53, 77–83). Second, the natural: God revives dead earth with rain, as explicitly paralleled later in this sūrah (vv. 33–35) and stated elsewhere:

> *"Know that God revives the earth after its death."* (Qur'an 57:17)

Third, the spiritual-communal: God brings peoples from the death of idolatry to the life of faith (al-Zamakhsharī) — as He did with the Companions, dead in paganism, raised in Islam. Fourth, the inward: God revivifies, through knowledge and truth, hearts made dead by heedlessness and ignorance (al-Alūsī, Ibn Kathīr) — the verse-10 heart, so long as breath remains, can still be raised. The verse follows the counsel to warn and leave the deniers to God (vv. 10–11) with perfect logic: the Prophet ﷺ need not despair of the spiritually dead around him, for the God who will raise corpses from graves can raise souls from denial — and if they persist, their account is recorded (the verse's second half) and their price will come due at the Resurrection (the verse's first word). Life-giving and record-keeping: mercy and justice in a single breath.

**"That Which They Have Sent Forth": Deeds That Precede Us**

*"And record that which they have sent forth"* — *wa naktubu mā qaddamū* — *qaddamū* meaning what they "put forward," sent ahead of themselves into the Hereafter. These are the deeds that will testify for or against them (cf. 5:80; 75:13; 82:5): prayers offered, charity given, words spoken, wrongs committed — every act dispatched forward like luggage sent before a journey, waiting at the destination. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the arrival vivid:

> *"On the Day when every soul finds present that which it has done of good, and that which it has done of evil, it will wish that between itself and it there were a great distance."* (Qur'an 3:30)

The recording is by God's command through the noble scribes (*kirāman kātibīn*, 82:11), and its precision is total — *"not even the weight of an atom"* escapes (34:3). Psychologically, this clause transforms daily life into eternal correspondence: every deed is a letter mailed to one's own future self, to be opened on the Day when *"no soul will be wronged in any way"* (v. 54). The wise live as careful correspondents.

**"That Which They Have Left Behind": Footsteps, Traces, and Legacies**

*"And that which they have left behind"* — *wa āthārahum* — *āthār* meaning literally "footsteps" or "traces." The occasion of revelation (*sabab al-nuzūl*) is famous: the Banū Salimah, a tribe of Madinah settled far from the Prophet's mosque, wished to relocate nearer to it, and this verse was revealed. The Prophet ﷺ said to them:

> *"Your footprints are recorded. Why then do you want to move?"* (Muslim 665, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh; cited by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Wāḥidī)

— meaning every step walked to prayer earns reward, so distance is profit, not loss. This is why some commentators date the verse to Madinah. But the meaning widens, as the scholars (al-Alūsī, al-Ṭabarī) insist, to all traces of good or evil one leaves: knowledge taught, institutions founded, children raised, examples set — and likewise innovations, injustices, and corruptions that outlive their authors. The celebrated ḥadīth defines the blessed subset:

> *"When the son of Adam dies, all his deeds come to an end except three: knowledge that is beneficial, a righteous child who prays for him, or ongoing charity (ṣadaqah jāriyah) that he leaves behind."* (Muslim 1631, from Abū Hurayrah; cited by Ibn Kathīr)

And the dread counterpart:
