> *"Whoever establishes an evil practice in Islam bears its burden and the burden of whoever acts upon it after him, without diminishing their burdens."* (Muslim 1017, from Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh)

Every life thus writes two books: the deeds it did and the deeds it caused. The footsteps to the mosque are the loveliest image of the second book's credit side: reward accumulating with each stride, in the dark before dawn, unseen by all but God.

**"A Clear Registry": The Imām in Which All Is Counted**

*"And We have counted all things in a clear registry"* — *wa kulla shayʾin aḥṣaynāhu fī imāmin mubīn* — *aḥṣaynā* meaning to count, encompass, and preserve exactly (from *iḥṣāʾ*, comprehensive enumeration). The *imām mubīn* ("clear registry"; *imām* literally "leader, exemplar, record") is understood in two complementary ways. Most Sunnī commentators (al-Alūsī, al-Ṭabarsī, al-Ṭabarī) take it as the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*, 85:22) — the *imām* ("leader") of all books, comprising all divine decrees from beginning to end (cf. 68:1 on the inscription of decrees). Others (al-Alūsī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) take it as the register of human deeds (cf. 17:71; 18:49). Both are "clear" (*mubīn*): nothing blurred, nothing lost. This verse is also, notably, one of the central texts cited by Shīʿī theologians for the divinely ordained imamate after the Prophet: al-Qummī relates from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, "I, by God, am the clear *imām*; I clarify truth from falsehood. I inherited it from the Messenger of God," and Shīʿī sources relate the Prophet's saying regarding ʿAlī, "He is the Imām in whom God records the knowledge of all things" (cited by al-Qummī, al-Ṭabarsī). Sunnī commentators, while honouring ʿAlī, retain the tablet/register reading as the verse's plain sense. The convergence all affirm: nothing escapes divine knowledge — neither a footstep in Madinah's dark nor a whisper in Makkah's night (cf. v. 76, *"We know what they hide and what they disclose"*).

**Living as One Whose Traces Are Counted**

The verse turns the believer into a conscious leaver of traces. First, *walk*: the footsteps-ḥadīth makes every walk to the mosque, every errand of mercy, every visit to the sick a recorded journey — the pious predecessors lengthened their routes to prayer to multiply footprints. Second, *plant ongoing charity*: a well, a book, a scholarship, a planted tree, a taught verse — each a pen that writes in thy book after thy hand is dust. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance has reward like the rewards of whoever follows him, without diminishing their rewards."* (Muslim 2674, from Abū Hurayrah)

Third, *fear the evil trace*: the mocking word that teaches a child to mock, the corrupt practice introduced in an office, the misleading post that travels beyond recall — all are *āthār*, counted in the same clear registry. Fourth, *take hope in revival*: the God who records footsteps is the God who raises the dead — the same verse promises both. The exhausted caller (vv. 10–11), the grieving bereaved (for whom Yā Sīn is recited), the sinner who thinks his heart verse-10-dead — all are addressed by the verse's first word: *We give life*. The registry preserves thy past; the Reviver can transform thy future. And with this verse the sūrah's opening movement closes: the Prophet ﷺ is confirmed (vv. 1–5), his people's state diagnosed (vv. 6–11), and the divine economy of resurrection and record established (v. 12) — before the parable of the town (vv. 13–30) shows it all enacted in history.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:13

> **And set forth for them as a parable the people of the town, when the message bearers came unto it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Iḍrib Mathal: The Command to Strike a Parable**

*"And set forth for them"* — *wa-iḍrib lahum mathalan* — *iḍrib* meaning literally "strike," the Qur'an's vivid verb for coining parables: a parable is *struck* like a coin or a tent-peg, impressed upon the mind with force. The command is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ: narrate to Quraysh, as their parable and prognosis, the story of *"the people of the town"* (*aṣḥāb al-qaryah*). The first phrase can also be rendered "set forth for them a parable: the people of the town" — the town's people *are* the parable. Most commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī) identify the town as Antioch (*Anṭākiyah*), the great Syrian city, though Ibn Kathīr honestly notes the identification "cannot be verified" — the Qur'an withholds the name deliberately, for named history can be dismissed as distant, while an unnamed town can be any town, including Makkah. The purpose is stated by the context: the Prophet is commanded to give Quraysh "an example of the fate that befell those who treated previous message bearers as they were treating him." Parable here is not entertainment but diagnosis: Makkah's symptoms match the town's; Makkah's prognosis, unless it repents, will match the town's end (vv. 28–29). The Qur'an's narratives are never mere chronicles; each is a mirror held to the present:

> *"In their stories there is a lesson for those possessed of minds."* (Qur'an 12:111)

**"When the Message Bearers Came": Mursalūn at the Gate**

*"When the message bearers came unto it"* — *idh jāʾahā al-mursalūn* — uses *mursalūn*, the same term applied to the Prophet ﷺ in verse 3 (*mina al-mursalīn*). The parallel is intentional and pointed: the messengers who entered that town stand where Muhammad ﷺ stands in Makkah, and the town's response previews Makkah's. Who were they? Most commentators view them as apostles sent by Jesus — disciples commissioned to carry his message to Antioch, "sent" by God in the sense that Jesus was commanded by God to send them (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī). On this view, after the first two were beaten and imprisoned — some say by the king's order (al-Alūsī) — a third, identified by some as Simon Peter, was sent as reinforcement. Another opinion (al-Alūsī, al-Ṭabarsī) holds they were *prophets* in the full sense, not apostles of a prophet — and this reading, the source commentary notes, "seems more likely," since the Qur'an calls prophets *mursalūn* in over a dozen verses and calls Muhammad ﷺ *mursal* in verse 3 and 2:252. Either way, the town received heaven's embassy twice and then a third time — grace compounded before judgment descended. The messengers "came" (*jāʾahā*): guidance travels to the heedless; it knocks before it condemns.

**Why Antioch — And Why the Name Does Not Matter**

If the town is Antioch, the parable gains historical texture: a cosmopolitan imperial city, proud of its philosophy, commerce, and temples — a greater Makkah, facing the same choice between ancestral gods and heaven's messengers. Yet the Qur'an's anonymity is itself wisdom, and the commentators draw three lessons from it. First, *universality*: an unnamed town belongs to every age — every city that receives truth and mocks its bearers becomes "the town." Second, *mercy*: naming and shaming a specific people's ancestors serves no guidance; the Qur'an names Pharaoh and Abū Lahab only where naming is itself the lesson, and veils names where the pattern is the point. Third, *focus*: the story's weight falls not on geography but on responses — the messengers' persistence, the town's threats, the running believer's courage, the single Cry. Modern readers should therefore resist treating the parable as archaeology to be verified or dismissed; it is cardiology — a scan of the reader's own heart. The question is never "where was the town?" but "am I its people, its messengers, or its running man?"

**Parable as the Sūrah's Central Proof**

Placed where it stands, the parable is the sūrah's hinge between doctrine and history. Verses 1–12 established principles: the Prophet is sent, most disbelieve, deeds are recorded, the dead will rise. Verses 13–30 now exhibit every principle enacted: sent messengers (v. 14) face mass disbelief (v. 15), persist in proclamation (vv. 16–17), are threatened (v. 18), are vindicated through a believer's martyrdom (vv. 20–27), while the deniers are extinguished in an instant (v. 29) — and the section closes with the divine lament over all such peoples (v. 30). The parable thus functions evidentially: Makkah's objection "this never happens" is answered with "it has happened"; its assumption "we are safe" is answered with "they assumed so too." Al-Rāzī notes the rhetorical escalation: abstract warning (vv. 6–10) moves many, but narrative warning moves more — hearts that resist syllogisms surrender to stories. This is why the Qur'an, the Wise Book (v. 2), argues so often through *qaṣaṣ* (narratives): the parable is wisdom in the form memory keeps.

**Hearing the Parable as Makkah Heard It**

For the first hearers, the command *"set forth for them"* must have raised the hairs on Quraysh's necks: their own Prophet, whom they called poet and liar, now announces a town that called messengers liars — and every listener knows, before the story ends, whose face will appear in its mirror. For today's reader, the application begins with the same self-placement. *Am I the town?* — Do I meet religious counsel with "you are but human beings like us" (v. 15), with threats or mockery when the message inconveniences me? *Am I the messenger?* — Do I deliver truth persistently and gently, returning emphasis after rejection (v. 16), claiming no authority beyond clear proclamation (v. 17)? *Am I the running man?* (vv. 20–27) — the question the parable most wants asked. The parable's opening verse also commissions every knowledgeable believer: *"set forth"* is in the imperative, and though addressed first to the Prophet ﷺ, his commission extends to his community — teach the stories, strike the parables, hold the mirrors. For towns are still choosing, messengers (in the lesser sense of inviters) are still coming, and the Cry (v. 29) still waits upon persistence. The parable is not past; it is perennial.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:14

> **When We sent two unto them, they denied them. So We strengthened them with a third, and they said, "Verily, we have been sent unto you!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Sent, Two Denied: The Arithmetic of Grace Refused**

*"When We sent two unto them"* — *idh arsalnā ilayhim ithnayn* — God ascribes the sending to Himself (*arsalnā*, "We sent"), whether the messengers were prophets directly commissioned or apostles sent through Jesus by divine command (see v. 13). The town's reply is immediate: *"they denied them"* (*fa-kadhdhabūhumā*) — *takdhīb*, denial, the Qur'an's standard verb for rejecting messengers: not mere doubt but active calling-them-liars. Two witnesses is, in Qur'anic law, the quorum of established testimony:

> *"And call upon two witnesses from among your men."* (Qur'an 2:282)

The town thus received legally complete testimony — and rejected it. Tradition adds that the first two were beaten and imprisoned (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī), some say by the king's order (al-Alūsī): denial escalated to violence, as it did in Makkah (the boycott, the beatings, the assassination plot) and as it does wherever truth threatens power. The verse's terseness is devastating: four words for grace (*We sent two unto them*), two for gratitude's opposite (*they denied them*). Heaven's embassy travels far; earth's answer is short.

**"So We Strengthened Them With a Third": Reinforcement, Not Abandonment**

*"So We strengthened them with a third"* — *fa-ʿazzaznā bi-thālith* — *ʿazzaznā* from *ʿizzah* (might, honour): God reinforced, empowered, granted prevailing strength. The pattern is divine pedagogy: rejection does not withdraw the message but reinforces it. One warner ignored becomes two; two denied become three. The Qur'an shows the same escalation with Moses and Aaron sent together to Pharaoh:

> *"Go thou and thy brother with My signs, and slacken not in remembrance of Me."* (Qur'an 20:42)

And the Prophet Muhammad's ﷺ own mission was progressively strengthened — from solitary caller to supported community to aided state. The logic is twofold: for the deniers, multiplied witnesses multiply inexcusability (to call one man liar is suspicion; to call three is perversity); for the messengers, reinforcement answers exhaustion — God does not leave His faithful outnumbered without aid. Some identify the third as Simon Peter; others leave him unnamed, for his function matters more than his name: he is proof that heaven outnumbers earth whenever earth counts its crowds. Makkah, hearing this, was meant to count: you deny one messenger now — what will you do when God strengthens him, as He strengthened them?

**"Verily, We Have Been Sent Unto You": The First Declaration**

*"And they said, 'Verily, we have been sent unto you!'"* — *innā ilaykum mursalūn* — the three speak as one voice. Note the modesty of the claim: not "we are gods," not "we are angels," not "we are kings" — but "we have been *sent*": passive, commissioned, subordinate to the Sender. This is the eternal uniform of true messengers, and the verse's wording (*ilaykum mursalūn*) will be repeated with stronger emphasis in verse 16 after denial — the message does not change under pressure; only its emphasis deepens. The declaration also establishes the town's responsibility with precision: *"unto you"* (*ilaykum*) — particularly, personally, unavoidably. General knowledge that God exists excuses none once particular messengers arrive with particular words. The Qur'an's judicial principle stands behind the sentence:

> *"And We never punish until We send a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

Two were sent; a third was added; the declaration was made. The town's file, from this verse forward, is complete — what follows is its response, and then its recompense.

**The Psychology of Denying in Stages**

The verse traces a psychology of collective denial worth studying. The town denied *two* — a pair that could have corroborated each other — which reveals that its rejection was never about insufficient evidence. Had evidence been the issue, the third would have settled it; instead, as the parable shows, the town answers three as it answered two: with the same charge ("you are but human," v. 15) and then threats (v. 18). This is denial's signature: it is evidence-insensitive because it is interest-driven — the town's gods, customs, and powers were threatened, so witnesses were counted as enemies regardless of number. Modern parallels abound: the smoker shown one study dismisses it; shown three, dismisses the researchers; the corrupt institution answers one whistleblower with denial and three with conspiracy charges. The Qur'an's insight is that hearts decide before minds weigh — *"they denied them"* precedes any examination of what they said. Al-Rāzī's conclusion: guidance requires not more proofs but a different posture — the posture of the running man (v. 20), who hears the same words the town heard and runs *toward* them.

**Strength in Numbers for Every Caller to Truth**

The verse equips every inviter, teacher, and reformer with three counsels. First, *expect initial denial*: two heaven-sent messengers were called liars — the parent, imam, or friend whose sincere counsel is mocked stands in prophetic company, not in failure. Second, *seek reinforcement, not retreat*: God's way with denial is addition — add a companion to the effort, add evidence, add patience, add prayer. The Prophet ﷺ, denied in Makkah, was strengthened with Madinah; denied by men, strengthened by angels at Badr; grieved by speech, strengthened by revelation (v. 76). Never answer rejection by abandoning the post. Third, *speak jointly and clearly*: the three said as one, "we have been sent unto you" — unity of message multiplies weight. A family, a study circle, a community that speaks truth with one voice carries the authority of the third messenger. And for the hearer of truth, the verse warns: count the witnesses rising around thee — the verse, the sermon, the friend, the dream, the illness, the death of a neighbour — each a reinforcement, each narrowing the excuse. The town had three; how many hast thou had?

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:15

> **They said, "You are but human beings like us, and the Compassionate has not sent down anything. You are but lying."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"But Human Beings Like Us": The Oldest Objection to Prophets**

*"You are but human beings like us"* — *mā antum illā basharun mithlunā* — *mā... illā* ("nothing but") reducing the messengers to mere humanity, as though humanity disqualified them. This is the stock objection of disbelieving peoples across the Qur'an, repeated with wearying sameness from Noah's people to Quraysh. Its logic is stated most nakedly elsewhere:

> *"And naught hindered men from believing when guidance came unto them, save that they said, 'Has God sent a human being as a messenger?'"* (Qur'an 17:94; cf. 6:8–9, 50; 11:12; 14:10; 23:33; 41:14; 64:6)

And Quraysh's own version, aimed at the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And they say, 'What ails this Messenger, who eats food and walks in the markets? Why is there not an angel sent down unto him to be a warner with him?'"* (Qur'an 25:7)

The objection feels like reverence for heaven ("God is too great to speak through a man") but functions as rebellion against it ("so we need not obey this man"). The Qur'an exposes the contradiction: had the messenger been an angel, the same objectors would have protested, "he is not one of us; we cannot follow him" — as God says:

> *"And had We made him an angel, We would have made him a man, and We would have confounded for them that which they confound."* (Qur'an 6:9)

An angel cannot model human patience, hunger, grief, or repentance; only a man can show men how men should live. The town demanded a messenger it could not imitate as excuse for disobeying one it could.

**"The Compassionate Has Not Sent Down Anything": Denial of Revelation Itself**

The second charge escalates from the messengers to the message: *"and the Compassionate has not sent down anything"* (*wa mā anzala al-Raḥmānu min shayʾ*) — a universal denial of revelation. Note the irony the commentators savour: the town swears by *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate — acknowledging God's mercy by name — while denying He has ever shown it through guidance. They keep the name and cancel the act, honour the title and reject the embassy. The Qur'an records the same formula on other lips:

> *"They measured not God with His true measure when they said, 'God has not sent down anything upon any human being.'"* (Qur'an 6:91)

The logic of the town's position collapses upon inspection: a Compassionate Lord who never guides is compassion without action — a sea that never waters. True compassion *must* communicate: the loving father warns the child of fire; the merciful physician prescribes; the caring king sends decrees. To affirm the Compassionate while denying all revelation is to affirm a contradiction. The sūrah's own structure refutes the town: the chapter opened by swearing *by* the sent-down Wise Qur'an (v. 2) as *revelation* of the Merciful (v. 5) — the town's sentence is pronounced false by the Book that quotes it.

**"You Are But Lying": From Objection to Accusation**

*"You are but lying"* (*in antum illā takdhibūn*) — the third clause abandons argument for accusation. Having offered no evidence that humans cannot be sent or that nothing was ever revealed, the town concludes with the liar-charge — the same word (*takdhib*) used of their denial in verse 14, now hurled as verdict. This is denial's classic descent: objection ("you are human") → dogma ("nothing was sent") → defamation ("you lie"). Each step requires less thought than the last; the final step requires none. The Qur'an shows Quraysh descending identically against the Prophet ﷺ — debating his humanity, denying his revelation, then calling him poet, sorcerer, madman, liar. Al-Rāzī observes the psychological function: "liar" is easier than "mistaken," for a mistake must be refuted while a liar may simply be dismissed. But the charge boomerangs, as the parable's end will show: the town called truthful men liars, and the single Cry (v. 29) exposed who had lied — to themselves most of all. False accusation is a debt the Last Day always collects, as the testifying limbs (v. 65) will confirm.

**Why Humanity Is the Messenger's Credential, Not His Disqualification**

The Qur'an's sustained answer to "but human" deserves gathering, for it is among revelation's most beautiful logics. First, *likeness enables following*: a human messenger's patience can be imitated, his prayers replicated, his character studied — *"Indeed, in the Messenger of God you have a beautiful example"* (33:21). An angel's worship (ceaseless, foodless, tireless) would crush, not guide. Second, *likeness enables mercy*: the human messenger knows hunger, orphanhood, bereavement, and fear from within, so his compassion is experiential, not theoretical — Muhammad ﷺ wept at graves, hungered in sieges, and forgave tormentors because he had felt what they felt. Third, *humanity proves the message's power*: that an unlettered man (7:157; 29:48), who "did not recite before this any Book; nor didst thou write it with thy right hand" (29:48), produced the Wise Qur'an is itself the miracle — the vessel's plainness certifies the content's heavenliness. Had a radiant angel descended with thunder, belief would have been compulsion, not faith; through a man, belief remains a moral act — and moral acts alone deserve reward. The town demanded a sign that would have destroyed the test; God sent a man who *was* the test.

**Answering "But Human" in Ourselves and Our Age**

The town's objection survives in modern dress and must be answered in the heart before it is answered on the tongue. *"The scholar is but human"* — true, and therefore his evidence, not his person, decides; reject the proof if thou canst, but do not dismiss the man for sharing thy species. *"The counsel came from my junior/my wife/my child"* — the town's disease in miniature: truth's value does not rise with the speaker's rank. *"Religion is man-made because men conveyed it"* — confusion of postman with author; the question is never whether the bearer eats food but whether the message mends souls, predicts truthfully, and coheres perfectly. Practically, the believer trains himself to *hear claims before weighing claimants*: when counsel stings, ask first "is it true?" and only later "who said it?" And when inviting others, take comfort: if heaven's own messengers were reduced to "but human," no inviter should expect immunity from reduction — "thou art but young," "but old," "but unqualified." The messengers' answer was not self-defense but renewed declaration (v. 16): restate the truth, strengthen the emphasis, and leave the verdict to God — who will, in the parable as in history, show within a single Cry whose word was weight and whose was wind.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:16

> **They said, "Our Lord knows. Verily we have indeed been sent unto you!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Our Lord Knows": Appeal From the Town's Court to Heaven's**

*"They said, 'Our Lord knows'"* — *qālū rabbunā yaʿlamu* — the messengers, called liars (v. 15), decline the town's tribunal and appeal to the one Witness who cannot be deceived. The move is profound in both theology and rhetoric. Theologically, it confesses that messengership is ultimately a matter between the Sender and the sent: no town's vote certifies it, no town's veto cancels it. God knows whom He sent; the sent know they were sent; the deniers' ignorance changes neither fact. The Qur'an later gives the Prophet ﷺ the identical appeal against identical denial:

> *"Say, 'God suffices as witness between me and you.'"* (Qur'an 13:43; cf. 29:52)

Rhetorically, the appeal reverses the burden: the town demanded the messengers prove themselves to men; the messengers submit their credentials to God and let the town prove *its* charge before Him. There is also quiet menace in the gentleness — "Our Lord knows" means He knows our truthfulness *and* your denial, our patience *and* your threats; the Witness for the defense is also the Judge of the prosecution. Al-Qurṭubī notes the messengers claim no miracle here, stage no spectacle, curse no one: they simply place the dispute in God's hands — the strongest act of the wronged, and the most frightening prospect for wrongdoers who believed no court was in session.

**The Second Declaration, More Emphatic Than the First**

*"Verily we have indeed been sent unto you!"* — *innā ilaykum la-mursalūn* — repeats the declaration of verse 14 (*innā ilaykum mursalūn*) with one decisive addition: the emphatic *lām* (*la-mursalūn*, "indeed sent"). The source commentary rightly observes: "the message bearers' second statement of their mission is more emphatic than the first, because it is a reaffirmation in the face of denial and rejection." The Arabic rhetoric is exact: verse 14 used *inna* (emphasis) alone; verse 16 doubles it (*inna* + *la-*), the Qur'an's standard escalation for answering contradiction — the greater the denial, the stronger the affirmation. The content does not change (truth does not revise itself under pressure); the *force* does. This is the prophetic pattern everywhere: Noah, commanded to build an ark on dry land amid mockery, built on; Hūd, called foolish by ʿĀd, repeated the call; Muḥammad ﷺ, boycotted and bereaved, recited on. The messengers neither retreat ("perhaps we were not sent") nor expand ("and we are more than sent") — they restate, reaffirm, and stand. Steadfastness (*thabāt*) under denial is itself a proof of commission: liars revise, embroider, or flee when challenged; the sent repeat.

**Why the Messengers Do Not Curse, Threaten, or Perform**

A striking feature of this verse is what it omits: no imprecation ("may God destroy you"), no ultimatum ("believe by Friday or else"), no miracle-on-demand. The messengers had been beaten and imprisoned (tradition on v. 14), called humans and liars (v. 15) — yet their reply contains only witness and restatement. The restraint is the prophetic ethic the sūrah holds up to Muḥammad ﷺ and, through him, to every inviter: the messenger controls the message, never the outcome; he owns the declaration, never the damnation. Cursing belongs to God if He wills (and the Cry of v. 29 will show He did); threatening belongs to revelation's warnings, not the warner's temper; miracles come by divine leave, not human pressure (cf. 6:109; 17:59). The Prophet ﷺ embodied the ethic at Ṭāʾif — bloodied, he refused the angel's offer to crush the town, saying instead:

> *"Rather, I hope that God brings forth from their loins those who worship God alone."* (al-Bukhārī 3231; Muslim 1795, from ʿĀʾishah)

The messengers of the town likewise leave the door open with their very restraint: so long as they only declare, the town may still receive. Only when the town threatens to stone (v. 18) does the parable's mercy begin its withdrawal.

**The Grammar of Certainty Under Contradiction**

The verse's Arabic grammar offers a masterclass in truthful speech under fire, and the classical rhetoricians (*ʿulamāʾ al-balāghah*) made it a textbook case. First principle: *match emphasis to denial* — state plainly to the open, emphatically to the resistant, most emphatically to the hostile. The messengers' escalation (v. 14 → v. 16) models it exactly. Second principle: *anchor certainty in the highest knowledge* — "Our Lord knows" precedes "we have been sent," rooting subjective conviction in objective omniscience. The inviter's confidence is thus never self-confidence ("trust me, I am sincere") but God-confidence ("God knows, and I convey"). Third principle: *repeat, do not reinvent* — the second declaration adds force, not content. Modern communicators, pressed by critics, often dilute ("what I meant was..."), deflect ("you misunderstand"), or dramatize; the messengers simply re-say. The lesson extends beyond preaching: the honest employee accused of theft, the faithful spouse accused of betrayal, the truthful witness disbelieved — all are taught here to appeal upward ("God knows"), restate plainly, and stand still. Heaven's vindication may come as a Cry (v. 29) or as a Garden (v. 26); it always comes.

**"God Knows" as the Believer's Refuge and Mirror**

For daily life, *"Our Lord knows"* is both shield and searchlight. As *shield*: when misjudged, misquoted, or maligned — by family, colleagues, or online crowds — the believer need not win every tribunal; he commits his cause to the Knower, restates truth once with emphasis, and refuses the spiral of endless self-defense. The Prophet ﷺ promised the fruit of such restraint:

> *"Whoever abandons disputation while he is right, a house is built for him in the highest Paradise."* (Abū Dāwūd 4800; al-Tirmidhī 1993, from Abū Umāmah; graded ḥasan)

As *searchlight*: "Our Lord knows" also falls upon the speaker himself — He knows whether my declaration is sincere or self-serving, whether my emphasis expresses conviction or temper, whether I invite to God or to myself. The messengers could appeal to divine knowledge because their hearts matched their words; the appeal is refuge only for the congruent. Practically: before any hard conversation — counsel to a friend, correction of a wrong, defense of innocence — pause and ask, "Can I say 'my Lord knows' over what I am about to utter?" If yes, speak and stand; if no, repent and be silent. The town's messengers spoke two sentences and changed eternity for one running man (v. 20) — proof that words weighed by heaven's knowledge need no weight from earth's applause.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:17

> **And naught is incumbent upon us save the clear proclamation."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Illā al-Balāgh al-Mubīn: The Job Description of Messengers**

*"And naught is incumbent upon us save the clear proclamation"* — *wa mā ʿalaynā illā al-balāghu al-mubīn* — *mā... illā* ("nothing but") fencing the messengers' duty on both sides: nothing *more* than proclamation is required of them, and nothing *less* than clarity will do. *Balāgh* is delivery — conveying the message intact to its addressees; *mubīn* is clarity — open, manifest, unambiguous, leaving no excuse of obscurity. Like the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ, the town's messengers are "only instructed to deliver the message and to guide those who heed it; they are not responsible for those who deny it" (see 36:10–11). The Qur'an hammers the job description across a dozen passages:

> *"The Messenger's duty is only the clear proclamation."* (Qur'an 5:92; cf. 3:20; 5:99; 13:40; 16:35, 82; 24:54; 29:18; 42:48; 64:12)

> *"Thou art not tasked with their guidance, but God guides whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 2:272; cf. 28:56)

Conversion is God's work; conveyance is the messenger's. The town demanded results it had no right to demand ("prove it by transforming us"); the messengers offer the only product heaven authorized: clear words, faithfully delivered. Every subsequent verse of the parable — the threats (v. 18), the running believer (v. 20), the Cry (v. 29) — unfolds from this division of labour: men proclaim, God disposes.

**Why Clarity Is the Messenger's Whole Burden**

The verse's single adjective, *clear* (*mubīn*), carries immense weight. Proclamation can fail in two directions: *obscurity* (truth garbled, jargon-choked, or half-spoken, so hearers cannot grasp it) and *compulsion* (truth forced, so hearers cannot freely choose it). *Mubīn* forbids the first; *"naught... save"* forbids the second. The messengers must speak so plainly that the town understands exactly what is claimed ("we have been sent unto you," v. 16) and what is required (abandon idols, worship the Compassionate, as the running man will spell out in vv. 22–25) — and then stop, leaving the will unviolated. This is why the Qur'an pairs proclamation with the removal of compulsion:

> *"There is no compulsion in religion. Rectitude has become clear distinct from error."* (Qur'an 2:256)

Clarity *replaces* coercion: when truth is made unmistakable, whoever rejects it rejects knowingly, and responsibility is complete without force. Al-Rāzī draws the legal corollary: the town's later punishment (v. 29) is just precisely because the proclamation was *mubīn* — punishment after unclear warning would be tyranny; after clear warning, it is justice. The clarity standard also judges every later proclaimer: the preacher who obscures (showing off erudition), the writer who confuses (hiding behind abstraction), the parent who commands without explaining — none has discharged *balāgh mubīn*. Heaven's messengers spoke plainly to plain folk; their heirs must do the same.

**Freedom From Results as Freedom For Persistence**

The verse's hidden gift is psychological liberation. A messenger responsible for *outcomes* would despair at verse 15's triple rejection ("human... nothing sent... liars"), bargain at verse 18's threats, and quit before verse 20's runner ever arrives. A messenger responsible only for *proclamation* cannot be defeated by rejection: his success was secured the moment he spoke clearly and sincerely. This is the theology that sustained Noah through 950 years of denial:

> *"And he remained among them a thousand years save fifty."* (Qur'an 29:14)

— centuries of "failure" by outcome-metrics, yet Noah stands among the five greatest messengers (*ulū al-ʿazm*) by fidelity-metrics. It sustained Muḥammad ﷺ through the Year of Sorrow, the boycott, and Ṭāʾif; it sustains every teacher whose students stray, every parent whose child rebels, every reformer whose people mock. Ibn Kathīr, commenting on this verse's parallels, stresses that the Prophet ﷺ was repeatedly consoled with exactly this boundary (6:107; 10:99; 88:21–22):

> *"So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a warder over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

The boundary does not lower zeal — the messengers spoke twice with rising emphasis (vv. 14, 16) — it *frees* zeal from the poison of despair. Work for God, not for numbers; numbers belong to Another's ledger.

**The Town's Accountability Is Sealed by This Sentence**

There is solemn legal consequence in the messengers' disclaimer: once duty is defined as clear proclamation, and once that proclamation has been delivered (vv. 14, 16) and will be further clarified by the running believer (vv. 20–25), the town's defense file is empty. It cannot plead ignorance ("no one told us" — three did), nor obscurity ("we could not understand" — fishermen and porters grasped it), nor compulsion ("we were forced to refuse" — none forced anything). All that remains is the will's naked choice — and the threats of verse 18 will expose that will fully. The Qur'an states the courtroom principle:

> *"Lest mankind have an argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

This verse is that principle spoken by the messengers themselves, in advance, on the record. Modern readers sometimes imagine divine judgment as arbitrary — thunderbolts upon the uninformed. The parable corrects the imagining step by step: sending (v. 14), declaring (vv. 14, 16), disclaiming coercion (v. 17), popular witness (vv. 20–25), persistence in threats (v. 18) — and *then* the Cry (v. 29). No step skipped, no excuse unburied. The God of the parable condemns no town unproclaimed-to; He condemns only towns that heard clearly and chose stones (v. 18).

**Proclaiming Clearly in Homes, Classrooms, and Public Squares**

The verse commissions every believer as a lesser proclaimer with the same two rails: *clarity* and *non-coercion*. Clarity in practice means: speak the listener's language (the messengers spoke Antioch's tongue, not heaven's); name the requirement concretely (not "be spiritual" but "pray, give, forgive"); distinguish thy words from God's ("the Qur'an says..." versus "in my opinion..."); and repeat without revision under pressure (vv. 14→16). Non-coercion means: invite, never impose — the parent teaches prayer by instruction, example, and loving discipline, never by crushing the child's will; the friend counsels once clearly, then leaves the door open; the online writer argues the point without damning the opponent. And when proclamation is rejected, the verse prescribes the messengers' response: do not escalate to cursing or violence (they left that to God), do not dilute to please (they repeated with emphasis), and do not despair of late hearers — for the parable's greatest fruit, the running man, appears *after* this disclaimer, proving that clear proclamation, faithfully discharged, travels further than its speakers know. Speak clearly; the runners are coming.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:18

> **They said, "Truly we augur ill of you. If you cease not, we shall certainly stone you, and a painful punishment will certainly befall you from us."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Taṭayyarnā Bikum: Blaming the Messengers for Bad Omens**

*"Truly we augur ill of you"* — *innā taṭayyarnā bikum* — *taṭayyur* (from *ṭayr*, "bird") is the ancient Arab practice of divining fortune from omens — originally the flight of birds, extended to all superstition: "since you came, our crops failed / our sick increased / our luck turned." The town's response to the messengers' clear proclamation (v. 17) is thus not counter-argument but *scapegoating*: attribute every misfortune to the righteous strangers. The Qur'an records the identical reflex against Moses:

> *"When good befell them, they said, 'This is ours.' And when evil befell them, they augured ill of Moses and those with him."* (Qur'an 7:131; cf. 27:47)

And against the Prophet Muḥammad's ﷺ followers in every age: famines blamed on the pious, plagues on the mosques, defeats on the reformers. The psychology is universal and worth dissecting: misfortune demands explanation, and explanation demands either self-examination (painful: "our sins brought this") or scapegoating (painless: "these strangers brought this"). The town chooses the painless lie. Note the evidential asymmetry: the messengers offered declarations backed by heaven's knowledge (v. 16); the town answers with *feelings* about luck — vibes against revelation. Superstition is disbelief's confession that it has no argument left: having failed to refute the message (vv. 14–17), it attacks the messengers' *aura*.

**From Superstition to Violence: "We Shall Certainly Stone You"**

*"If you cease not, we shall certainly stone you"* — *la-in lam tantahū la-narjumannakum* — *rajm* (stoning) being the town's threatened penalty for persistence in preaching. The grammar heaps emphasis: *la-in* (oath-conditional "if indeed") + *lam tantahū* ("you do not desist") + *la-narjumannakum* (oath-emphatic "we shall most certainly stone you"). Stoning is specifically the mob's murder — collective, participatory, requiring no authority's sword: every hand picks a stone, every conscience hides in the crowd. The threat reveals what argument could not: the town *fears* the message's power. One does not stone the obviously false — one laughs at it. Stones are reserved for words that might persuade: the crowds gathering, the questions spreading, the runner already on his way (v. 20). Quraysh followed the same escalation against the Prophet ﷺ: mockery → boycott → beating → assassination-plot — each step conceding the message's force while trying to crush its voice. The threatened stoning also darkly foreshadows the parable's first blood: tradition holds the running believer himself was stoned (see v. 26) — the town stones the message's fruit when it cannot stone heaven's root.

**"A Painful Punishment From Us": The Town Plays God**

*"And a painful punishment will certainly befall you from us"* — *wa la-yamassannakum minnā ʿadhābun alīm* — doubles the threat: not only death by stones but *ʿadhāb alīm* ("painful punishment") — the Qur'an's standard phrase for *divine* chastisement, here parodied on human lips. The town appoints itself god: it will inflict ʿadhāb, it will determine pain, its "from us" (*minnā*) apes heaven's "from Us." The blasphemy is precise: the messengers came from the Compassionate (v. 15 denied it; v. 16 reaffirmed it); the town answers as a rival compassionate-turned-wrathful deity — "our torment Lā you." Al-Rāzī notes the revelation's irony: the very phrase the town threatens (*ʿadhāb alīm*) is the phrase God will execute — upon the town, within a single Cry (v. 29). Threats against heaven's envoys are loans taken against one's own future: Pharaoh threatened to crucify the magicians who believed (7:124; 20:71) and drowned; the town threatens ʿadhāb and receives it. The Qur'an's law stands:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

Human "from us" dissolves before divine "from Us" — the town's two verses of threats (v. 18) are answered by God's one Cry (v. 29).

**The Anatomy of Persecution: Omen, Ultimatum, Torment**

The verse compresses persecution's universal three-act structure into a single sentence, and recognizing the structure arms every later victim and warns every later perpetrator. Act one, *stigmatize*: "we augur ill of you" — mark the righteous as unlucky, dangerous, extremist, divisive; make association with them socially costly. Act two, *ultimatum*: "if you cease not" — demand silence as the price of safety; the issue is framed as the victim's stubbornness ("just stop preaching/praying/wearing/teaching and none of this happens"). Act three, *torment*: "we shall stone you... painful punishment from us" — violence presented as the victim's choice ("you brought this on yourself"). Every persecution in history — Makkah's boycott, Madinah's hypocrites, and all later inquisitions — follows the script. The messengers' prior disclaimer (v. 17) exposes the script's injustice: they claimed only to proclaim clearly — the most non-violent act imaginable — and the town answers clarity with stones. Persecution is thus revealed as confession: the persecutor testifies that he has no words left, only rocks. And the sūrah's verdict on all three acts arrives swiftly: the omens rebound (v. 19), the ultimatum expires unheeded, and the torment returns to sender (vv. 28–29).

**Standing Firm When Truth Is Called Bad Luck**

The verse trains believers for the town's tactics in every age. When piety is called "bad luck" — the hijab blamed for unemployment, the beard for suspicion, the mosque for division, the prayer for "backwardness" — recognize *taṭayyur*, not analysis, and answer as the messengers will in verse 19: misfortune's cause lies in the accusers' own deeds, not the believers' presence. When the ultimatum comes — "cease, or else" — count the cost honestly (the messengers faced real stones, not metaphors) and remember the Prophet's ﷺ promise:

> *"The greatest striving (jihād) is a word of truth before a tyrannical ruler."* (Abū Dāwūd 4344; al-Tirmidhī 2174, from Abū Saʿīd; graded ḥasan)

— and his practice: he neither ceased nor cursed, neither fled the truth nor sought the stones. Practical counsel: do not answer superstition with superstition (no counter-omens, no fear of the town's curses — *"no affliction befalls save by God's leave,"* 64:11); do not answer ultimatums with panic or bravado, but with the messengers' calm restatement; document threats, protect the vulnerable, and commit the outcome to the Knower (v. 16). And never, ever join a stoning — literal or digital: the verse's mob, stones in hand, is the Qur'an's portrait of everyone who piles onto a truth-teller today. When the crowd lifts rocks (or hashtags) against one who spoke heaven's words clearly, the believer's place is not in the crowd but — as the next verses will show — running from the outskirts to stand beside the voiceless.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:19

> **They said, "Your auguring ill is upon yourselves though you have been reminded? Nay, you are a prodigal people!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṭāʾirukum Maʿakum: The Omen Is Yours, Not Ours**

*"Your auguring ill is upon yourselves"* — *ṭāʾirukum maʿakum* — *ṭāʾir* (literally "bird," hence omen, fortune) *with you*: the messengers return the town's superstition (v. 18) to sender. Your bad luck travels in your own saddlebags; your misfortunes are fastened to your own deeds, not to our footsteps. The Qur'an states the principle as cosmic law:

> *"And [for] every man We have fastened his omen upon his neck."* (Qur'an 17:13)

— each soul wears its fortune like a collar: obedience brings blessing, defiance brings ruin, and no stranger's arrival alters the ledger. The messengers thus replace *superstition* (misfortune caused by others' aura) with *moral causation* (misfortune caused by one's own choices) — the same correction Moses gave Pharaoh's people (7:131) and Ṣāliḥ gave Thamūd (27:47). The logic is testable: the town's crops, health, and fortunes were failing, if at all, because of its own idolatry, injustice, and now its persecution of heaven's envoys — heaven's displeasure follows heaven's messengers' rejection as night follows sunset. To blame the messengers for the cloud is to blame the physician for the fever he diagnosed. Al-Rāzī savours the justice: the town said "your presence is our bad omen"; the messengers reply "your rejection is your bad omen" — and within ten verses, the single Cry (v. 29) will prove whose omen it was.

**"Though You Have Been Reminded?": The Rebuke of Grace Refused**

*"Though you have been reminded?"* — *a-in dhukkirtum* — the verse's most delicate phrase, transmitted in several canonical readings that enrich rather than contradict. In the widespread reading (*a-in dhukkirtum*), it is an indignant question with eloquent ellipsis: "Do you augur ill and threaten stoning *even when you have been reminded?*" — the reminder (the messengers' message) should have *ended* superstition and violence, yet the town answers grace with stones. In another canonical reading (*ayna dhukkirtum*, "now that you have been reminded," i.e., *al-āna dhukkirtum*; al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī), the sense is temporal: "at this very moment of reminder — *now*, when heaven speaks to you — you threaten?" In a third (*an dhukkirtum*, "because you have been reminded"; al-Zamakhsharī), the town's rage is *caused* by the reminder: they threaten *because* they were warned — truth itself is the irritant. All three circle one rebuke: the reminder is favour, and answering favour with fury is perversity. The question-mark is the messengers' last diagnostic tool: they hold the mirror ("look what you are doing *while being reminded*") before the runner arrives (v. 20) and the door narrows. For Makkah's hearers, the echo was unmistakable: you too threaten the Reminder-bearer *because* he reminds — what does that make you?

**"Nay, You Are a Prodigal People": Isrāf of the Soul**

*"Nay, you are a prodigal people!"* — *bal antum qawmun musrifūn* — *bal* ("nay, rather") correcting the town's self-image, *musrifūn* from *isrāf* (prodigality, excess, transgression of bounds). *Isrāf* in the Qur'an covers all extravagance — of wealth (7:31), of food, of sin, and, climactically, of disbelief itself:

> *"Thus do We recompense the prodigal who believes not in the signs of his Lord."* (Qur'an 20:127; cf. 10:12 on the prodigal)

The town is *mus rif* because it exceeds every limit: in superstition (blaming the innocent), in ingratitude (answering reminder with threats), in violence (stoning for speech), and in persistence (two messengers denied, three threatened). Prodigality here is not merely wastefulness but *transgression-as-lifestyle* — a people that has normalized excess until threats sound reasonable and stones feel like justice. The label also quietly answers verse 18's "painful punishment from us": prodigals of violence will receive the wage of violence; the Qur'an's economic law is exact — *"your auguring... upon yourselves"* governs punishment too. The messengers do not curse the prodigals; they *diagnose* them — the way a physician names a disease plainly so the patient might yet seek cure. Naming is mercy: "prodigal" can still repent; only the Cry (v. 29) ends the clinic.

**The Reflexive Nature of Good and Evil Deeds**

The verse's first clause states what scholars call the Qur'an's *reflexive* moral law: deeds rebound upon doers. The Qur'an repeats the physics of this rebound across a dozen images: charity spent *"is for yourselves, when you spend only seeking the Face of God"* (2:272); miserliness — *"whosoever is miserly is only miserly unto himself"* (47:38); deception — *"they deceive none but themselves"* (2:9); misguidance — *"they lead none astray but themselves"* (3:69; 4:113); plotting — God plots against the plotters (52:42); and the summary:

> *"Whosoever does evil, it is against himself."* (Qur'an 4:111; cf. 17:7; 41:46)

The omen-verse is this law applied to persecution: stones lifted against messengers are stones mortgaged against the lifters; threats of ʿadhāb are drafts drawn on the threateners' own account. History in the parable will balance the books within verses; history outside it balances them across generations — Pharaoh's drowning, Quraysh's Badr, every tyranny's Cry. The reflexive law also governs the messengers' side: their patience under threat rebounds as honour eternal — recited in Yā Sīn by millions, nightly, until the Last Day. Every deed is a bird (*ṭāʾir*) released: it circles, and it returns — to its releaser's own neck (17:13). Choose, then, which birds to loose.

**Returning Omens, Rejecting Superstition, Naming Excess**

Three practices flow from the verse into daily life. First, *refuse superstitious blame*: when misfortune strikes — illness, loss, failure — the Qur'anic reflex is self-examination and repentance, not scapegoating the pious neighbour, the new mosque, or the "unlucky" person. The Prophet ﷺ demolished *ṭiyarah* (omen-taking) wholesale:

> *"There is no contagion [by itself], no omen-taking (ṭiyarah), no hâmah, no Ṣafar."* (al-Bukhārī 5757; Muslim 2220, from Abū Hurayrah)

— and taught optimism (*faʾl*) in its place: the believer expects good from God and examines himself in hardship. Second, *receive reminders without rage*: when counsel stings, notice the town's reflex rising ("who are you to remind me?") and choose the runner's reflex instead (v. 20: run *toward* the reminder). Anger at admonition is prodigality's pulse; gratitude for it is guidance's. Third, *name excess plainly but mercifully*: the messengers called the town *musrifūn* — direct, unsoftened, yet without curse or cruelty. The parent names the child's extravagance, the friend names the friend's transgression, the citizen names the regime's excess — not to damn but to diagnose, while the clinic of repentance stands open. For the verse's last mercy is its timing: "you are prodigal" was spoken *before* the Cry — diagnosis while cure remained. Every *musrif* who hears this verse hears it before *his* Cry; the omen is still, rewriteably, upon himself — and repentance rewrites omens into blessings.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:20

> **And from the outskirts of the city, a man came running. He said, "O my people! Follow the message bearers!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Min Aqṣā al-Madīnah: The Believer From the Margins**

*"And from the outskirts of the city"* — *wa jāʾa min aqṣā al-madīnati* — *aqṣā* meaning the farthest, remotest quarter: the believer lives at the city's edge, far from its palaces, temples, and mobs. The geography is theology. Truth's champions so often come from the margins — Moses from exile, the Prophet ﷺ an orphan, Bilāl an Abyssinian slave — while centres of power produce the threats (v. 18). The outskirts-dweller sees what the centre cannot: distance from the crowd's noise preserves clarity, and distance from power's favours preserves courage. Tradition names him Ḥabīb al-Najjār (Ḥabīb the Carpenter), described as one who "worshipped God in seclusion" (al-Alūsī) — a hidden monotheist before the messengers arrived, whose solitary faith had prepared him to recognize public truth. Some accounts add that he followed the first two messengers after they healed his son (al-Alūsī, al-Qurṭubī) — gratitude opening into conviction. Whether through secluded worship or witnessed mercy, the margin-dweller arrives at the centre's crisis with the one asset the centre lacks: a heart already God's. Makkah's hearers would have felt the parallel's sting: *your* margins — slaves, youths, outsiders — are believing (Bilāl, ʿAmmār, Ṣuhayb) while your centre plots. God has always drafted His vanguard from the outskirts.

**Rajulan Yasʿā: Running — The Verse's Central Verb**

*"A man came running"* — *rajulun yasʿā* — *saʿy* meaning to run, hasten, strive with urgency. The Qur'an could have said "came" or "walked" or "arrived"; it says *running*. Every element of the verb preaches. *Running* means he heard the news (messengers threatened with stoning, v. 18) and *moved* — faith as locomotion, not sentiment. *Running* means he came from *far* (the outskirts) yet arrived *fast* — distance excuses absence, and he refused the excuse. *Running* means he feared arriving *late* — stones were being gathered; counsel delayed is counsel denied. The Qur'an honours such haste elsewhere:

> *"So hasten toward forgiveness from your Lord."* (Qur'an 3:133)

> *"And the foremost, the foremost — they are the ones brought near."* (Qur'an 56:10–11)

Tradition counts him among history's three supreme fore-runners. A report attributed to the Prophet ﷺ declares:

> *"The foremost among the people are three who did not disbelieve in God for the blinking of an eye: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, the companion of Yā Sīn, and the believer among the people of Pharaoh [40:28–29]. They are the truthful (ṣiddīqūn)."* (Cited by al-Suyūṭī, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Ṭabarsī)

The runner's haste is thus canonized as *ṣiddīqiyyah* — supreme truthfulness expressed as speed. Faith that walks when it could run has not yet understood the stones are rising.

**"O My People!": Love For the Very Crowd That Will Stone Him**

*"He said, 'O my people!'"* — *yā qawmi* — two words of aching tenderness opening his plea. He does not say "O idolaters," "O tyrants," "O ignorant ones" — though they are all three and will soon murder him (tradition on v. 26). He says *"my people"* — *qawmī*, possessive, belonging, loving. His identity is with them even as his theology is against them; he claims them while they will disown him. This is the prophetic etiquette of address: Noah — *"O my people, worship God"* (7:59); Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb — each *"O my people"* to peoples that rejected them. Love precedes logic; belonging authorizes bluntness. The runner's affection also fulfills the messengers' ethic (vv. 16–17) in popular form: no curse, no contempt, only kinship urgently pleading. Psychologically, *"my people"* disarms the crowd's frame — they expected a partisan of foreigners ("the messengers' man"); he presents as their own ("your man, from your outskirts, running for your sake"). Persuasion's first battle is membership: insiders are heard, outsiders stoned. The runner wins membership with two words — and then spends it entirely, asking them to follow the outsiders (the messengers). Love that risks itself for the beloved's eternity: this is *naṣīḥah* (sincere counsel) incarnate.

**"Follow the Message Bearers!": The Plea in Its Boldness**

*"Follow the message bearers!"* — *ittabiʿū al-mursalīn* — the imperative (*ittabiʿū*, "follow!") plural, addressed to the whole threatening crowd: the lone runner commands the mob. Consider the courage's dimensions: he is one, they are a city; he is from the outskirts, they hold the centre; they have just vowed stoning (v. 18), and he walks into the stoning-ground to recruit for the victims. His plea's logic will unfold across verses 21–25 (they ask no reward, they are guided; my Creator deserves my worship; your gods cannot intercede; I believe — listen to me), but the command precedes the argument: *follow!* — then *why*. Urgency orders it so: stones rise fast, syllogisms slow. The command also reveals the runner's own station: he does not say "follow *me*" but "follow *them*" — the truest followers deflect following to their guides, the truest inviters to God route hearers past themselves. He is a signpost that runs. And his plea's scope is total — *ittabiʿū* (follow) meaning full adherence, not partial admiration: embrace their God, abandon your gods, reorder your city. Half-following would not save the town from the Cry (v. 29); the runner demands the whole cure because he sees the whole disease.

**Running From Our Own Outskirts Today**

The verse is the sūrah's summons to action, and its applications run in every direction. *Run when truth is threatened*: the news that reaches thee — a truth-teller mocked at work, a mosque opposed, a reformer isolated, a friend sliding toward ruin — is thy messenger-threatened moment. The town's majority stayed home; the outskirts' one ran. God records runners, not spectators. *Run from wherever thou art*: "outskirts" excuses none — the youth ("I am too young"), the elder ("too old"), the new Muslim ("too ignorant"), the sinner ("too stained"), the distant ("too far") — the runner owned every excuse and ran anyway. His qualification was not learning or lineage but *arrival*: he showed up, urgently. *Run with "O my people" on thy lips*: counsel thy family, colleagues, and nation as *thine* — with belonging, not contempt. The activist who despises his people cannot guide them; the runner loved his murderers-to-be. The Prophet ﷺ modeled it at Ṭāʾif (bloodied, praying for their progeny) and at the conquest of Makkah (injured, declaring *"no blame upon you today,"* 12:92). *Run before the stones fly*: counsel proffered after ruin is eulogy; the runner ran *while* threats were threats. That phone call, that visit, that verse sent, that apology offered — run, for thou knowest not when thy town's Cry (or thy own last breath) falls. And remember the runner's rank: unnamed in the verse (God says only *"a man"*), yet immortal — *"the companion of Yā Sīn,"* a *ṣiddīq*, recited wherever Yā Sīn is recited until time ends. Heaven's roll-call honours runners above rulers: the town's king is forgotten dust, while the outskirts' runner is prayed for by millions. Obscurity that runs outshines eminence that sits.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:21

> **Follow those who ask not of you any reward and who are guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Lā Yasʾalukum Ajran: The First Test — No Price Tag on Truth**

*"Follow those who ask not of you any reward"* — *ittabiʿū man lā yasʾalukum ajran* — the running believer's (v. 20) first argument for the messengers: their message carries no invoice. *Ajr* (reward, wage, fee) covers every currency of self-interest: money, status, power, followers, flattery. The messengers sought none — they arrived as strangers, endured beating and prison (tradition on v. 14), faced stoning (v. 18), and asked only to be heard. This disinterestedness is the Qur'an's universal litmus test for prophethood. Noah declares it:

> *"And if you turn your backs, I have not asked of you any reward. My reward lies only with God, and I am commanded to be among those who submit."* (Qur'an 10:72)

And the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ is commanded to declare it thrice:

> *"Say, 'I ask not of you any reward for it.'"* (Qur'an 6:90; 38:86; 42:23; cf. 26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180)

The logic is razor-sharp: liars lie *for* something — wealth, dominion, desire. Strip away every possible gain, add persecution as the certain wage, and lying becomes inexplicable — no one pays stones to purchase falsehood. The messengers' poverty of motive is thus the wealth of their proof. The town demanded credentials ("you are but human," v. 15); the runner supplies the one credential that cannot be forged: *they want nothing from you*. What they want is *for* you — your salvation — which is precisely what no liar wants.

**Wa Hum Muhtadūn: The Second Test — Guided Themselves**

*"And who are guided"* — *wa hum muhtadūn* — the second test: the messengers not only guide but *are* guided — walking the road they point to. *Ihtidāʾ* (guidedness) means inward possession of truth: sound doctrine, upright character, coherent lives. The runner, who knew them (tradition: he had followed them after his son's healing, or recognized their worship), testifies from observation: these men pray to one God, live purely, speak truthfully, endure patiently — their lives *are* their message's commentary. The two tests together form an airtight syllogism: (1) they seek nothing from you (so they do not deceive for gain); (2) they are guided themselves (so they do not err in content). Disinterested *and* upright: what remains but truth? The order matters: disinterestedness first (motive), guidedness second (substance) — for a sincere but misguided man misleads innocently, while a guided but mercenary man sells truth corruptly. The messengers pass both; the town's priests and soothsayers — who charged for oracles and lived by temple revenues — failed both. Makkah's hearers could apply the tests instantly: Muḥammad ﷺ asked no wage (though Quraysh offered him kingship and wealth to desist) and lived the guided life visibly (*al-ṣādiq al-amīn* before revelation, the night-vigils after). The runner's criteria convict Makkah's rejection as surely as the town's.

**Why the Runner Argues Credentials Before Theology**

Notice the apologetic strategy: before arguing *what* is true (vv. 22–24: one Creator, no interceding idols), the runner argues *whom* to trust (v. 21). The sequence is psychologically masterful. A hostile crowd cannot process theology while distrusting theologians — every doctrine will be filtered through "but these men are liars" (v. 15). So the runner first detoxifies the source: *whatever you think of their claim, look at their character — unpaid, upright*. Only then (vv. 22–25) does he present the content, and even then in first-person experiential form ("*why should I not worship...?*" — v. 22), not as the messengers' dictate. Modern persuaders call this *ethos before logos* (credibility before logic); the outskirts' carpenter practiced it millennia before the label. The strategy also protects the runner: he speaks as fellow-townsman and witness, not as fourth messenger — his testimony ("I have seen these men; they ask nothing, they live purely") is evidence the crowd can verify without accepting revelation first. Al-Rāzī notes the wisdom: the runner builds a bridge of commonly-accepted values (everyone honours selflessness and integrity) toward uncommonly-rejected truths (one God, coming judgment). Begin where the hearer stands; walk him to where he must go.

**The Two Tests as Eternal Discernment Tools**

The verse bequeaths every generation two questions for vetting religious claimants — and the Qur'an's rogues' gallery shows why both are needed. *Balaam-like* figures (7:175–176) had knowledge (guided in content) but sold it for worldly reward (failed test one) — learning without disinterestedness becomes sorcery for hire. *Kharijite-like* zealots may seek no money (passing test one in form) but lack guidance (failing test two) — sincerity without soundness becomes terrorism. True guides pass both: the Prophet ﷺ, his Companions, and their heirs in scholarship and piety — men and women who teach without charging the needy, counsel without courting followers, and live what they preach when none watches. The tests also expose modern counterfeits: the preacher whose gospel enriches him (private jets from donations), the guru whose liberation enslaves followers to himself, the ideologue whose revolution installs him — each fails test one regardless of eloquence. And the content test (two) requires the hearer to *learn*: one cannot judge guidedness without knowing guidance — which is why the Qur'an commands study of the Reminder (v. 11) alongside trust in the trustworthy. Follow the unpaid and the upright; verify uprightness by the Book; and when both align — *follow*, as the runner commanded, without delay.

**Becoming One Who Can Be Followed**

The verse finally turns its tests upon the believer himself, for every Muslim is someone's potential guide — to children, friends, colleagues, readers. *Ask no reward*: serve, teach, counsel, and help without invoicing — neither money, nor praise, nor leverage, nor even gratitude demanded. The Prophet ﷺ promised the wage-structure of selflessness:

> *"No one eats any food that is better than that from the work of his own hand."* (al-Bukhārī 2072, from al-Miqdām)

— earn thy living independently, so thy counsel stays unpurchased. The mother who serves without guilt-tripping, the teacher who helps without favouritism, the friend who advises without superiority — each passes test one. *Be guided*: live the counsel — pray what thou preachest, forgive as thou advisest, abandon what thou condemnest. Children detect unguided guidance instantly ("thou smokest yet forbiddest smoking"); so do colleagues and crowds. The runner could point to the messengers because they passed; can others point to thee? The terrible inverse also warns: whoever seeks followers for ego, wealth, or power while misguided becomes the town's priest, not its runner — and the Cry (v. 29) awaits systems built on paid falsehood. But the glorious promise stands: the unpaid, upright inviter — however obscure, however outskirts — is the runner's heir, and the runner's end (vv. 26–27: Garden and honour) is his horizon. Ask nothing; be guided; and watch God send runners to *thy* aid as He sent one to the messengers'.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:22

> **Why should I not worship Him who originated me, and unto Whom you will be returned?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa Mā Liya: The Question That Answers Itself**

*"Why should I not worship...?"* — *wa mā liya lā aʿbudu* — "and what is with me, that I should not worship...?" The runner (v. 20) frames his theology as a rhetorical question — speech's most disarming form. He does not bark "worship God!" at the stone-holding crowd; he wonders aloud, as if thinking in their presence: *what reason could I possibly have for withholding worship?* The question contains its answer (none), yet invites the hearer to supply it — and in supplying it ("indeed, no reason"), the hearer convicts himself. This is persuasion's gentlest weapon: the first-person (*I*) shields the second-person (*you*) from direct assault — the crowd listens to a man's self-reflection rather than enduring a preacher's indictment, and the reflection's logic seeps past defenses. Al-Rāzī admires the diplomacy: the runner, one against a city, cannot afford the messengers' declarative mode ("we have been sent"); he adopts the inquisitive mode, making the crowd his jury rather than his target. And the jury, honestly asked, can return only one verdict: nothing excuses refusing worship to one's Originator. The question that answers itself is thus also the question that judges its hearers.

**Faṭaranī: Worshipping the One Who Originated Me**

*"Him who originated me"* — *alladhī faṭaranī* — *faṭara* meaning to originate, create primordially, bring into being without precedent — the same root as *fiṭrah*, the primordial nature upon which God fashioned mankind:

> *"Set thy face to religion as a ḥanīf, in the primordial nature (fiṭrah) from God upon which He originated (faṭara) mankind — there is no altering the creation of God; that is the upright religion, but most of mankind know not."* (Qur'an 30:30)

The runner's argument is therefore ontological before it is scriptural: my very *making* obligates my worship. The One who designed my eyes deserves their tears; who shaped my tongue, its praise; who authored my existence, its purpose. The Qur'an states the purpose-clause explicitly:

> *"And I did not create jinn and mankind, save to worship Me."* (Qur'an 51:56)

Read with 30:30 and 51:56, the verse implies, as the source commentary notes, "that the worship of God is central to the primordial nature with which human beings were created" — worship is not an imposition upon nature but nature's fulfillment, the way flight fulfills the bird and fragrance the flower. The runner, likely an unlettered carpenter, thus voices natural theology's master-argument: existence is gift, gift implies Giver, Giver deserves gratitude, and gratitude's highest form is worship. No seminary required — only the honesty to ask *wa mā liya* ("what is with me?") and the courage to follow the answer.

**"And Unto Whom You Will Be Returned": The Turn to the Crowd**

*"And unto Whom you will be returned"* — *wa ilayhi turjaʿūn* — the runner pivots mid-verse from *I* (my origination) to *you* (your return): second-person plural, the whole crowd addressed. The shift is deliberate and devastating: origination (*faṭara*) looks backward to creation, return (*turjaʿūn*) forward to judgment — the sentence spans the entirety of existence, birth to resurrection, and places God at both ends. You came from Him without consultation; you will return to Him without escape. The passive (*turjaʿūn*, "you will be returned") stresses inevitability: return is not a journey one may decline but a tide that takes all — as the sūrah's closing verse will seal: *"unto Whom you shall be returned"* (v. 83). For the crowd, the clause transforms the runner's personal question into their universal warning: *I* worship because *I* was originated; *you* will answer because *you* will be returned. The eschatological turn also answers the town's deeper evasion: idolatry is often less about metaphysics than about avoiding accountability — many gods diffuse judgment, while one Originator-Returner concentrates it. The runner concentrates it in five words: one Source behind you, one Tribunal before you. Choose worship now, or compulsion then.

**The Runner's Theology: Complete Monotheism in One Verse**

Marvel at the verse's doctrinal completeness: in a single sentence, the outskirts' carpenter affirms *tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* (God as originating Lord), *tawḥīd al-ulūhiyyah* (God alone as worthy of worship — implied in "why should I *not*" worship Him, i.e., why worship any other?), and *maʿād* (the Return). Creation, worship, judgment: the three pillars of the sūrah's message (see Introduction) compressed into one breath. He argues nothing from the messengers' authority ("because they said so") but from universally accessible premises: you exist (undeniable), existence requires an Originator (rationally necessary), the Originator deserves worship (morally obvious), and death requires an account (universally suspected). This is *fiṭrah*-apologetics — argument from premises the idolater already holds: the town's pagans themselves believed in a supreme Creator (as the Qur'an repeatedly notes — *"If thou askest them who created the heavens and the earth, they will say God,"* 31:25), so the runner begins on common ground (the Originator) and walks them to disputed ground (exclusive worship, certain return). Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's implicit challenge to the town's gods: which of your idols *originated* you? Which will you be *returned* to? Silence is the only honest answer — and silence, before a running man's question, is the beginning of guidance.

**Asking "Why Should I Not Worship?" in Daily Life**

The verse gives every believer a diagnostic question for moments of spiritual sluggishness. *Why should I not pray?* — He who originated my limbs requests their bowing minutes a day; what reason withstands the asking? *Why should I not give?* — He who originated my wealth's means requests a fraction for His poor; what excuse survives *wa mā liya*? *Why should I not forgive, veil, fast, repent, recite?* — each reluctance, interrogated by the runner's question, dissolves like mist, for reluctance's reasons (laziness, pride, fear of people) are embarrassments, not arguments. The question also reframes worship from burden to coherence: the runner does not ask "why *should* I worship?" (as if worship needed justification) but "why should I *not*?" — worship is the default, the natural, the expected; *refusal* is the anomaly requiring excuse. Live this inversion: let prayer be the given and missing it the scandal; let honesty be the reflex and lying the crisis. And when inviting others, borrow the runner's grammar: wonder aloud rather than thunder down — "What reason have I for skipping worship of the One who made me?" — and let the hearer's own *fiṭrah* (30:30) answer. Finally, tether every day's beginning (origination: "He made this day for me") to its end (return: "unto Him I go"): the verse's two poles, remembered morning and evening, straighten the hours between them into a path — the straight one (v. 4) the runner ran.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:23

> **Shall I take gods apart from Him? If the Compassionate desired harm for me, their intercession would avail me naught, nor would they save me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A-Attakhidhu: The Absurdity of Adopting Gods**

*"Shall I take gods apart from Him?"* — *a-attakhidhu min dūnihi ālihah* — the interrogative *a-* plus the emphatic first-person: "Shall *I* — I who was originated by Him (v. 22), I who will be returned to Him — *adopt* gods instead?" *Ittikhādh* (taking, adopting) exposes idolatry's artificiality: gods are *taken* — selected, crafted, appointed — by the very creatures who then bow to them. The carpenter (tradition's Ḥabīb al-Najjār) knew wood: he had himself shaped timber into beams and perhaps into idols — and the man who has carved a god cannot worship it without laughing or weeping. The Qur'an presses the absurdity across a dozen passages — Abraham to his father:

> *"O my father, why dost thou worship that which hears not, sees not, and avails thee naught?"* (Qur'an 19:42)

And the temple-scene's devastating logic:

> *"They worship apart from God that which neither harms them nor benefits them."* (Qur'an 10:18)

The runner's question, as al-Shawkānī notes, means: "Shall I take gods other than God, worship them, and leave the worship of the One Who is worthy of worship?" The verb *take* convicts: the true God is not *taken* — He *takes* us (originates, sustains, returns); gods that must be taken are projects, not principals. Monotheism is thus realism (acknowledging the Unadopted Originator); polytheism is artifice (adopting the non-originating). The carpenter asks the city of craftsmen: shall the maker adopt his make as maker?

**"If the Compassionate Desired Harm": The Stress Test of Divinity**

*"If the Compassionate desired harm for me"* — *in yuridi al-Raḥmānu bi-ḍurr* — the runner subjects the town's gods to theology's stress test: a real god must *protect* — must be able to shield his worshipper when ultimate harm threatens. The conditional (*in*, "if") concedes the scenario arguendo: suppose the Compassionate (*al-Raḥmān* — the town's own acknowledged name for God, v. 15) wills harm (*ḍurr* — affliction, punishment, the eschatological sentence) for me. What then? The test is brilliant in its framing: it does not ask whether idols can *bless* (charms and harvests can be faked or coincided) but whether they can *rescue* — for rescue is the one divine service that cannot be counterfeited, and the Day of Judgment its final examination. The Qur'an applies the same test to all false saviours:

> *"Say, 'Have you considered those whom you call upon apart from God? Show me what they created of the earth, or whether they have a share in the heavens.'"* (Qur'an 46:4)

And the angels' own confession on that Day will generalize the runner's point: the worshipped will disown the worshippers (25:17–18; 28:62–64). The runner asks the town to run the simulation *now*, before the Cry (v. 29) runs it for them: picture yourselves before the Compassionate's tribunal — which god steps forward?

**"Their Intercession Would Avail Me Naught, Nor Would They Save Me"**

*"Their intercession would avail me naught"* — *lā tughni ʿannī shafāʿatuhum shayʾan* — *shafāʿah* (intercession) being the idolaters' chief hope: "our gods will plead for us." The runner denies it absolutely (*shayʾan*, "anything at all"). *"Nor would they save me"* — *wa lā yunqidhūn* — *inqādh* (rescue, deliverance by force): neither pleading nor power, neither advocacy nor extraction. The double denial is total: the gods can neither *persuade* God nor *overpower* Him — the only two conceivable modes of rescue. Qur'anic intercession-theology confirms the boundaries: intercession *exists* — angels and prophets will intercede — but *entirely by God's leave*:

> *"On that Day intercession will be of no benefit, save [that of] those whom the Compassionate has granted leave and with whose word He is content."* (Qur'an 20:109)

> *"Who is there who may intercede with Him save by His Leave?"* (Qur'an 2:255; cf. 2:48; 2:255c)

Idols, devils, and tyrants receive no such leave; they cannot even intercede for themselves, let alone their votaries. The runner's logic is thus airtight within the town's own premises: you admit a supreme Compassionate (you swore by Him, v. 15); the supreme will is irresistible; therefore subordinate "gods" are either His obedient servants (in which case worship *Him*, not them) or impotent rivals (in which case abandon them). Either way: *shall I take gods apart from Him?* — never.

**The Carpenter's Logic and the City's Economy**

The verse also carries an economic edge the first hearers would have felt. The town's gods were not merely statues but *systems* — temples with treasuries, priests with fees, festivals with markets, oaths with fines. "Taking gods" meant paying them: vows, sacrifices, tributes. The runner's question therefore threatens an economy: if the gods cannot intercede or rescue, every coin spent on them is tribute to impotence. His own trade sharpens the point: a carpenter knows the cost of wood and the worthlessness of the carved after the carving — the idol is lumber plus labour, and only the labour was ever real. The Qur'an generalizes the economics of idolatry:

> *"Those whom you call upon apart from Him own not even the pellicle of a date stone."* (Qur'an 35:13)

Modern idolatries replicate the economy precisely: wealth that cannot intercede at 3 a.m. against dread, status that cannot rescue from the grave, influencers and ideologies that plead nothing before the tribunal — each exacts lifelong tribute and defaults at the final audit. The runner's stress test applies to them all: *when the Compassionate desires — a reckoning, a cancer, a Cry — what avails?* Only what He authorizes: faith, repentance, righteous deeds, and the intercession He grants to the righteous (above all the Prophet's ﷺ *shafāʿah ʿuẓmā*, the Greatest Intercession — al-Bukhārī 4712; Muslim 194). Invest, then, where rescue is real.

**Choosing Saviours Before the Day of No Saving**

Practically, the verse is a portfolio review for the soul. *List thy "gods"*: whatever thou servest, trustest, or fearest apart from Him — career, wealth, patrons, appetites, anxieties, the opinions of crowds. *Stress-test each*: if the Compassionate desired harm — illness, loss, death, judgment — would this intercede? Would it rescue? The honest answer liquidates false holdings: no promotion pleads at the grave, no fortune extracts from the Fire, no crowd's approval weighs on the Scale. *Transfer the holdings*: worship (prayer, reliance, love, fear) to the One whose leave governs all intercession. The Prophet ﷺ taught the transfer's daily form:

> *"When thou askest, ask of God; and when thou seekest help, seek help from God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516, from Ibn ʿAbbās; graded ṣaḥīḥ)
