And for inviters, the runner models *argument by rescue*: when counselling the idolater of modernity (the wealth-worshipper, the desire-worshipper, the self-worshipper), ask his gentle question — "When ultimate harm comes, will this save thee?" — and let the silence preach. For the runner's question, first asked running (v. 20) before a mob with stones (v. 18), has outlived the mob, the stones, and the gods: asked in Yā Sīn, recited over the dying (see Introduction), it still performs its surgery — cutting adopted gods away from originated hearts, one honest answer at a time.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:24

> **Truly would I then be in manifest error.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Idhan Lafī Ḍalāl Mubīn: The Verdict Upon Himself**

*"Truly would I then be"* — *innī idhan* — *idhan* ("then, in that case") making the sentence conditional upon verse 23's hypothesis: *if* I took impotent gods apart from the Originator-Returner (v. 22) who alone decides harm and rescue (v. 23), *then* — the verdict — *"I would be in manifest error"* (*lafī ḍalālin mubīn*). The *la-* (emphatic "truly") seals the self-judgment. Note the courage of the grammar: the runner pronounces upon *himself* the sentence the crowd deserves — he places his own neck in the verdict's collar first, letting the town overhear its own diagnosis in his self-warning. Had he said "truly *you* are in manifest error," stones would have flown mid-sentence; saying "*I* would be," he holds the mob rapt — and every honest listener completes the transfer: *if he would be astray for it, what are we, who do it?* This is the rhetoric of *taʿrīḍ* (indirect address): condemn the sin upon oneself hypothetically, and let the sinners' consciences apply it actually. The prophets used it; the wise still do; the runner, carpenter of wood, proves carpenter of words — joining warning to tact so seamlessly the joint is invisible.

**Ḍalāl Mubīn: Error That Is Manifest — And That Manifests**

*"Manifest error"* — *ḍalāl mubīn* — *ḍalāl* (straying, error) from the root of losing the road (*ḍalla*: to go astray, to lose one's way), *mubīn* (manifest, clear) in the active sense: error that *makes manifest* — that reveals, exposes, displays itself. The commentators (al-Ṭabarī; cf. al-Bayḍāwī's reading *ḍalāl mubayyan*, "error made manifest") draw out the double clarity: idolatry is error *evident* to reason (worshipping the carved instead of the Carver needs no seminary to refute) and error *evidencing* — it manifests the strayer's inward ruin outwardly, the way fever manifests infection. The Qur'an applies *ḍalāl mubīn* across a catalogue of strayings — to those who take devils as patrons (4:119), who disobey God and His messenger after clear decree (33:36), who worship what neither harms nor benefits (22:12), and to Quraysh's own charge reversed upon them (the town called guidance error; heaven calls their error manifest). The phrase's frequency is itself a mercy: God *names* straying plainly so none strays unwarned — the road-signs are *mubīn* (clear), and only the deliberately blind (vv. 8–9) miss them. The runner's use is climactic within his plea: verses 22–23 argued (Originator deserves worship; idols cannot rescue); verse 24 *concludes* — therefore, idolatry is not an alternative opinion but *manifest* straying. Logic has delivered its verdict; only the will's surrender (v. 25) remains.

**Why the Runner Judges the Sin, Not (Yet) the Sinners**

A subtle mercy governs the verse: the runner declares the *act* manifest error without declaring the *actors* manifestly damned. The conditional (*idhan*, "would be") keeps the sentence hypothetical and therefore the door open: *were* I to do it, I *would* be astray — implying *you who do it are astray, yet may cease to be*. This is the Qur'anic etiquette of takfīr-avoidance in miniature: describe falsehood plainly, invite persons lovingly, and leave final judgments to God. The messengers modeled it (vv. 16–17: declare, disclaim damnation); the runner mirrors it; the Prophet ﷺ commanded it:

> *"Whoever says to his brother, 'O disbeliever,' it returns upon one of them."* (al-Bukhārī 6104; Muslim 60, from Ibn ʿUmar)

The distinction between *describing error* and *damning the erring* is the difference between a physician (who names the cancer to cure the patient) and an executioner (who names it to bury him). The runner is physician: his "manifest error" is diagnosis shouted urgently in the emergency ward, with treatment (v. 25: *"I believe... so listen to me"*) offered in the next breath. Modern inviters must preserve the distinction under fiercer pressure: the age demands either silence about error (tolerance as taboo on truth) or damnation of the erring (takfīr as sport). The runner's verse forbids both: name the straying *manifest*, love the straying *man*, and run — always run — with the cure.

**Manifest to Whom? The Epistemology of Obviousness**

The verse raises a philosophical question: if idolatry is *manifest* error, why do multitudes — the town, Quraysh, modernity's clever — commit it? The sūrah has already answered: shackles (v. 8), barriers and veils (v. 9), heedlessness (v. 6), and prodigality (v. 19) obscure the manifest. Manifestness is objective (the road-sign is clear); perception is conditional (the veiled eye misses it). The Qur'an states the paradox:

> *"And We have made clear the signs for a people who know... for a people who reflect... for a people who hear."* (Qur'an 2:118; 10:24; 30:28 — refrains across the Book)

— clarity is complete, yet received only by the knowing, reflecting, hearing. The sun is manifest; the blind man misses it through no fault of the sun. This resolves the tension between "manifest error" (v. 24) and "they see not" (v. 9): the error is manifest *in itself*, veiled *to them* by their own persistent refusal — and the runner's plea is heaven's attempt to unveil it before the Cry unveils everything (vv. 29, 52). The epistemological mercy: so long as a warner still speaks (the runner is speaking!), the veil may still lift. Manifestness plus proclamation equals inexcusability — but also opportunity: the manifest needs only one honest glance to be seen. The runner begs for that glance.

**Calling Error Manifest While Loving the Erring**

The verse's practice divides into three disciplines. First, *clarity about falsehood*: the believer must inwardly regard shirk (associating partners with God) — in its gross forms (idols, graves, sorcerers) and subtle forms (showing off, wealth-worship, desire-obedience) — as *ḍalāl mubīn*, not as lifestyle choice. The Prophet ﷺ warned of the subtle form:

> *"The most frightening thing I fear for my community is associating others with God. I do not mean they will worship sun, moon, or idols. I mean they will perform works for other than God with a hidden desire."* (Aḥmad 4:124, from Shaddād ibn Aws — cited on 36:74)

Name inward idols manifestly: this showing-off is straying, this riyāʾ is error — clarity is repentance's prerequisite. Second, *gentleness toward persons*: correct the erring as the runner did — hypothetically ("were I to do this, I would be astray"), lovingly (*"O my people,"* v. 20), urgently (running, v. 20) — never with contempt, mockery, or damnation. The parent says "lying would put *me* in manifest error — and I fear it for thee, my beloved"; the friend, the teacher, likewise. Third, *self-application first*: before diagnosing others' manifest errors, run the runner's conditional upon thyself — *were I to persist in this grudge, this neglect, this compromise, would I not be in manifest error?* The believer who judges himself manifestly finds humility to guide others gently. And when the diagnosis is delivered and the cure (v. 25) offered, leave results to God (v. 17): some crowds stone the physician (v. 26, tradition), yet the diagnosis stands — manifest, recorded (v. 12), and vindicated by the Cry (v. 29) and the Garden (vv. 26–27) alike.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:25

> **Truly I believe in your Lord, so listen to me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āmantu Bi-Rabbikum: Faith Declared Before Stones**

*"Truly I believe"* — *innī āmantu* — the runner's (v. 20) plea culminates in open testimony: after commanding (v. 20: "follow!"), credentialing (v. 21: unpaid, guided), reasoning (vv. 22–23: Originator, no rescuing idols), and concluding (v. 24: else manifest error), he now *confesses*. *Āmantu* (I believe) is the performative verb of conversion — the same root as the testimony (*shahādah*) by which outsiders enter Islam. He speaks it publicly, before a crowd that vowed stoning (v. 18), unarmed, alone, from the outskirts. The *innī* ("truly I") contrasts with the town's *innā* ("truly we augur ill," v. 18): their emphasis was superstition, his is submission. *"In your Lord"* (*bi-rabbikum*) — *your* Lord, not "my Lord" — is the plea's final tenderness and tactic: the God he confesses is *theirs* — their Creator, Owner, and Returner (v. 22) — whether they acknowledge Him or not. He does not import a foreign deity; he witnesses to the Abandoned One of their own *fiṭrah* (30:30). Saying "your Lord" also summons them: *He is yours — claim Him!* The confession's setting magnifies its rank: faith whispered in safety is precious; faith shouted where stones are rising is *ṣiddīqiyyah* (supreme truthfulness, v. 20) — the station tradition assigns him alongside ʿAlī and Pharaoh's believer. The tongue that testifies under threat testifies most truly, for only conviction outweighs survival.

**Fasmaʿūn: "So Listen to Me" — The Plea's Last Word**

*"So listen to me"* — *fa-ismaʿūn* (with the first-person suffix elided for urgency: *fa-ismaʿūnī*, "so hear me!") — the runner's closing imperative. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr) record two readings of its addressee, both luminous. First: it calls the *people* — "listen to me [and follow God and His messengers]" — the plea's summary: I have argued, now *hear*. *Samʿ* (hearing) in Qur'anic idiom means heeding-obedience, not mere audition — as in *"we hear and we obey"* (2:285). He begs not for ears but for compliance. Second: it calls the *messengers* — "listen to me [and bear witness for me before God]" — the convert asking the commissioned to certify his testimony in heaven's court. On this reading, the dying witness (tradition: he was stoned as he spoke, v. 26) secures heavenly documentation of his faith — the wise arranging witnesses for the Final Tribunal. Both senses cohere: to people, *heed*; to messengers, *witness*. The imperative's abruptness (*fasmaʿūn* — two syllables, no object but "me") conveys extremis: breath is short (stones may already fly), so the sentence is short. The whole plea — command, credentials, cosmology, confession — funnels into one verb: *hear*. Heaven heard, though earth stoned: the next verse (*"It was said unto him, 'Enter the Garden!'"* — v. 26) is God's eternal *samiʿnā* ("We heard") answering his *fasmaʿūn* ("hear me").

**The Structure of a Perfect Invitation: Five Verses, Five Moves**

The runner's plea (vv. 20–25) deserves study as revelation's model of popular *daʿwah* (invitation), for God preserved it verbatim as pedagogy. Move one, *solidarity* (v. 20): *"O my people!"* — belong before you bludgeon; love authorizes bluntness. Move two, *witness* (v. 21): *they ask no wage, they are guided* — establish the source's credibility on shared values before presenting disputed content. Move three, *reason* (vv. 22–23): *Originator deserves worship; idols cannot rescue* — argue from premises the hearer holds (a supreme Creator) to conclusions he resists (exclusive worship). Move four, *verdict* (v. 24): *else manifest error* — name falsehood plainly but hypothetically upon oneself, letting consciences self-apply. Move five, *confession and call* (v. 25): *I believe in your Lord, so listen* — embody the message (show the transformed life) and request decision. Command → credentials → cosmology → conclusion → confession: five moves, zero curses, zero contempt, zero compromise. Every parent counselling a child, every friend admonishing a friend, every preacher addressing skeptics, inherits the outline. Its power is proven by its fruit: though the town stoned the speaker, heaven enthroned the speech — recited in Yā Sīn across fourteen centuries, converting multitudes the runner never met. Faithful structure outlives stoning.

**Testimony Under Threat: The Jurisprudence of Courage**

The verse raises the question of open testimony in danger: must the believer confess like the runner, or may he conceal like Pharaoh's believer initially did (40:28, *"a believing man from Pharaoh's people, hiding his faith"*)? The scholars harmonize: concealment is *permitted* under compulsion (*"save one who is compelled while his heart is at peace in faith,"* 16:106 — revealed for ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir under torture), but declaration is *superior* when it may guide others — the runner's rank (*ṣiddīq*) above the concealer's safety. The Prophet ﷺ defined the hierarchy:

> *"The master of martyrs is Ḥamzah, and a man who stands before an unjust ruler, commands and forbids him, and is killed for it."* (al-Ḥākim, al-Mustadrak 4884; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Ḥākim, ḥasan by others)

The runner stood before an unjust *crowd* — the democratized tyrant — and paid the same price for the same honour. Yet courage is not recklessness: the Law permits weighing benefit (will my declaration guide any? — the runner hoped the whole city) against harm (will it destroy me pointlessly? — he judged eternity worth the stones). His calculus was heaven's: *"your Lord"* awaited his testimony, and the Garden (v. 26) awaited his soul. Ordinary believers face lesser stonings — ridicule, exclusion, career-cost — and the verse calibrates their courage: if he confessed where stones flew, shall I conceal where only eyebrows rise? The minimum is non-denial (never disown the Lord to please the crowd); the maximum is the runner's shout. Between them, each conscience, counseled by scholars, finds its post — but all face the verse's question: *dost thou believe in thy Lord? Then let somebody hear it.*

**"So Listen to Me" in the Believer's Daily Round**

The verse's two sentences become two daily practices. *"Truly I believe in your Lord"*: renew explicit testimony — in prayer (*iyyāka naʿbudu*, "Thee alone we worship," 1:5), in trial ("I believe; help my unbelief," adapting 2:260), in public (unashamed prayer, unhidden fasting, unapologetic greeting *as-salāmu ʿalaykum*). Testimony must be *heard* to function: the silent believer edifies none; the runner's faith, shouted, edifies millions centuries later. Let children hear thy *āmantu* (testify before them, pray before them, repent before them); let colleagues sense it (integrity that whispers "my Lord sees"); let the mirror hear it each morning. *"So listen to me"*: earn hearings by the runner's method — solidarity before counsel (*"O my"* — belong to those thou warnest), witness before doctrine (show the upright life), reason before verdict (argue gently), verdict before silence (name error plainly), and embodiment always (be the *āmantu* thou announcest). And when unheard — when the crowd chooses stones over listening — remember the verse's sequel: earth may refuse thy *fasmaʿūn*, but heaven answers *"Enter the Garden"* (v. 26). Speak to be heard by God; if men hear too, it is surplus mercy. The runner's last word was *listen*; God's first reply was *enter* — the eternal exchange awaiting every voice raised for truth.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:26

> **It was said unto him, "Enter the Garden!" He said, "Would that my people knew**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qīla: The Passive of Heavenly Honour**

*"It was said unto him"* — *qīla* — the divine passive, the speaker unnamed because the Speaker needs no naming: God said, or the angels and the righteous said by His command (al-Alūsī, al-Rāzī). The passive (*qīla*, "it was said") is honour's grammar — the way courts announce *"it is decreed"* and kings *"it is commanded"*: the majesty lies in the verdict, not the herald. Between verse 25's *"so listen to me"* and this verse's *"Enter the Garden"* falls the tradition's held breath: the crowd answered testimony with stones — "after his declaration to the people (v. 25), the man was stoned and killed" (al-Alūsī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarsī) — while others hold God took him alive to heaven. Either way, the narrative's swiftness is the point: no interval, no grave-cold waiting, no tribunal-delay — testimony (v. 25) touches reward (v. 26) like flint touches spark. The Qur'an elsewhere promises martyrs this immediacy:

> *"And count not those who are slain in God's way as dead. Nay, they are alive with their Lord, provided for."* (Qur'an 3:169)

The runner ran (v. 20) and never stopped: from the outskirts to the crowd, from the crowd to the Garden — one continuous *saʿy* (striving), ending where all striving aims. Earth's last word to him was stones; heaven's first is *Enter*.

**"Enter the Garden!": The Imperative of Welcome**

*"Enter the Garden!"* — *udkhuli al-jannah* — the imperative (*udkhul*) of invitation, not trial: no "wait," no "reckoning first," no "weighing" — *enter*. The Garden (*al-jannah*) here is Paradise itself in its post-martyrdom immediacy (the martyr's soul in bliss before the Resurrection's general entry, as the ḥadīth describes souls in green birds — Muslim 1887, from Ibn Masʿūd). The command's abruptness after the plea's length (vv. 20–25) is revelation's rhetoric of recompense: long striving, instant reward; extended patience, immediate welcome. Contrast the town's imperatives: *"cease"* (v. 18, backed by stones) versus God's *"enter"* (backed by eternity) — two authorities, two verbs, two destinies. The runner obeyed neither the town's "cease" nor self-preservation's "flee"; he earned heaven's "enter." Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's consolation for every persecuted inviter: thy vindication is not scheduled for the Last Day alone — it begins at thy last breath. The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the martyr's instant honour:

> *"The martyr has six things with God: he is forgiven with the first flow of blood, shown his place in the Garden, protected from the punishment of the grave, secured from the Greatest Terror, crowned with the crown of dignity, and married to seventy-two of the wide-eyed maidens."* (al-Tirmidhī 1663; Ibn Mājah 2799, from al-Miqdām ibn Maʿdīkarib; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

Stones opened the runner's veins; the Garden opened its gates. Exchange complete; profit infinite.

**"Would That My People Knew": Love Beyond the Grave**

*"He said, 'Would that my people knew'"* — *yā layta qawmī yaʿlamūn* — *yā layta* ("O would that!") the Arabic particle of wistful longing: the martyr, *inside* bliss, yearns backward toward his murderers. *"My people"* (*qawmī*) — still *his*, still beloved, though they stoned him mid-testimony (tradition) — and *"knew"* (*yaʿlamūn*) — knew what verse 27 will specify (his forgiveness, his honour). The psychology is the verse's miracle: no vindication ("would that they knew *how wrong they were*"), no vengeance ("would that they suffered as I did"), no relief ("glad to be rid of them") — only evangelistic longing: *would that they knew, so they might come here too*. Qatādah (d. 117/726), among the foremost early commentators, preserved the tradition's most piercing detail: "the people began to stone him while he was praying for God to guide them, and as they continued to stone him, he continued to pray for them, even while suffering a gruesome death at their hands" (Ibn Kathīr). Stoned, he prayed; dying, he guided; arrived, he longed. This is *naṣīḥah* (sincere counsel) perfected — love that death cannot kill and Paradise cannot distract. The Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ, his heir in this station, prayed for Ṭāʾif's stoners (see v. 17) and for Uḥud's wounders:

> *"O God, forgive my people, for they know not."* (al-Bukhārī 3477; Muslim 1792, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd — the Prophet's prayer recounting a prophet beaten by his people)

The runner's *yā layta* and the Prophet's *ighfir li-qawmī* are one love in two mouths.

**The Martyr's Longing as Theology of Daʿwah**

The verse encodes the inviter's theology entire. First, *guidance is the goal, not victory*: the runner does not wish his people knew *he was right* but that they knew *the truth that would save them* — vindication serves salvation, never ego. Second, *love outlasts rejection*: "my people" survives stoning — the inviter who ceases to love the rejecters ceases to invite them, and becomes their judge instead. Third, *death does not end concern*: the martyr's first thought in Paradise is missionary — bliss is incomplete while the beloved are outside. (So too, tradition holds, the Prophet ﷺ will intercede while others plead for themselves.) Fourth, *knowledge is the assumed cure*: *"would that they knew"* — he presumes that *knowing* (his forgiveness, his honour, the reality behind the messengers' words) would convert them — ignorance, not wickedness, is his diagnosis of their crime. This presumption of ignorance excuses without excusing: it motivates prayer ("forgive them, for they know not") while preserving accountability (they *refused* to know — vv. 15, 18). Al-Rāzī concludes: the perfect inviter combines the runner's three postures — *running* to warn (v. 20), *praying* while wounded (Qatādah's report), *longing* in bliss (this verse). Warn on thy feet, forgive on thy knees, long from thy rest: this is *daʿwah* without remainder.

**Dying, Longing, and Entering in Daily Miniature**

The believer lives this verse in three daily rehearsals. *Enter*: treat every prayer's end, every fast's breaking, every night's sleep as a miniature *udkhul* — God inviting thee into micro-paradises (acceptance, sweetness, rest) as foretastes of the Garden. Prepare for the final *Enter* by answering the daily ones: who enters prayer reluctantly should fear entering Paradise reluctantly — practice welcome. *Would that they knew*: cultivate the martyr's backward longing while still among the living — when blessed with guidance, a verse's sweetness, a prayer's tears, immediately think *yā layta qawmī yaʿlamūn* — "would that my family, my friend, my colleague knew this" — and then *tell* them: forward the verse, share the lesson, invite to the prayer. Bliss hoarded is bliss halved; the runner's joy was complete only when shared, even posthumously. *My people, still*: refuse to disown — the wayward child, the mocking relative, the hostile coworker remain *qawmī* (my people) no matter their stones. Pray for them while stoned (Qatādah's image: bleeding lips moving in supplication), speak of them tenderly in absence, and hope for their *yaʿlamūn* (their knowing) beyond thy dying. For the verse promises the inviter's double immortality: *entering* (thy bliss secured) and *longing* (thy love remembered) — the Garden for thy soul, and Yā Sīn for thy story, recited over the dying (see Introduction) until the Cry ends recitation. Run, testify, forgive, enter — and long, forever, that thy people knew.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:27

> **How my Lord forgave me and placed me among the honored."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bimā Ghafara Lī: "For What My Lord Forgave Me"**

*"How my Lord forgave me"* — *bimā ghafara lī rabbī* — *bimā* carrying the double sense the commentators (al-Ṭabarsī) preserve: *"how/for what"* — both the *manner* (how entirely, how generously He forgave) and the *cause* (for what deed — his faith, his running, his testimony — He forgave; or for what sins — now covered — He forgave). *Ghafara* (to forgive) is to cover, shield, helmet (*mighfar*): God's forgiveness covers sins the way a helmet covers the head — protection, not mere overlooking. *"My Lord"* (*rabbī*) — the martyr's possessive: the Lord he confessed as *"your Lord"* before the crowd (v. 25) is now, in bliss, intimately *his* — confession has ripened into communion. The verse's placement as the content of *"would that my people knew"* (v. 26) means the martyr's evangelistic longing centers on *forgiveness*: would that they knew *sinners are forgiven* — that the door they imagine barred stands open, that the God they imagine only wrathful is first forgiving. His own biography proves it: an outskirts-dweller, late-comer to the messengers, speaker of one plea — forgiven entirely. If *he* was forgiven, who among them could despair? The martyr preaches from Paradise the sermon the town most needed on earth: *forgiveness is real, and it is for the likes of you*.

**Wa Jaʿalanī Mina al-Mukramīn: Honour After Pardon**

*"And placed me among the honored"* — *wa jaʿalanī mina al-mukramīn* — pardon blossoms into honour: God not only *covered* his sins but *crowned* his person — *"among the honored"* (*mina al-mukramīn*), the company of prophets, martyrs, and righteous in Paradise's noblest ranks. The sequence (forgiveness → honour) is salvation's complete grammar: *maghfirah* removes the debt (Hell averted), *ikrām* bestows the wealth (Paradise granted, rank elevated) — precisely the two tidings promised to the Reminder-followers in verse 11 (*"forgiveness and a generous reward"*). The runner is verse 11's parable embodied: he followed the Reminder (the messengers' message), feared the Compassionate unseen (testifying where only God would vindicate), and received both wages. *"Among"* (*min*) repeats the preposition of prophetic company (v. 3: *"among the message bearers"*): the carpenter from the outskirts now keeps eternal company with the messengers he defended for an afternoon. One plea's fellowship on earth has become endless fellowship in heaven. Al-Rāzī notes the consolation for obscure believers: honour with God requires no earthly rank — the town's king (tradition: the messengers' persecutor) lies in the Cry's dust (v. 29), while the unnamed runner (*"a man,"* v. 20) sits among the honoured. Heaven's peerage inverts earth's: the stoned are throned.

**The Martyr's Two Wishes: What He Wants Them to Know**

The martyr wishes his people knew *two* things — forgiveness and honour — and the pair diagnoses the town's (and every sinner's) double despair. Despair one: *"my sins are too great to be forgiven"* — answered by *ghafara lī* (He forgave *me* — the late, lowly, little-known). Despair two: *"even if forgiven, I am too stained for honour"* — answered by *mina al-mukramīn* (He *honoured* me — seated me with the noble). Together they proclaim grace superabundant: God does not merely *tolerate* the repentant (bare admission) but *celebrates* them (honoured rank). The Qur'an states the policy universally:

> *"Save those who repent, believe, and do righteous deeds — for them God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds."* (Qur'an 25:70)

— replacement, not mere removal: sins transmuted into honours. The Prophet ﷺ illustrated the policy's mathematics:

> *"By the One in Whose Hand is my soul, were you not to commit sins, God would do away with you and bring a people who commit sins and then ask God's forgiveness, and He would forgive them."* (Muslim 2749, from Abū Hurayrah)

The martyr's posthumous sermon is thus the Gospel of repentance in miniature: *I sinned, I ran, I testified, I was stoned — and He forgave me and honoured me. Would that you knew — would that you came.* Every despairing soul reading Yā Sīn over a grave (see Introduction) hears the dead runner preaching to the living despairing: *thy sins are not greater than His covering, nor thy lowliness lower than His honouring*.

**Qatādah's Image: Praying While Being Stoned**

Qatādah's (d. 117/726) preserved detail — "the people began to stone him while he was praying for God to guide them, and as they continued to stone him, he continued to pray for them, even while suffering a gruesome death at their hands" (Ibn Kathīr) — deserves lingering, for it is this verse's earthly mirror. Forgiven-and-honoured in heaven (v. 27), he was forgiving-and-honouring on earth: forgiving (praying for murderers mid-murder) and honouring (treating stoners as *qawmī*, "my people," v. 20, worthy of guidance). The symmetry reveals heaven's law: *as thou treatest, thou art treated* — the forgiving are forgiven, the honouring honoured. The Prophet ﷺ stated it:

> *"The merciful are shown mercy by the Most Merciful. Be merciful to those on earth, and the One above the heaven will have mercy upon you."* (Abū Dāwūd 4941; al-Tirmidhī 1924, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And its inverse warns: the vengeful are left to vengeance, the contemptuous to contempt. The runner's gruesome death — prolonged, public, agonizing — also answers the question of suffering's meaning: his pain purchased praying (each stone prompted a supplication), his praying purchased Paradise, his Paradise purchases this verse's eternal preaching. No pain is wasted that is prayed through. The believer in lesser agonies — illness, slander, grief, failure — inherits the method: *while* they stone (criticize, exclude, afflict), *pray* — for them, for guidance, for forgiveness. Stones become supplications, supplications become honours, honours become *mina al-mukramīn*.

**Preaching Forgiveness and Honour From Our Own Gardens**

The verse assigns the living three ministries drawn from the martyr's two nouns. *Ministry of forgiveness-proclamation*: tell despairing souls — the addict, the adulterer, the apostate-returning, the lifelong neglectful — *ghafara lī* in thy own voice: "He forgave *me*, and I was [name thy outskirts]; He will forgive thee." Testimony evangelizes where theology argues: thy forgiven past is thy credential. Never let a sinner despair while this verse stands: *"Say, 'O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of God's mercy. Truly God forgives all sins.'"* (39:53). *Ministry of honour-restoration*: treat the repentant as *mukramīn* (honoured), not as suspects on probation — seat them in the front, marry them honourably, employ them trustingly, bury them prayerfully. The community that forgives but dishonours (perpetual stigma) has learned only half the verse. *Ministry of posthumous longing*: like the martyr, invest in guidance that outlives thee — teach a child a verse, fund an ongoing charity (*ṣadaqah jāriyah*, v. 12), write counsel that will preach after thy burial. For the runner's *yā layta qawmī yaʿlamūn* (v. 26) still converts: every reciter of Yā Sīn is his *qawm* (people), every reading his knowing. And for the dying over whom Yā Sīn is recited (see Introduction): this verse is the lullaby of transition — *forgiven* (fear not thy sins) *and honoured* (hope for thy rank). Whisper it at deathbeds; believe it at thy own. The runner ran, testified, bled, prayed — and woke *mukram* (honoured). The path is open; the Company awaits; *would that we all knew* — and knowing, ran.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:28

> **And after him We did not send down a host from Heaven against his people; nor would We send down.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa Mā Anzalnā: No Army Descended — The Insult of Ease**

*"And after him We did not send down"* — *wa mā anzalnā min baʿdihi* — *"after him"* (*min baʿdihi*): after the runner-martyr (vv. 20–27), after his testimony, his stoning, his entry into bliss. *"A host from Heaven against his people"* — *ʿalā qawmihi min jundin mina al-samāʾ* — *jund* (host, army, troop): God sent no angelic legion, no heavenly battalion, against the town. The denial (*mā anzalnā*, "We did not send down") is not apology but *contempt*: the town was too insignificant to warrant an army. Empires merit hosts — Pharaoh drowned by sea-commanded, Quraysh met by angels at Badr — but this town, for all its stones and threats (v. 18), rated no expedition. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī take the verse as introducing the punishment: God "wiped them out with a single cry (v. 29) rather than sending angels against them" — extermination by *sound*, not siege. The rhetoric humbles all persecuting powers: the mob that felt mighty stoning one runner (v. 26, tradition) was, to heaven, a gnat-swarm unworthy of swatting by soldiers — a single shout sufficed. Tyranny's self-image (legions, threats, "painful punishment from us," v. 18) meets reality's verdict: *no host was even dispatched*.

**"Nor Would We Send Down": The Policy of Proportion**

*"Nor would We send down"* — *wa mā kunnā munzilīn* — the second denial generalizes the first into policy: "nor are We such as to send down" (hosts for such purpose). The construction (*mā kunnā* + participle) denotes uncharacteristic action — *it is not Our way*. God *could* dispatch legions (He possesses *"hosts of the heavens and the earth,"* 48:4, 7); He *declines* to waste them on what a Cry accomplishes. The alternative rendering ("nor what We used to send down," al-Kashshāf, al-Ṭabarsī) shades the meaning toward habit: sending hosts is not Our custom in such cases. Either way, the verse states divine *economy*: omnipotence does not perform; it proportions. Men display power to prove it (parades, threats, v. 18's bluster); God conceals power within ease — the single Cry (v. 29) that no army could survive. Al-Rāzī draws the awe: the town's annihilation required less divine effort than the town's warning required divine patience — centuries of messengers (by the parable's logic), three envoys (v. 14), one runner (v. 20), answered finally by one sound. Patience is vast; punishment is brief. The Qur'an's refrain stands behind the verse:

> *"Thy Lord is not unjust to His servants."* (Qur'an 41:46; cf. 3:182; 8:51)

— and, we may add, never excessive: judgment fits the crime's weight, never God's power's.

**The Second Reading: No Revelation After the Rejected One**

Other commentators (al-Ṭabarsī, al-Ṭabarī) read *"host from Heaven"* (*jund mina al-samāʾ*) as the angels of revelation — meaning God "did not send another revelation after having sent the message bearers": the town received its embassy (vv. 14–17) and its popular witness (vv. 20–25); no second edition will follow. On this reading, the verse warns by *closure*: revelation is an opportunity, not a subscription — towns (and souls) that stone their warners receive no replacements. The source commentary judges this reading "more consistent with this story in its function as a warning to the Quraysh, for it implies that accepting the revelation sent to the Prophet Muḥammad is their only opportunity and they will not receive another." Makkah, hearing the parable, was meant to feel the narrowing: *your* messengers stand before you now (Muḥammad ﷺ); no prophet follows him (33:40, the Seal); no Qur'an succeeds this Qur'an — *"after him"* (the runner; and by parallel, after this Prophet) no host descends but judgment's. The two readings (no army / no further revelation) converge in urgency: heaven's resources for this town are spent. What remains is the Cry.

**Divine Restraint as the Parable's Quiet Theme**

Read in sequence, the verse crowns a parable-long display of divine restraint worth gathering. God sent *two*, then *three* (v. 14) — reinforcement, not retaliation. The messengers answered denial with reaffirmation (v. 16), not imprecation. They disclaimed coercion (v. 17). The runner pleaded with love (vv. 20–25), prayed while stoned (Qatādah, v. 26), and longed for his murderers in Paradise (v. 26). God honoured the martyr instantly (vv. 26–27) — yet still no host descended. Restraint upon restraint, mercy upon mercy — and *then*, only then, the Cry (v. 29). The verse thus forbids the caricature of a hasty, wrathful deity: heaven's armies stood ready through beating, imprisonment, threats, and murder — and stood *down*. When judgment finally comes, it comes as *under*-reaction (one Cry where legions were deserved), not over-reaction. The Qur'an states the character explicitly:

> *"And thy Lord is the Forgiving, Possessed of Mercy. Were He to take them for that which they earn, He would hasten the punishment for them."* (Qur'an 18:58)

He does not hasten. The town's timeline proves it; Makkah's reprieve (years of warning before Badr, and mercy even after) confirms it; every sinner's continued breath re-proves it daily. *"Nor would We send down"* is wrath's leash — long, but not endless. The Cry ends the verse's sentence as surely as it ended the town.

**Living Under Restraint Before the Cry**

The verse disciplines both the persecuted and the persecuting, the despairing and the complacent. *For the persecuted*: God sends no host *yet* — and His delay is not His absence. The runner died without angelic rescue; his vindication was Paradise (v. 26), not prevention. Expect heaven's timetable, not thine: Badr's angels (8:9) came after years of boycott; the town's Cry came after the martyr's blood. Thy task is testimony (v. 25), not summoning legions — heaven dispatches what it wills, when it wills. *For the persecuting and complacent*: no host *yet* means mercy, not immunity. The town mistook restraint for impotence ("where are their gods? where is their army?") — until the Cry corrected the theology in one syllable. Every morning without judgment is *"nor would We send down... yet"* — respite for repentance (cf. v. 44: *"enjoyment for a while"*), not evidence of safety. *For inviters*: imitate heaven's proportion — warn repeatedly, reinforce patiently (v. 14's third messenger), plead lovingly (vv. 20–25), and leave force to God. The caller who would summon hosts (curses, violence, coercion) against rejecters has not learned this verse: God Himself declined. Proclaim (v. 17), love (v. 20), forgive (v. 26, Qatādah), and trust the Cry to God's wisdom. And for all: hear the verse's narrowing — *after him*, no host but judgment. After *this* Prophet (the Seal), no revelation but this Book. After *this* reading of Yā Sīn, no excuse but heedlessness. The hosts stand down; the Cry stands ready; the runner stands honoured — choose whose standing is thine.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:29

> **It was but a single cry. Then, behold, they were extinguished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**In Kānat Illā Ṣayḥatan Wāḥidah: Destruction in One Syllable**

*"It was but a single cry"* — *in kānat illā ṣayḥatan wāḥidah* — *in... illā* ("nothing but"): the town's annihilation — its palaces, mobs, priests, stones, threats (v. 18), and king — totaled *one* Cry. *Ṣayḥah* is a piercing shout, a scream, a blast — the same word for Thamūd's destruction (*"the Cry seized them,"* 11:67) and for the Last Day's Trumpet-Cry (vv. 49, 53). *Wāḥidah* ("single, one") stresses that no second was needed: heaven did not reload. The verse's brevity *performs* its content: eleven Arabic words for an essay's annihilation — the sentence ends almost before it begins, the way the town did. Ibn Kathīr: "the destruction for having disbelieved was instant." Consider the disproportion the parable has built: three messengers (v. 14), double declarations (vv. 14, 16), one runner's five-verse plea (vv. 20–25), a martyrdom, a Garden-entry, a posthumous sermon (vv. 26–27) — pages of mercy — answered by the town with threats (v. 18) and stones — and settled by God in *one sound*. The Qur'an's physics of punishment is consistent: Pharaoh's army, drowned in moments; ʿĀd, wind-scattered; Lot's people, turned and stoned from heaven; Quraysh's chiefs, felled at Badr in a morning. Omnipotence does not labour; it *utters*. The God whose creation-word is *"Be!" and it is* (v. 82) needs no longer word for un-creation.

**Fa-Idhā Hum Khāmidūn: "Behold, They Were Extinguished"**

*"Then, behold, they were extinguished"* — *fa-idhā hum khāmidūn* — *fa-idhā* ("then, behold!") the particle of sudden disclosure: turn, and see. *Khāmidūn* (extinguished) is the parable's master-metaphor: from *khamada* (a fire dying out, embers cooling to ash). The town is a *fire gone out* — yesterday's blaze (markets roaring, furnaces glowing, threats crackling) today's cold ash. The image works on every level. *Physically*: life is heat; death is cooling — the Cry stopped hearts the way water stops flame. *Socially*: the mob's fever (threats, stones, mockery) broke instantly — no rally, no revenge, no succession-crisis; extinction, not defeat. *Spiritually*: they had extinguished guidance's light within (vv. 8–9: veiled, walled) — now their own light is extinguished without. The Qur'an uses the root for Hell's inmates' despair (*"whenever it abates, We increase for them a blaze,"* 17:97 — the fire that never fully *khamada* for them), making the town's earthly extinction a foretaste of un-extinguished Fire. And the beholding (*idhā*) summons witnesses: *come, see* — the market silent, the stones unthrown, the threateners breathless. Archaeology's tells (mounds of dead cities) are this verse's footnotes: travellers still *behold* the *khāmidūn* — Persepolis, Petra, Pompeii — fires out, ash remaining. Every ruin whispers *ṣayḥah wāḥidah*.

**The Cry That Echoes Through the Sūrah**

This Cry (v. 29) is the first of three *ṣayḥah*/trumpet blasts structuring Yā Sīn's eschatology, and the commentators link them deliberately. Cry one (here): the town's historical destruction — a local Last Day, proving God *can* extinguish instantly. Cry two (v. 49): *"a single cry that will seize them while they dispute"* — the Hour's first blast, ending *this* world as the town's Cry ended *that* town. Cry three (vv. 51, 53): the Resurrection's blast(s), raising the dead — extinction reversed by the same vocal omnipotence. The three form a chiasm of divine speech: Cry destroys the deniers (v. 29) → Cry destroys the world (v. 49) → Cry re-creates the world (vv. 51–53) → Word creates all (*"Be! and it is,"* v. 82). The parable's Cry is thus *evidence* for the eschatological Cries: whoever doubts God can end the world in "a single cry" (v. 53, *"as the blinking of an eye, or nearer,"* 16:77) is shown a town ended in one. History is Last-Day-in-miniature; the town's ash is the world's preview. Makkah's scoffers ("when will this promise come?" — v. 48) are answered by precedent: *it came to a town like yours, in less time than your question took to ask*. The Cry that was proves the Cry that will be.

**Justice, Proportion, and the Instantaneity of Sentence**

The verse's instantaneity raises the theodicy question — was sudden extinction *just*? The parable has answered cumulatively, and the answer must be gathered. *Warning*: two messengers, then three (v. 14); double declaration (vv. 14, 16); a townsman's five-verse plea (vv. 20–25). *Time*: beating and prison endured (tradition), threats threatened (v. 18) yet unexecuted by heaven, a martyrdom mourned in Paradise (vv. 26–27) while the town still breathed. *Restraint*: no host sent (v. 28). *Proportionality*: one Cry — not legions, not prolonged siege, not torture — extinction, not torment (torment awaits the Hereafter, vv. 63–64). *Discrimination*: the messengers and the martyr were not in the ash — heaven extracts its own before extinguishing (as Lot's family before the overturning, 15:65). Every condition of just judgment met; every mitigation exhausted. The instantaneity is therefore not rashness but *readiness*: the sentence was long-due (cf. v. 7's *ḥaqqa al-qawl*, "the Word has come due"), long-warned, and executed at the appointed breath. Suddenness punishes only the unprepared-by-choice: the prepared (messengers, martyr) met the Cry already in God's care. For modern readers uncomfortable with swift judgment, the verse asks the mirror-question: *how many Cries hast thou been warned of, and how prepared art thou for one?* The town had three warners; thou hast this sūrah, recited over graves (see Introduction) precisely to prepare the living.

**Living Within Earshot of the Cry**

The verse is Yā Sīn's memento mori in miniature, and its practices are urgent. *First, settle accounts swiftly*: the Cry allows no bequest, no return (v. 50) — so bequeath now (write the will, clear the debts, seek the forgiveness, mend the tie). The Prophet ﷺ warned:

> *"It is not right for a Muslim who has something to bequeath to spend two nights without having it written with him."* (al-Bukhārī 2738; Muslim 1627, from Ibn ʿUmar)

— two nights, for the Cry may come on the third. *Second, extinguish thy fires before they are extinguished*: the rage-fire, the greed-fire, the lust-fire, the grudge-fire — each *khumūd* (dying-out) thou choosest now (forgiveness, charity, fasting, prayer) spares thee compelled *khumūd* then. *Third, keep embers of good alive*: the runner's embers (testimony, prayer, love) were carried into the Garden (vv. 26–27) while the town's fires went out — invest in un-extinguishable fuel (faith, righteous deeds, ongoing charity, v. 12). *Fourth, visit the extinguished*: walk ruins, graveyards, and hospitals with *fa-idhā hum khāmidūn* on thy lips — beholding extinction cures the delusion of permanence (cf. v. 31: *"how many generations... We destroyed"*). *Fifth, warn of the Cry with the runner's love*: tell the heedless — gently, urgently — that towns end in a sound, worlds in a blast (v. 49), and every soul in a final breath as sudden as *ṣayḥah wāḥidah*. For the verse's last mercy is its address: *thou* readest it *before* thy Cry — the town heard threats (v. 18) and chose stones; thou hearest this verse and may choose running (v. 20). One Cry ended them; one verse may save thee. *Behold* — and believe.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:30

> **Alas for the servants! Never did a messenger come unto them, but that they mocked him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Yā Ḥasratan: The Divine Lament Over History**

*"Alas for the servants!"* — *yā ḥasratan ʿalā al-ʿibād* — *ḥasrah* (regret, grief, lamentation) with the vocative *yā* (O): "O regret/grief upon the servants!" The speaker is God, lamenting — the Qur'an's most tender anthropopathic expression: the Almighty voicing sorrow over creatures who destroy themselves. The majority (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī) read it as amazement at "the extreme injustice disbelievers do to themselves and the punishment they will suffer" — lament *for* their fate, not divine repentance (God does not regret; He *reveals* the regret they will feel, voicing it in advance). Others (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) take *"servants"* (*ʿibād*) as the prophets — God's lament *for* His messengers' sufferings at mockers' hands. Both senses bleed into one: heaven grieves the mockers' ruin *and* the mocked's wounds. The parallel passage confirms the universality:

> *"And never did a messenger come unto them but that they mocked him."* (Qur'an 15:11)

— *never* (*mā... illā*, "not... but"): mockery is disbelief's constant uniform across all peoples, all ages. The lament closes the parable (vv. 13–29) by widening it: the town was not anomaly but instance — *every* messenger's people mocked, *every* mocking people faces the Cry. *Yā ḥasratan* is history's epitaph and Makkah's prognosis in three words.

**Istihzāʾ: Mockery as Disbelief's First Weapon**

*"But that they mocked him"* — *illā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* — *istihzāʾ* (mockery, ridicule, derision): the town called messengers "but human... liars" (v. 15), augured ill (v. 18), threatened stones (v. 18) — and beneath all ran mockery's current. The Qur'an catalogues mockery as every prophet's reception: Noah — *"whenever chiefs of his people passed by him, they mocked him"* (11:38); Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, Shuʿayb — each derided; Moses — Pharaoh's *"am I not better than this despicable one?"* (43:52); Jesus — plotted against; Muḥammad ﷺ — poet, sorcerer, madman (15:6; 37:36; 68:51). Why mockery *first*? Because it is cheapest: argument requires thought, violence requires resolve, but a sneer requires nothing — and achieves dismissal without engagement. Mockery is also *social*: it recruits the crowd (laughter bonds mockers), isolates the victim (none stands with the laughed-at), and inoculates against conviction (who follows a joke?). Al-Rāzī notes mockery's boomerang structure in revelation: mockers mock warners of Hell, then Hell's keepers mock the mockers — *"Taste! Truly thou art the mighty, the honourable!"* (44:49) — derision returned with divine irony. The verse warns every mocker: today's sneer at truth is tomorrow's sentence *by* Truth. And it consoles every mocked believer: thy reception *matches* the messengers' — mockery is not refutation but *certification* of prophetic company.

**"The Servants": Who Is Lamented, Who Laments**

The lament's object — *"the servants"* (*al-ʿibād*) — is deliberately universal: not "the town," not "Quraysh," but *servants* — all who serve (or refuse to serve) the Master. The universality performs three functions. First, it *includes Makkah*: Quraysh, mocking Muḥammad ﷺ as the town mocked its three, stands inside *al-ʿibād* — the lament hovers over Makkah's streets as it hovered over Antioch's. Second, it *includes every later mocker*: each generation's mockers (of prophets, scriptures, believers, modesty, prayer) join the lamented company — the verse is never past-tense. Third, paradoxically, it *honours even the lamented*: they are still called *"servants"* (*ʿibād*) — God's, by creation if not obedience. He who might have called them "criminals" (*mujrimūn*, as v. 59 will, at judgment) calls them "servants" while lamenting — possession persists through rebellion. The lament's *subject* (God) reveals mercy's depth: omnipotence needs nothing from mockers, yet grieves their self-ruin. The Qur'an voices the grief elsewhere as the Prophet's ﷺ own anguish:

> *"So let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them."* (Qur'an 35:8)

— the Prophet ﷺ *was* expending his soul in *ḥasarāt* (regrets) over mockers; God here shows He shares the sorrow yet forbids its excess. Heaven grieves, but heaven also *judges*: lament (v. 30) precedes the destroyed-generations verse (v. 31) — grief voiced, then sentence displayed. Mercy laments; justice proceeds.

**From Parable to Principle: The Sūrah's Hinge Verse**

Verse 30 is the parable-section's keystone and the sūrah's hinge: it seals vv. 13–29 (the town) and launches vv. 31–44 (destroyed generations, cosmic signs). The hinge turns on generalization: *from* "this town mocked" *to* "all towns mock" — parable becomes law. The law then powers everything after: because *all* mock, *all* need the destroyed-generations warning (v. 31); because mockery persists, signs must be displayed (vv. 33–44); because mockers demand timing (v. 48), Cries must be described (vv. 49–53). The verse also answers the parable's lingering question — *why did the town mock?* — with history's shrug: *that is what servants do to messengers*. The answer is not explanation but *normalization*: mockery is disbelief's nature, not its argument. Recognizing the nature disarms it: the inviter who expects mockery (as *sunnah* of history) is not shattered by it; the hearer who recognizes his own mockery (as the town's echo) may repent of it. Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's comfort for Muḥammad ﷺ specifically: *thy* mockers are not novel, *thy* wounds not unprecedented, *thy* vindication not uncertain — every mocked messenger was vindicated (the town's Cry, v. 29; Noah's flood; Moses' sea; and soon, Badr). *Yā ḥasratan* grieves with thee, then points to verse 31: *see what became of mockers before*.

**Refusing Mockery, Enduring Mockery, Grieving Rightly**

Three disciplines flow from the lament. *First, never mock*: neither prophets (nor their heirs), neither scriptures (nor their rulings), neither believers (nor their piety). Mockery of beards, veils, prayers, or "fundamentalists" joins *yastahziʾūn* — the verse's verb — regardless of century. The Prophet ﷺ warned:

> *"It is enough evil for a man to despise his Muslim brother."* (Muslim 2564, from Abū Hurayrah)

— despising is mockery's seed; mockery, its fruit; the Cry, its harvest. When tempted to sneer at truth or its bearers, hear *yā ḥasratan* pronounced over thyself — and repent. *Second, endure mockery prophetically*: the mocked believer (for prayer at work, for honesty in cheating cultures, for modesty in exposure cultures) stands where messengers stood — the sneers *certify* his company. Respond as they did: reaffirm (v. 16), disclaim coercion (v. 17), love the mockers (*"O my people,"* v. 20), pray while stoned (Qatādah, v. 26), and leave vindication to God (v. 29). Never mock back — the runner uttered zero sneers across six verses. *Third, grieve rightly*: feel *ḥasrah* (sorrow) over the mocking — their self-ruin, their coming Cry — but not soul-expending despair (35:8). Grieve as God grieves: lamenting *and* warning, sorrowing *and* sending (signs, verses, runners). Pray for mockers (*"forgive my people, for they know not"*) while preparing for their Cry. For the verse's final mercy is its timing: *yā ḥasratan* is pronounced *before* verses 31–83 — lament while warning continues, grief while guidance lasts. The mockers are lamented, not yet extinguished; the served (messengers) are mocked, not yet vindicated. Both verbs await their completion — mockery in the Cry (vv. 29, 49), lament in the Garden's *"Peace!"* (v. 58). Choose, while choice lasts, which sentence shall complete thy verse.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:31

> **Have they not considered how many generations before them We destroyed, such that they return not unto them?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A-Lam Yaraw: The Question of Consideration**

*"Have they not considered"* — *a-lam yaraw* — *raʾā* here meaning to consider, reflect, take lesson — not mere seeing but *seeing-with-understanding*. The interrogative *a-* (*have they not...?*) is rebuke's grammar: the evidence lies openly before them, and their failure to consider it is culpable. The subject (*they*) embraces, as the commentators note, the former peoples, the Makkans, or both: the destroyed cannot return to warn, and the living refuse to learn — a double silence heaven breaks with this question. *"How many generations"* (*kam ahlaknā mina al-qurūn*) — *kam* (how many!) exclamatory multitude: ʿĀd, Thamūd, Noah's people, Lot's, Midian, Pharaoh's host — an innumerable catalogue the Qur'an details across a dozen sūrahs. *"We destroyed"* (*ahlaknā*) — divine agency undisguised: civilizations do not merely "fall" (passive, academic); they are *destroyed* (active, judicial) — by famine, flood, wind, Cry, quake, or the slow rot of God-withdrawn blessing. The question's logic is empirical: *count the ruins*. Arabia itself was ringed with them — Thamūd's carved houses at al-Ḥijr on the Syrian trade route Quraysh travelled annually, ʿĀd's empty south, Sodom's dead sea to the northwest. The Makkans *passed* destruction twice yearly in caravan and never *considered* it. Revelation demands the caravans become classrooms.

**"Such That They Return Not": The One-Way Door of Destruction**

*"Such that they return not unto them"* — *annahum ilayhim lā yarjiʿūn* — the destroyed do not come back: no return to warn the living ("we were wrong; do not follow us"), no return to resume life, no return until the Resurrection's general return (vv. 51–53). The finality serves two warnings. First, *no posthumous counsel*: the living cannot await the dead's advice — guidance must be taken from the living warners (messengers, vv. 14–17; runners, vv. 20–25; this Qur'an) before joining the silent. The rich man of the parable pleads from Hell, *"send Lazarus to my brothers"* — and is refused (the Qur'an's version: *"behind them is a barrier until the Day they are raised,"* 23:100). Second, *no second chance*: destruction's door locks from inside — the town of vv. 13–29 will never reconstitute to apologize; Pharaoh's *"I believe"* at drowning was rejected (*"Now? When thou hast disobeyed before?"* — 10:91). The clause thus answers procrastination's whisper ("I will repent later, consider later, believe later"): the destroyed said *later* until there was no later. Al-Rāzī notes the verse's quiet terror: every reader stands on the *before* side of an identical sentence — *before* destruction (personal death, the Cry, v. 49) — with the destroyed on the *after* side, mute. The *before* is brief; the *after* is eternal. *Consider* — now.

**Generations (Qurūn) as the Qur'an's Historical Laboratory**

*Qurūn* (generations, peoples, centuries) is the Qur'an's unit of historical experiment: each *qarn* receives warners, chooses response, and reaps result — a controlled trial of truth versus rebellion run dozens of times with invariant outcome: believers saved (often few — Noah's handful, Lot's household-minus-one), deniers destroyed (often many). The Qur'an presents the dataset explicitly:

> *"And how many a generation before them did We destroy who were mightier in power than them! They traversed the lands — is there any escape?"* (Qur'an 50:36)

> *"Have they not journeyed in the land and seen how was the end of those before them? They were mightier than them in power."* (Qur'an 30:9; cf. 40:21, 82; 47:10)

The refrain's point is anti-exceptionalism: *you are not the exception*. Every destroyed *qarn* believed itself exceptional — ʿĀd in strength (*"who is mightier than us in power?"* — 41:15), Thamūd in craft (carved mountains), Pharaoh in divinity ("I am your lord most high," 79:24), Quraysh in centrality (custodians of the Kaʿbah). Might, craft, sanctity, centrality — none exempted. Modernity's exceptionalism ("but we have science/democracy/technology") is the same claim in new costume, and the verse strips it: *qarn* after *qarn*, the costumes changed, the Cry remained. History is not progress toward immunity but repetition toward verdict — until the final *qarn* faces the final Cry (v. 49). The wise read ruins as mirrors; only fools read them as anomalies.

**Why the Destroyed Cannot Return — And Why the Living Must Go to Them**

The verse's logic inverts the expected direction: the dead cannot return to the living (*lā yarjiʿūn*), so the living must *go* to the dead — journey, consider, learn. The Qur'an commands the journey repeatedly: *"Have they not journeyed in the land...?"* (30:9; 40:21; 47:10) — *sīrū fī al-arḍ* ("journey in the earth") — archaeology as worship, tourism as theology. The Prophet ﷺ modeled it at al-Ḥijr (Thamūd's ruins): he veiled his face, quickened his mount, forbade using its water, and said:

> *"Do not enter upon these punished ones except weeping; if you cannot weep, do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 433; Muslim 2980, from Ibn ʿUmar)

Ruins are not photo-opportunities but *mawāʿiẓ* (admonitions): enter weeping, or not at all. The verse also answers the demand for resurrected witnesses ("bring back our fathers to testify," as Quraysh demanded, 44:36): the destroyed return not — by divine law (23:100, the *barzakh*-barrier) — so testimony must come through revelation's preserved record (this Qur'an narrating their ends) and reason's honest inference (ruins + warners-rejected = judgment). To demand the dead's return while rejecting the living Book is to demand signs while refusing sight (vv. 8–9). God gives instead the superior witness: a Book that cannot die, narrating peoples that did.

**Considering Ruins in an Age That Ignores Them**

The verse prescribes *iʿtibār* (taking lesson) as a spiritual discipline with concrete forms. *First, read destruction-narratives regularly*: the Qur'an's *qaṣaṣ* (Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, Shuʿayb, Moses — sūrahs 7, 11, 26) are this verse's footnotes — study them as case-files, each ending *"so see how was the end."* *Second, visit silence*: graveyards weekly (the Prophet ﷺ: *"Visit graves, for they remind of the Hereafter"* — Muslim 976, from Abū Hurayrah), hospitals, ruins, museums of dead empires — each a *qarn* that returns not. Stand before them with *a-lam yaraw* on thy lips: *have I considered?* *Third, audit exceptionalism*: when thy nation, party, family, or self whispers "we are exempt" (too strong, too righteous, too modern, too central), answer with the verse's *kam* (how many!): how many strong (ʿĀd), skilled (Thamūd), sacred (Quraysh's custodianship did not save its deniers at Badr), and sure — all *ahlaknā* (We destroyed). *Fourth, act on non-return*: since the dead advise not, *be* the living adviser — warn thy household (the runner ran, v. 20), write thy will (v. 29's lesson), repent *today* (destruction's door locks). For the verse's final mercy is its tense: *have they not considered* — asked of the *living*, while considering avails. The destroyed cannot return to thee; but thou canst still refrain from joining them. Consider — and live.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:32

> **Each of them shall be a group arraigned before Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa In Kullun Lammā Jamīʿun: The Grammar of Total Gathering**

*"Each of them shall be"* — *wa in kullun lammā* — the Arabic heaps universality: *in* (not/indeed) + *kull* (each, every) + *lammā* (but/except, emphatic) + *jamīʿ* (all together): "each of them shall *all* be" — every individual (*kull*) within the totality (*jamīʿ*), none missing, none doubled. The destroyed generations of verse 31 — who "return not" to *this* world — *will* return to the *next*: non-return to earth (v. 31) precedes compelled return to judgment (this verse). *"A group arraigned before Us"* — *muḥḍarūn* (made present, arraigned, produced): the judicial passive — they do not *come* (voluntary) but are *brought* (compelled), presented like defendants, *before Us* (*ladaynā* — in Our presence, Our vicinity: no distance, no veil, no attorney). The verse (with v. 53: *"they will all be arraigned before Us"*) establishes the Resurrection's first principle: *universality without exception* — believers and disbelievers, prophets and peoples, kings and outcasts, the town's stoners and its stoned runner — *jamīʿ* (all). The Qur'an states it as creation's appointment:

> *"And thy Lord shall come, and the angels rank on rank. And Hell shall be brought that Day."* (Qur'an 89:22–23)

No *qarn* (generation, v. 31) is exempt from the *jamīʿ* (gathering): destruction scattered them in time; resurrection collects them in eternity.

**Two Arraignments: The Gathering and the Reckoning**

The commentators reconcile this verse (all arraigned) with verses indicating only disbelievers arraigned for punishment (30:16; 34:38) by distinguishing *two* arraignments. First, the *gathering* (*ḥashr*): all mankind produced before God — this verse and verse 53 — the assembly itself, where ranks are sorted (believers to the right, deniers to the left; 56:8–9; 69:19, 25). Second, the *reckoning-unto-punishment*: disbelievers driven from the assembly to the Fire's edge (34:38; 30:16), while believers proceed to the Garden (v. 55: *"busy rejoicing"*). The Prophet ﷺ described the gathering's dread:

> *"Mankind will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection barefoot, naked, uncircumcised." ʿĀʾishah said, "O Messenger of God, men and women together, looking at one another?" He said, "O ʿĀʾishah, the matter will be too grave for them to look at one another."* (al-Bukhārī 6527; Muslim 2859, from ʿĀʾishah)

And its sorting:

> *"The first to be judged among people on the Day of Resurrection..."* (Muslim 1905 — the ḥadīth of the show-off martyr, scholar, and philanthropist judged first)

The two-stage structure answers both hope and fear: hope — the gathering includes the righteous (thou shalt see prophets, martyrs, the runner, v. 26); fear — the gathering *exposes* all before sorting any (no one bypasses *muḥḍarūn*, made-present). Presence precedes verdict; exposure precedes honour. Even the forgiven stand *ladaynā* (before Us) — awe first, then *"Peace!"* (v. 58).

**"Before Us": The Intimacy and Terror of Ladaynā**

*"Before Us"* (*ladaynā*) — *ladā* denoting nearest presence, immediate vicinity: not "before Our court" (distant tribunal) but "before *Us*" (the Person, as it were, of the Judge). The pronoun's intimacy terrifies and consoles by turns. It terrifies: excuses crafted for human courts ("I forgot," "they misled me," "I was busy") dissolve *ladā* the All-Knowing — *"We know what they hide and what they disclose"* (v. 76) — and limbs testify (v. 65) in the presence no lie enters. The Qur'an voices the criminals' realization:

> *"Woe unto us! What ails this book, that it leaves neither small nor great save that it has counted it?"* (Qur'an 18:49)

It consoles: the same *ladaynā* that exposes also *receives* — the runner entered the Garden by direct invitation (v. 26), the Garden's people hear *"Peace!"* from the Lord Himself (v. 58), and the wronged find their Judge has witnessed their wrong (4:148: *"God loves not harsh speech save from one wronged"*). *Ladaynā* is thus the verse's devotional center: live *now* as *muḥḍar* (one-made-present) — prayer as *ladaynā*-rehearsal (standing, reciting, bowing before Him), solitude as *ladaynā*-reality (He nearer than the jugular vein, 50:16) — and the final *ladaynā* will be homecoming, not ambush. The arraigned who practiced presence will find Presence practiced in mercy.

**Continuity With the Sūrah: Record, Cry, and Arraignment**

The verse locks into Yā Sīn's judicial architecture at every joint. Backward: verse 12's registry (*"We have counted all things in a clear registry"*) is the *dossier* for this arraignment — the *muḥḍarūn* are produced *with* their books (17:71: *"every people called to its book"*). The town's Cry (v. 29) was arraignment's *preview* — sudden production from life to death; this verse is its *completion* — production from death to judgment. Forward: the trumpet blasts (vv. 51–53) are this verse's *mechanism* — the Cry that *produces* (*fa-idhā hum... muḥḍarūn*, v. 53, identical verb); the sealed mouths and speaking limbs (v. 65) are its *procedure*; the Garden/Fire contrast (vv. 55–64) its *outcome*. Nothing in the sūrah is ornamental: record (v. 12) → death/Cry (vv. 29, 49) → raising (vv. 51–52) → arraignment (vv. 32, 53) → verdict (vv. 54–64) — a single judicial spine. Al-Rāzī admires the order's pedagogy: the sūrah *shows* judgment's stages before judgment arrives, granting readers the unique privilege of *preparing* for arraignment while arraignment is still verse, not event. Every recitation of Yā Sīn is a rehearsal *muḥḍar*: stand, hear, tremble — then amend, before the standing is compelled.

**Preparing for Muḥḍarūn: Practices of the Soon-Arraigned**

The verse converts eschatology into agenda. *First, audit with the registry*: since *muḥḍarūn* means produced *with records* (v. 12), review thine nightly — the Prophet's ﷺ counsel through ʿUmar's maxim: *"Call yourselves to account before you are called to account"* (al-Tirmidhī, *Ṣifat al-Qiyāmah*; mawqūf on ʿUmar, universally cited). Each night's *muḥāsabah* (self-reckoning) is voluntary *iḥḍār* (self-production) that lightens compelled *iḥḍār*. *Second, travel light*: the arraigned bring only deeds (wealth, kin, status left at the grave — 6:94: *"you have come unto Us alone, as We created you the first time, leaving behind what We bestowed"*). Reduce luggage: settle debts, return trusts, forgive debtors, shed grudges — arrive *ladaynā* unencumbered. *Third, secure witnesses in advance*: limbs will testify (v. 65) — employ them now in righteous testimony (tongue in *dhikr*, hands in charity, feet to the mosque, v. 12's footprints). The earth itself will testify (99:4–5: *"it will recount its tidings"*) — pray in many places, do good in many lands, multiplying venue-witnesses. *Fourth, rehearse presence*: pray as *muḥḍar* — the Prophet ﷺ: *"Worship God as though thou seest Him, for though thou seest Him not, He sees thee"* (Muslim 8, from ʿUmar — the *iḥsān*-clause of the Gabriel ḥadīth). Five daily arraignments voluntarily attended transform the final Arraignment from terror to reunion. For the verse's final mercy is its *jamīʿ* (all): thou shalt not stand alone — prophets, martyrs, the runner, thy righteous beloved stand *with* thee, and above all, the Merciful presides. *Muḥḍarūn ladaynā* — produced, all, before Us: dread for the heedless, dawn for the ready. Ready thyself — the production-order is signed, the trumpet tuned, the registry (v. 12) already counting.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:33

> **A sign unto them is the dead earth: We revive it and bring forth grain therefrom, that they may eat thereof.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyatun Lahum: The Earth as Exhibit A**

*"A sign unto them"* — *āyatun lahum* — opens the sūrah's second great movement (vv. 33–44): from *history* (the town, destroyed generations) to *nature* — the gallery of cosmic signs proving resurrection. *Āyah* (sign) is the Qur'an's master-term for evidence: verses are *āyāt* (signs in speech), nature's wonders *āyāt* (signs in creation), miracles *āyāt* (signs in act) — one word uniting Book and world as two editions of one message. Al-Qurṭubī notes *āyah* here carries three senses at once: *contemplation* (something to ponder), *blessing* (bounty to enjoy), and *warning* (proof that convicts ingratitude). *"Unto them"* (*lahum*) — for the deniers of resurrection (vv. 48, 77–79), the heedless (v. 6), the mockers (v. 30): the sign is *addressed*, gift and summons together. *"The dead earth"* (*al-arḍ al-maytah*) — *maytah* (dead, lifeless): the same adjective for corpses. Arabia knew the image intimately: wadis cracked, dust seas, years without rain — then clouds, downpour, and within days green eruption. The Qur'an's rhetorical genius: it chooses as resurrection's *first* exhibit not a rare miracle but the *commonest* one — every harvest a rehearsal, every spring a sermon. Whoever has eaten bread has witnessed resurrection and cannot plead ignorance of its possibility.

**"We Revive It": Divine Agency in Every Germination**

*"We revive it"* — *aḥyaynahā* — *We* (divine plural of majesty): revival is God's direct act, not nature's habit. The verb (*aḥyā*, to give life) is the same as verse 12's *"We give life to the dead"* (*nuḥyī al-mawtā*) — the sūrah's deliberate echo: earth's revival and corpses' revival are *one* power's two works. The Qur'an forges the parallel explicitly across a dozen passages:

> *"And He revives the earth after its death. Even so shall you be brought forth."* (Qur'an 30:19; cf. 7:57; 16:65; 22:5; 29:63; 30:24, 50; 35:9; 41:39; 43:11; 45:5; 50:9–11)

The logic is *a fortiori* and empirical: thou *seest* dead soil — inert, cracked, seedless-seeming — drink rain and erupt with life thou didst not plant and canst not fully explain (what *is* germination's spark, even to botanists?). If God performs this resurrection *annually before thine eyes*, on what ground difficult He the final one? The denier's objection ("who revives bones, decayed?" — v. 78) is answered preemptively: *the One who revives earth, decayed*. Al-Rāzī adds the metaphysical depth: earth's revival is the *harder* case (scattered, dissolved, anonymous matter reconstituted into ordered life), yet none doubts it — human revival (each soul preserved, each body reconstituted by its Knower, v. 79: *"He knows every creation"*) is the *easier*, yet many doubt. Doubt reverses reason's verdict: believe the witnessed, and the promised follows.

**"And Bring Forth Grain... That They May Eat": Resurrection as Provision**

*"And bring forth grain therefrom, that they may eat thereof"* — *wa akhrajnā minhā ḥabban fa-minhu yaʾkulūn* — the sign's purpose is *nourishment*: revival feeds. *Ḥabb* (grain — wheat, barley, rice: civilization's staples) is specified as provision's foundation — bread, the staff of life, the daily resurrection men eat without pondering. *"That they may eat"* (*yaʾkulūn*) — the verbs descend from majesty (*We revive*) to intimacy (*they eat*): omnipotence serves appetite; the Cry-wielder (v. 29) is also the Bread-giver. The Qur'an elsewhere traces the full chain of gift:

> *"Have you considered that which you sow? Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower?"* (Qur'an 56:63–64)

— farmers *sow* (human part), God *grows* (divine part) — and gratitude's absence indicts:

> *"Will they not then give thanks?"* (v. 35, the passage's refrain)

As in verse 12, the commentators (al-Alūsī, Ibn Kathīr) add the inward parallel: as God revives dead earth into grain, He revives dead hearts (heedless, v. 6; sealed, vv. 8–9) into faith — through the rain of revelation. The hardened heart that drinks the Qur'an and sprouts prayer, charity, and tears is *this verse* enacted inwardly. Every convert is earth's spring in a soul; every repentance, a harvest from dust.

**The Sign's Threefold Jurisprudence: Ponder, Enjoy, Thank**

Al-Qurṭubī's triple sense (contemplation, blessing, warning) structures the believer's response. *Ponder* (*tafakkur*): the verse commands implicit reflection — *how* does rain + dust = bread? Modern agronomy details mechanisms (imbibition, enzymes, photosynthesis) but no laboratory manufactures the *life* — mechanisms are God's *means*, not His *rivals*. Pondering ends in the Qur'an's refrain:

> *"Truly in that are signs for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 13:3; cf. 3:190–91; 41:53 on creation's wonders as proofs of Oneness, Wisdom, Omnipotence)

*Enjoy* (*tamattuʿ*): eating is worship's fuel — the verse *intends* consumption (*yaʾkulūn*), not ascetic refusal. The Prophet ﷺ: *"The food of one suffices two"* (al-Bukhārī 5393) — blessing multiplies sharing — and grain's abundance obligates feeding (v. 47's *"spend of that wherewith God has provided you"*). *Thank* (*shukr*): verses 35 and 73 close the passage's movements with *"will they not then give thanks?"* — ingratitude converts blessing into evidence-for-prosecution (the *muḥḍarūn*, v. 32, will answer for every loaf). The three form a cycle: pondering sweetens enjoying, enjoying obligates thanking, thanking deepens pondering. The denier breaks the cycle at its first link (eats without pondering); the believer completes it at table thrice daily. Every meal is thus a miniature of the verse: dead earth, revived, become grain, become thee — *consider, eat, thank*.

**Eating Resurrection Thrice Daily in the Modern World**

The verse lands on modernity with special force, for modernity has professionalized eating-without-pondering. Grain arrives as packaging — no soil, no rain, no *aḥyaynahā* (We revived) visible — and the eater, severed from the sign, finds resurrection "unscientific" while chewing it. Practices of reconnection: *name the revival at meals* — the Prophet's ﷺ table-manners (*tasmiyah* before, *ḥamdalah* after — al-Bukhārī 5458; Muslim 2734) are this verse's liturgy: *"Praise be to God who fed us, gave us drink, and made us Muslims."* Teach children bread's biography (dead earth → rain → grain → loaf → thee) as resurrection-catechesis: *the God who built thee from dust-and-rain rebuilds thee from dust-and-Cry*. *Gardening as theology*: plant, water, watch — the seed's black burial and green resurrection preaches v. 33 without words. *Famine-consciousness*: billions eat earth's revival marginally — share grain (charity, v. 47), waste none (7:31: *"waste not"*), and taste in every loaf both earth's past resurrection and thine own future one. For the verse's final mercy is its daily frequency: God did not make resurrection's proof a once-in-history spectacle (witnessed by few, doubted by many) but a thrice-daily meal (witnessed by all, doubted only by the determinedly veiled, vv. 8–9). Thou hast eaten ten thousand resurrections; the eleven-thousandth — thine own (vv. 51–53) — requires no greater power than the loaf in thy hand. *Consider, eat — and believe.*

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:34

> **And We place gardens of date palms and grapevines therein and make springs flow forth,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jannātin Min Nakhīl: Gardens as Resurrection's Showcase**

*"And We place gardens of date palms and grapevines therein"* — *wa jaʿalnā fīhā jannātin min nakhīlin wa aʿnāb* — *We place* (*jaʿalnā*): the same divine agency as verse 33's *"We revive"* — gardens are God's *placing*, not earth's accident. *Jannāt* (gardens, plural of *jannah* — the same word as Paradise): earthly gardens are Paradise's namesakes and foretastes — every orchard a *jannah*-in-miniature, every vintage a whisper of *"gardens beneath which rivers flow."* *Date palms and grapevines* (*nakhīl wa aʿnāb*) are specified, as al-Qurṭubī notes, because Arabs deemed them the best of fruits — the staple-sweet (dates: desert's bread, traveler's provision, fast-breaker) and the luxury-fruit (grapes: fresh and dried, shade and sweetness) — together spanning necessity and delight. The pairing recurs as provision's emblem:

> *"And gardens of date palms and grapevines."* (Qur'an 2:266; cf. 23:19)

By naming the noblest, the verse implies all: olives, pomegranates, figs (6:99, 141; 95:1) — *"all forms of plant life by which people are nourished"* (al-Qurṭubī). Theologically, the specification answers resurrection-doubt with *variety*: the same dead earth (v. 33), the same rain, produces *palm* (towering, perennial, fibrous) and *vine* (trailing, seasonal, juicy) — distinct natures from identical dust. If one soil yields a thousand species, shall one Cry not yield a billion persons, each distinct (v. 79: *"He knows every creation"*)? Diversity from dust is resurrection's daily proof.

**"And Make Springs Flow Forth": Water as Life's Vein**

*"And make springs flow forth"* — *wa fajjarnā fīhā mina al-ʿuyūn* — *fajjarnā* (We caused to gush, burst open): water *explodes* from rock — the verb of Moses' stone (*"burst forth twelve springs,"* 2:60) and of Judgment's upheaval. *ʿUyūn* (springs, fountains — also "eyes"): earth's eyes weeping life, or earth's veins bleeding mercy. The ordering (gardens *then* springs, though springs *feed* gardens) is rhetoric's delight: mention the beloved (fruit) before its means (water), letting gratitude precede analysis. Hydrologically, the verse compresses the water-cycle's miracle: rain (v. 33's implied reviver) → aquifer → spring → garden → fruit → thee — each link a divine *jaʿl* (placing). The Qur'an elsewhere unveils water's primacy:

> *"And We made from water every living thing."* (Qur'an 21:30)

— the verse's springs are that axiom flowing visibly. For Arabia — where a spring meant tribe, settlement, survival — *fajjarnā* was no metaphor but biography: Zamzam's gush saved Hagar and Ishmael; Madinah's wells anchored the Hijrah. Every spring is Hagar's miracle repeated: despairing earth, sudden gush, life secured. And eschatologically, springs preview Paradise's rivers (47:15: *"rivers of water unpolluted, rivers of milk... rivers of wine... rivers of honey"*) — earth's *ʿuyūn* are heaven's rivers in rehearsal. Drink, and remember: the Gusher of springs floods Gardens (v. 55–57) as easily as orchards.

**Gardens and Springs as Anti-Idolatry**

The verse's *We* (*jaʿalnā... fajjarnā*) quietly demolishes the town's (and Quraysh's) gods (vv. 23, 74): *which* idol placed a garden? *Which* goddess gushed a spring? The Qur'an presses the challenge:

> *"Is He Who created the heavens and the earth, and sent down water for you from the sky, whereby We cause luxuriant gardens to grow — you could never cause their trees to grow — a god with God? Nay, they are a people who ascribe equals."* (Qur'an 27:60)

The logic is the runner's (vv. 22–23) in botanical form: worship belongs to the Placer and Gusher, not the placed-and-gushing. Al-Rāzī notes the verse also refutes the "nature-worship" perennially tempting the aesthetic: gardens enchant — blossom, fragrance, shade, birdsong — and enchantment slides into worship (sacred groves in every paganism). The verse reassigns enchantment's Object: admire the garden, worship the Gardener. *"We place"* fences every orchard with monotheism: beauty is *made*, and the Made points to the Maker. Modern nature-mysticism ("the universe provides") commits the town's error in green dress — receiving *jannāt* while ignoring *jaʿalnā*.
