The verse corrects the grammar: gardens do not *happen*; they are *placed*. Springs do not *occur*; they are *gushed*. Thanksgiving's direction is heavenward, or it is misdelivered.

**From Sign to Table: The Passage's Movement Toward Eating**

Placed between verse 33 (grain for eating) and verse 35 (fruit for eating and thanks), this verse is the passage's center — and its movement is deliberately *toward the mouth*: grain (v. 33) → gardens-springs (v. 34) → fruit-eating-thanks (v. 35). Revelation walks the reader from field to orchard to table, making theology *tasteable*. The movement mirrors the sūrah's larger pedagogy: abstract (Wise Qur'an, v. 2) → historical (town, vv. 13–30) → natural (earth, vv. 33–35) → cosmic (night, sun, moon, vv. 37–40) → personal (ships, v. 41; cattle, vv. 71–73) → eschatological (Garden's fruit, v. 57 — *"therein they have fruit"*). Gardens-then (v. 34) rhyme with Gardens-after (v. 57): earth's *jannāt* are appetizers; Paradise's *jannah* the feast. The eater who ponders the appetizer (v. 34) qualifies for the feast (v. 57); the eater who gulps unthankfully (v. 35's *"will they not then give thanks?"*) forfeits both. Al-Qurṭubī's triple sense (contemplation, blessing, warning — v. 33) applies per fruit: *ponder* the palm (decades of sweetness from dust), *enjoy* the grape (gift, not wage), *heed* the warning (the Giver resurrects). Every orchard is thus a classroom with edible textbooks; every spring, a lecture in flow. The wise enroll seasonally — spring's blossom, summer's fruit, autumn's harvest, winter's rest — reading *jaʿalnā* (We placed) in each term's curriculum.

**Planting, Watering, and Tasting Gardens Today**

The verse yields practices for hands, mouths, and hearts. *Plant*: the Prophet ﷺ made planting eschatological defiance — *"If the Hour comes while one of you holds a seedling, let him plant it"* (Aḥmad 3:191, from Anas; graded ṣaḥīḥ) — hope's last act outlasting world's end. Gardens thou plantest (trees, knowledge, institutions, children) are *ājaʿalnā*-partnerships: God gives growth, thou givest hands — and ongoing charity (v. 12: *ṣadaqah jāriyah*) flows like *ʿuyūn* (springs) after thy burial. *Water*: give drink — the Prophet ﷺ: *"The best charity is giving water"* (Abū Dāwūd 1679; al-Nasāʾī 3694, from Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah; graded ḥasan) — wells, fountains, and cups in thirsty hands continue verse's *fajjarnā* (gushing) through thine. *Taste theologically*: eat dates and grapes (and all fruit) with the verse's grammar — *He placed, He gushed, I eat, I thank*. The Prophet ﷺ broke fasts with dates (Abū Dāwūd 2356, from Anas) — earth's *nakhīl* (palms) sweetening worship's resumption. *Visit gardens ponderingly*: orchards, parks, and farms as *āyāt* (signs) — walk them with verses 33–35 recited, reading palms as resurrection-arguments and springs as mercy-manifests. And *yearn through foretaste*: let every garden's shade remind of Paradise's (v. 56: *"reclining upon couches in the shade"*), every fruit of its (v. 57: *"therein they have fruit"*). Earth's *jannāt* are trailers; heaven's *jannah* the film. Watch the trailer thankfully — and live for the premiere.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:35

> **That they may eat of its fruit and of that which their hands have worked. Will they not then give thanks?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Liyaʾkulū Min Thamarihi: Eating — The Sign's Destination**

*"That they may eat of its fruit"* — *li-yaʾkulū min thamarihi* — the *lām* of purpose: earth's revival (v. 33), gardens-and-springs (v. 34) exist *so that* men *eat*. Theology arrives at the table: omnipotence (reviving dust), wisdom (palms-and-vines variety), mercy (gushing springs) — all funnel into *eating*. *"Its fruit"* (*thamarihi*) — the pronoun debated: *its* (the earth's/plant's fruit through spring-water — al-Qurṭubī) or *His* (God's fruit, He the Producer — al-Alūsī, Ibn Kathīr). Both true: earth yields it, God produces it — means and Maker in one morsel. The Qur'an's food-theology peaks here: eating is not base necessity embarrassing the spiritual but *divine purpose* dignifying the material — God revives worlds *to feed thee*. The Prophet's ﷺ table confirms the dignity: he ate thankfully, fed generously, and promised:

> *"The one who eats and gives thanks has the same reward as the one who fasts and is patient."* (al-Tirmidhī 2486; Ibn Mājah 1765, from Abū Hurayrah; graded ḥasan-ṣaḥīḥ)

— gratitude elevates eating to fasting's rank. The verse thus sanctifies appetite: hunger is not shame but *summons* — to the Giver through the gift. Every fruit is a love-letter from the Reviver (v. 33), delivered by palm-post and spring-caravan, addressed: *eat, beloved, and know Me*.

**"And of That Which Their Hands Have Worked": Labour's Loaf**

*"And of that which their hands have worked"* — *wa mā ʿamilathu aydīhim* — *hands* (*aydī*) as labour's emblem: plowing, sowing, pruning, pressing, baking — man's *ʿamal* (work) joining God's *jaʿl* (placing). Read positively (the translation's sense), the verse honours *earning*: eat God's fruit *and* thy labour's product — the paycheck's bread alongside the orchard's. The Prophet ﷺ crowned hand-earned food:

> *"No one eats any food that is better than that from the work of his own hand."* (al-Bukhārī 2072, from al-Miqdām — cited on this verse)

— David ate from his hands (armour-craft) though a king; the lawful-earning labourer outranks the idle inheritor. The alternative reading (*wa mā ʿamilathu aydīhim* as "though their hands did *not* make it" — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī) inverts to pure grace: eat fruit *your hands never created* — for all fruits are ultimately God's (27:60; 56:64: *"Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower?"*). The two readings harmonize as labour's theology: *work as if provision depends on hands, know as if it depends on God* — hands *earn*, God *creates*; wages are *means*, fruit *gift*. Either way, the verse dignifies the worker and humbles him together: thy hands matter (so labour nobly), yet produce nothing ultimate (so thank humbly). The office, farm, and kitchen are this verse's chapels; the paycheck, its *thamar* (fruit).

**A-Fa-Lā Yashkurūn: The Refrain of Ingratitude**

*"Will they not then give thanks?"* — *a-fa-lā yashkurūn* — the interrogative of astonishment: *after* revival (v. 33), gardens (v. 34), fruit-and-labour (v. 35a) — *still* no thanks? The *fa-* (then) marks ingratitude's absurdity *following* blessing: gift received, Giver ignored. The Qur'an names ingratitude humanity's statistic:

> *"Truly God is Possessed of Bounty for mankind, but most of mankind do not give thanks."* (Qur'an 2:243; cf. 10:60; 12:38; 22:66; 25:50; 40:61)

*Shukr* (thanks) in revelation is tripartite: heart's recognition (this is from God), tongue's praise (*al-ḥamdu li-Llāh*), limbs' obedience (using gifts in His service — strength for justice, wealth for charity, knowledge for guidance). Its opposite, *kufr* (disbelief), literally means *covering* — ingratitude *covers* the Giver, hiding Him beneath the gift. The verse's question thus diagnoses disbelief's root: resurrection-denial (v. 48), messenger-mockery (v. 30), charity-scoffing (v. 47) all sprout from unthankfulness — the heart that will not thank will not bow, give, or believe. Conversely, thanksgiving is faith's seed: the thankful eater asks *Whom* to thank, finds *the Reviver*, and follows where thanks leads — worship (v. 22), following (v. 21), running (v. 20). *A-fa-lā yashkurūn* is therefore the passage's altar-call: blessings displayed (vv. 33–35a), response demanded (v. 35b). The only unacceptable answer is silence.

**The Economics of Gratitude: Why Thanks Matters to the Thanked-For**

A philosophical reader asks: why does omnipotence *want* thanks? Does God *need* praise? The Qur'an answers: thanks benefits the thanker, not the Thanked — *"whosoever gives thanks, gives thanks for himself"* (31:12; cf. 27:40). Gratitude's profits are the grateful's: psychological (contentment — the thankful count gifts, the unthankful, lacks), social (generosity — thankful receivers become cheerful givers, v. 47's command fulfilled), and eschatological (increase — *"if you give thanks, I shall surely increase you,"* 14:7 — and preservation from punishment, 4:147: *"Why would God punish you, if you give thanks and believe?"*). Ingratitude's losses likewise rebound (v. 19's reflexive law): the unthankful eater enjoys fruit yet forfeits Increase, eats earth's gardens yet loses heaven's (v. 57), consumes resurrection's proof (v. 33) yet denies resurrection (v. 78). Al-Rāzī frames it as the rationality of worship: thanks is *truth* (acknowledging the true Producer) and *justice* (rendering the due response) — ingratitude is falsehood (ascribing fruit to "nature"/"luck"/"my hands alone") and injustice (withholding the Giver's due). *A-fa-lā yashkurūn* is thus reason's question and justice's demand in one: *will you not be true and just — over fruit?*

**Thanking at Tables, in Fields, and Through Hands**

The verse ritualizes gratitude in three arenas. *At tables*: bracket every meal with remembrance — *tasmiyah* (naming God) before, *ḥamdalah* (praising) after — the Prophet's ﷺ minimum; add reflection (this fruit's biography: dead earth → rain → garden → spring → hands → me — vv. 33–35) and sharing (feed another from thy fruit — v. 47's *"spend of that wherewith God has provided you"*). Teach children the verse's question as table-grace: *will we not then give thanks?* — answered by chorus *al-ḥamdu li-Llāh*. *In fields and workplaces*: honour hand-work (earn lawfully, excellently — *itqān* — as worship), yet confess hand-limits (no hand grows grain — 56:64) — begin tasks with *bismillah* (acknowledging the Grower), end with *ḥamdalah* (thanking the Enabler). The farmer, coder, mason, and merchant all eat *"of that which their hands have worked"* — and all hands work by Another's enabling. *Through thanks-multiplication*: convert thanks into thanks-worthy acts — the thankful eater feeds (charity), the thankful earner gives (zakāh/ṣadaqah), the thankful healthy visits the sick — gratitude that does not *act* is sentiment; gratitude that *gives* is *shukr*. For the verse's final mercy is its *lām* of purpose (*li-yaʾkulū* — *that* they may eat): God revived earth *for* thy eating — purpose fulfilled in thy nourishment, crowned by thy thanks. Eat, then — *and thank*. The fruit is sweet; the Thanks is sweeter; the Increaser (14:7) sweetest. *A-fa-lā yashkurūn* — will *thou*?

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:36

> **Glory be unto Him, Who has created the pairs, all of them, from what the earth makes grow, and from themselves, and from what they know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Subḥāna: Glory as the Passage's Doxology**

*"Glory be unto Him"* — *subḥāna* (transcendent, flawless, far-removed) — the verse erupts in *tasbīḥ* (glorification) after the gallery of earthly signs (vv. 33–35): revival, gardens, springs, fruit, labour. *Subḥāna* declares God *beyond* all the idolaters associate with Him (v. 23's impotent gods, v. 74's taken gods) *and* voices amazement at creation's splendour — transcendence and wonder in one word. The Qur'an positions *subḥāna* at awe's peaks — the Night Journey (17:1), creation's pairs (here), dominion's end (v. 83, the sūrah's seal) — doxology punctuating cosmology. The placement after eating-and-thanks (v. 35) teaches worship's sequence: *behold* blessing (vv. 33–35a) → *thank* for it (v. 35b) → *glorify* beyond it (v. 36a). Thanks (*shukr*) acknowledges gifts; glory (*tasbīḥ*) acknowledges the Giver's incomparability — gifts *received*, then Giver *exalted*. The Prophet ﷺ weighted the word's scale:

> *"Two words are light upon the tongue, heavy upon the Scale, beloved to the Compassionate: 'Glory be to God and praise Him' (subḥāna Llāhi wa bi-ḥamdihi) and 'Glory be to God the Magnificent' (subḥāna Llāhi al-ʿAẓīm)."* (al-Bukhārī 6406; Muslim 2694, from Abū Hurayrah)

Recited after meals (v. 35's context), *subḥāna* converts digestion into devotion: the eater glorifies the Feeder's transcendence over all need of food.

**Azwāj Kullahā: The Pairs, All of Them**

*"Who has created the pairs, all of them"* — *alladhī khalaqa al-azwāja kullahā* — *azwāj* (pairs, spouses, kinds, species): al-Qurṭubī notes the double sense — *kinds* (the myriad species God created) and *pairs* (male-female, complementary dualities). *"All of them"* (*kullahā*) universalizes: no pair uncreated, no duality unordained. The Qur'an's pairing-axiom stands behind the verse:

> *"And of all things We created pairs, that haply you may remember."* (Qur'an 51:49)

— *"that haply you may remember"* (*tadhakkarūn*): pairs exist *to remind* — of the One who is *not* a pair, the Single (*al-witr*, the Odd) beyond duality. Night-day, sun-moon, male-female, life-death, seen-unseen, heaven-earth, mercy-wrath, Garden-Fire — creation's binary code spells *waḥdāniyyah* (divine Oneness) by contrast: all things paired *except* their Maker. Ibn Kathīr cites 51:49 as this verse's commentary: pairing is creation's signature, singleness the Creator's. Philosophically, the verse anticipates complementarity's ubiquity — positive-negative charges, matter-antimatter, the double helix's strands — each pair's mutual need testifying mutual Contingency and the Necessary One beyond. The denier of resurrection (v. 78) is answered by pairing's logic: He who paired dust with rain to birth grain (vv. 33–35) pairs souls with bodies to birth persons — and can re-pair them after dissolution (vv. 51–53, 79). Pairing proves the Pairer; the Pairer promises re-pairing.

**"From What the Earth Makes Grow, and From Themselves, and From What They Know Not": Three Realms of Pairing**

The verse inventories pairing across three domains, ascending from visible to invisible. *"From what the earth makes grow"* (*mimmā tunbitu al-arḍ*) — botany's pairs: date-palm male-female (Arabia knew artificial pollination — the Prophet ﷺ alluded to it, Muslim 2361), every species in complementary forms — the same gardens of verse 34 now revealed as *paired*. *"And from themselves"* (*wa min anfusihim*) — *anfus* (souls/selves): humanity's pairs — *"male and female"* (53:45: *"He creates the two (zawjayn) — male and female"*), mates (*azwāj*) from a single soul (4:1; 6:98; 7:189; 39:6) — and inwardly, soul's own dualities (spirit-body, reason-desire, conscience-impulse). *"And from what they know not"* (*wa mimmā lā yaʿlamūn*) — the unbounded remainder: unseen pairs — angels' ranks, jinn's kinds, cosmic forces, subatomic dualities, realities no eye has seen:

> *"And He creates that which you know not."* (Qur'an 16:8)

The third clause is revelation's humility-lesson and science's charter in one: creation *exceeds* knowledge — always has (the verse's first hearers knew nothing of galaxies, germs, or genes), always will. Every age's *"what they know not"* dwarfs its known; the wise confess the remainder while studying the known. Al-Rāzī marvels: the verse claims universal pairing *including* the unknown — a falsifiable-sounding universal uttered in seventh-century Arabia, confirmed wherever inquiry reaches (polarity in magnetism, duality in light, symmetry in particles). The unknown pairs await discoverers; their Pairer awaits glorifiers. *Subḥāna* — glory for the known *and* the unknown.

**Pairing as Answer to Loneliness, Pride, and Despair**

Beyond cosmology, the verse ministers psychologically. *To loneliness*: pairing is creation's law — solitude is the exception (and even Adam received Eve, 4:1). The unmarried, the widowed, the isolated are promised pairing's providence in this world (companionship, community, marriage) or the next (v. 56: *"they and their spouses"*). Loneliness is a season, not a sentence — the Pairer pairs. *To pride*: thou art *half* of pairs — male-or-female, child-of-parents, member-of-species — incompleteness is thy nature; self-sufficiency thy delusion. The arrogant chin (v. 8) forgets it is one of a pair (chin-and-neck, head-and-heart); humility remembers pairing. *To despair*: death *unpairs* (soul-body), but the Pairer re-pairs (vv. 51–53, 79) — grief's separation is temporary sundering, reunion (with beloved, with body, with Lord) the eternal pairing. The Qur'an names the ultimate pairing:

> *"And when the souls are paired."* (Qur'an 81:7 — souls rejoined to bodies, or righteous to righteous)

— resurrection as cosmic re-wedding. Every earthly pairing (marriage, friendship, parent-child) is thus a rehearsal and a promissory note: enjoy the rehearsal thankfully (v. 35), and trust the Promiser for the performance. The Pairer who paired thee once (soul-body in the womb, 22:5; 23:14) pairs thee again (soul-body from the grave, v. 51).

**Glorifying the Pairer in Thought, Marriage, and Inquiry**

Three practices honour the verse. *Glorify in thought*: make *subḥāna* the reflex of wonder — at couples (human, animal, botanical), at complements (night-day, mercy-might, vv. 5's *al-ʿAzīz al-Raḥīm*), at paradoxes resolved in Oneness. The Prophet ﷺ glorified at creation's sights (new moon, thunder, rain) — follow him: let pairing preach. *Honour pairing in marriage*: marriage is the verse's human centerpiece — *"from themselves"* pairs. Treat it as divine *khalq* (creation), not social contract alone: choose gratefully, dwell lovingly (*mawaddah wa raḥmah*, 30:21), endure patiently, and remember singleness's dignity too (the Unpaired One suffices the unpaired season). For spouses in strife: thou art *zawj* (pair-half) — halves complete, not compete. *Inquire into the unknown*: the verse's third clause (*"what they know not"*) commissions science as worship — study unknown pairs (the researcher probing duality glorifies the Pairer knowingly or not), while confessing each discovery reveals vaster unknowns. The believer-scientist reads 16:8 (*"He creates that which you know not"*) as grant-proposal and humility-oath together. And at every pairing pondered, return to the verse's first word: *subḥāna* — glory to the Unpaired Pairer, the Single behind all duals, the One (112:1: *"Say: He is God, One"*) whom pairs proclaim. Creation counts by twos; the Creator counts by One. *Glory be unto Him.*

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:37

> **And a sign unto them is the night: We strip the day therefrom, and behold, they are in darkness.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyatun Lahum: Night as the Cosmic Sign**

*"And a sign unto them is the night"* — *wa āyatun lahumu al-layl* — the second cosmic sign (after earth's revival, vv. 33–35; before sun, v. 38; moon, v. 39; orbits, v. 40): *night* (*al-layl*). The Qur'an names night-day alternation among creation's supreme proofs, alongside sun-moon:

> *"He rolls the night up into the day and rolls the day up into the night, and He made the sun and the moon subservient, each running for a term appointed."* (Qur'an 39:5; cf. 10:6; 17:12 on night-day's relation)

Why *night* first? Because night is resurrection's daily *death*: the world darkens, activity ceases, men lie as corpses in beds (sleep as *"the lesser death"* — 39:42: *"God takes the souls at death, and those that die not in their sleep"*), then dawn revives all — a funeral-and-resurrection every twenty-four hours. *"Unto them"* (*lahum*) — for resurrection-deniers (vv. 48, 78): thou diest nightly and risest morningly — yet doubtest the final rising? The sign's frequency is its force: earth's revival (v. 33) argues annually (seasons), night argues *daily* — 30,000 little resurrections per lifetime, each testifying the Great One (vv. 51–53). The denier sleeps through his own proof nightly, then demands proof daily. *Consider the night* — the verse's implicit imperative — and the Cry (v. 49) will find thee unsurprised.

**Naslakhu Minhu al-Nahār: Stripping Day Like Skin**

*"We strip the day therefrom"* — *naslakhu minhu al-nahār* — *naslakha* from *salkh* (flaying, skinning an animal): God *skins* daylight off the world the way a slaughterer skins hide off flesh. The verb's vividness is deliberate — cosmic butchery as theology: day is night's *covering* (hide over body), and sunset is flaying — light peeled, darkness (the carcass, the original) exposed. The image reverses naïve cosmology: men imagine day as norm, night as intruder; revelation reveals night as *substrate* (the universe is dark; stars are pinpricks), day as *garment* temporarily draped. Modern astronomy confirms the metaphor's precision: space is black; daylight is a thin scatter-shell around one star's planet — skin indeed, microns-thin cosmically, stripped nightly. The *We* (*naslakhu*) asserts agency: sunset is not mechanics' accident but Majesty's act — each dusk a divine *salkh*, each dawn a re-clothing (*"He clothes the night with day,"* 7:54 — *yughshī*, covering/garmenting). Al-Rāzī marvels at the verb's sensory theology: Bedouin hearers *knew* skinning (daily slaughter) — the verse translates astronomy into abattoir, the cosmos into the familiar. God speaks men's trades to teach heaven's truths: to herders, skinning; to sailors, ships (vv. 41–43); to farmers, grain (v. 33). The Wise Qur'an (v. 2) is wise in *imagery* too.

**Fa-Idhā Hum Muẓlimūn: "Behold, They Are in Darkness"**

*"And behold, they are in darkness"* — *fa-idhā hum muẓlimūn* — *fa-idhā* (then, behold!) suddenness: stripping complete, darkness disclosed — *muẓlimūn* (in darkness, darkened): men *entered* into *ẓulmah* (darkness). The plural encompasses all: kings in palaces, caravans in deserts, ships at sea (vv. 41–43), the town's stoners (v. 18) and the runner (v. 20) — darkness democratizes. *Ẓulmah* carries moral resonance: the same root as *ẓulm* (injustice, wrongdoing) — darkness as sin's element (deeds hidden in night, 6:76's *"when night covered him"*), and disbelief's (vv. 8–9's veiled see-not). The Qur'an contrasts the two darknesses: night's *physical* darkness (a *sign*, this verse — good, reflective, restful) versus heedlessness's *spiritual* darkness (a *punishment*, vv. 8–9 — blinding). The believer navigates the first by lamps (literal and scriptural — *"a light whereby he walks among men,"* 6:122) and flees the second by guidance. *"Behold"* (*idhā*) summons witnesses: step outside at dusk in desert (the first hearers' observatory) and *watch* the flaying — horizon's hide peeling westward, stars (the carcass's exposure) emerging. No telescope needed; only *consideration* (v. 31's *a-lam yaraw*). Nightly, God performs the universe's most-watched sign before its least-watching audience.

**Night's Mercies: Rest, Covering, and Intimacy**

The verse names night as *sign*, but the Qur'an elsewhere unfolds night as *mercy* — and the mercies deepen the sign. *Rest* (*sakan*): *"He made the night for rest"* (10:67; 25:47; 78:9–11: *"We made your sleep for rest, the night as covering, the day for livelihood"*) — the flayed world sleeps: bodies repair, minds consolidate, hearts unclench. Resurrection's rehearsal (sleep-death, dawn-raising) is *also* compassion's recess — God kills thee gently nightly to prove He can, and revives thee kindly to prove He will. *Covering* (*libās*): night clothes private life — spouses (*"they are a garment for you,"* 2:187), repentance (tears unseen, v. 11's *"fears the Compassionate unseen"*), the Prophet's ﷺ Hijrah-night passage (v. 9's veiling as mercy). *Intimacy* (*munājāh*): night is lovers' hour — *"they used to sleep little of the night, and in the dawn's early hours they sought forgiveness"* (51:17–18); the Prophet ﷺ: *"Our Lord descends each night to the lowest heaven when a third of the night remains, saying: 'Who calls upon Me that I may answer him?'"* (al-Bukhārī 1145; Muslim 758, from Abū Hurayrah). The stripped day (this verse) thus unveils not mere darkness but *devotion's theater*: stars for navigation (16:16), stillness for recitation (73:6: *"the rising of night is mightier in impression"*), solitude for tears. The denier sees in night only absence (of light); the believer, Presence (of the Near). Same darkness; opposite beholding (*fa-idhā*).

**Keeping Night as Sign, Rest, and Tryst**

The verse schedules the believer's nights in three watches. *Ponder the stripping*: pause at *maghrib* (sunset) — watch light flay, recite this verse, remember the Greater Stripping (soul from body, v. 49's Cry *"while they dispute"*) and the Greater Clothing (resurrection's dawn, vv. 51–52). Sunset's daily awe inoculates against the Cry's shock: practiced beholders do not panic. *Rest in the covering*: honour sleep as divine gift and resurrection-rehearsal — sleep early (the Prophet ﷺ disliked pre-ʿishāʾ sleep and post-ʿishāʾ talk — al-Bukhārī 568), sleep purely (wudūʾ, *āyat al-kursī*, *duʿāʾ* — *"in Thy name I die and live,"* al-Bukhārī 6314), rise thankfully (*"praise be to God who gave us life after causing us to die,"* al-Bukhārī 6312) — each night a little death survived, each dawn a little resurrection previewed. *Rise for the tryst*: claim night's third — even two units (*rakʿahs*), even minutes — for the Unseen-Fearer's (v. 11) appointment: recite Yā Sīn itself at night (the forgiveness-promise, see Introduction), weep unseen, ask extravagantly (the Descender asks *who calls?*). For the verse's final mercy is its *daily* repetition: God strips day *every* evening — 365 annual invitations to behold, rest, and rise. The town (vv. 13–29) ignored its warners and met one Cry (v. 29); thou receivest nightly cries (dusk's flaying, dawn's raising) — heed them, and the final Cry (v. 49) will find thee awake. *Behold* the darkness — and find the Light (24:35) within it.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:38

> **And the sun runs to a dwelling place of its own. That is the decree of the Mighty, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa al-Shamsu Tajrī: The Running Sun**

*"And the sun runs"* — *wa al-shamsu tajrī* — *tajrī* (runs, flows, courses): the sun as *runner* — the same verb-family as the believer's *saʿy* (v. 20) and rivers' flowing. The Qur'an's cosmology is kinetic: sun *runs*, moon glides (v. 40), night-day *roll* (39:5), all *swim* in orbits (v. 40: *yasbaḥūn*). The running-sun image stunned desert hearers (who watched it sprint horizon-to-horizon daily) and intrigues modern ones (who learn the sun *runs* — 220 km/s around the galaxy, dragging its planets). Both readings honour the verb: apparent running (daily course, appointed for time-and-worship — prayer-times, fasting, ḥajj by its stations) and real running (galactic orbit, 225-million-year year). The Qur'an's verbs accommodate both without committing to either's physics — revelation describes *reliably* (for guidance) without describing *exhaustively* (leaving science its province). What the verse *insists* is teleology: the sun runs *to* (*li-*) — purposively, appointedly, obediently. Fire that runs errands for Another is servant, not god — the verse quietly cremates sun-worship (practiced by Sabaeans, Persians, and every solar cult): runners are *sent*; only the Sender is divine. Worship the Decree-Maker (v. 38b), not the decree-follower.

**Li-Mustaqarrin Lahā: "To a Dwelling Place of Its Own"**

*"To a dwelling place of its own"* — *li-mustaqarrin lahā* — *mustaqarr* (settling-place, dwelling, appointed term/resting): the sun runs *toward* its *mustaqarr*. The commentators unfold three senses, traditionally held together. First, the *eschatological*: the sun's final settling on Judgment Day — its prostration beneath the Throne, its rising from the west. Abū Dharr's ḥadīth anchors it: the Prophet ﷺ asked at sunset, *"Dost thou know where the sun goes?"* — "God and His Messenger know best" — *"It travels till it prostrates itself underneath the Throne and asks permission to rise again, and it is permitted. Then [near the Hour] it is about to prostrate itself, but its prostration will not be accepted, and it will ask permission to go on its course, but it will not be permitted and will instead be ordered to return whence it has come, and so it will rise in the west"* (al-Bukhārī 3199; Muslim 159 — cited by Ibn Kathīr). Second, the *seasonal*: the sun's run to its solstices — northernmost/southernmost extremes before reversing — *mustaqarr* as solstice ("sun-stopping," Latin *solstitium* — Ibn Kathīr). Third, the *cessationist* reading: *"it has no dwelling place"* (*lā mustaqarra lahā*) — reportedly preferred by Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī) — the sun *never* settles, running perpetually until the Hour. All three glorify: whether running *to* rest (eschaton), *between* rests (solstices), or *without* rest (perpetual) — the sun runs *ordered*, and Order implies Ordainer. The runner-believer (v. 20) ran once to martyrdom; the sun runs daily to its *mustaqarr* — all creation runs; only God abides (55:27: *"there remains the Face of thy Lord"*).

**Dhālika Taqdīru al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm: Decree of Might and Knowledge**

*"That is the decree of the Mighty, the Knowing"* — *dhālika taqdīru al-ʿazīzi al-ʿalīm* — *taqdīr* (decreeing, measuring, determining): the sun's course is *measured* — speed, path, term — by the *ʿAzīz* (Mighty — none diverts it) and *ʿAlīm* (Knowing — none surprises Him in it). The names pair power with precision: might *enforces* the orbit, knowledge *designs* it. Consider the design's fineness: solar distance (8 light-minutes — nearer, scorch; farther, freeze), output stability (millennia-steady furnace), cycle regularity (prayer-times predictable to the minute, Ramadan's fasting-windows, ḥajj's days — worship *scheduled* by the sun's *taqdīr*). The Qur'an generalizes:

> *"He made the sun a radiance, and the moon a light, and determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning [of time]."* (Qur'an 10:5; cf. 17:12)

— time itself (years, reckoning, sacred months, 9:36) is *taqdīr*'s gift. Al-Rāzī notes the names also answer idolatry: sun-worshippers ascribe might to the sun; the verse reassigns might to its Measurer (*al-ʿAzīz* measures; the sun is measured). And resurrection-doubters (v. 78) are answered: the Measurer who *taqdīr*s a star's billion-year run can *taqdīr* thy body's reassembly (v. 79) — *"He knows every creation."* Might-and-Knowledge that steers suns steers souls — to graves (v. 51's raising) and Gardens (v. 55's rejoicing) with equal ease.

**The Prostrating Sun: Cosmology of Worship**

Abū Dharr's ḥadīth (above) unveils the verse's deepest layer: the sun *worships* — travelling, prostrating beneath the Throne, seeking permission, rising by leave. The Qur'an confirms creation's universal worship:

> *"Hast thou not considered that unto God prostrate whosoever is in the heavens and whosoever is on the earth, and the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the beasts, and many among mankind?"* (Qur'an 22:18)

— the sun *prostrates* (22:18), *glorifies* with all (*"there is naught but that it glorifies His praise, though you understand not their glorification,"* 17:44). How sun-prostration *looks* is of the Unseen (*ghayb*) — under-Throne in a modality beyond physics — and the believer affirms without imaging (as with all *ghayb*: the Trumpet, the Scale, the Bridge). The *lesson*, however, is plain: if the mighty sun — 1.3-million-earths of fire — prostrates daily and begs leave to rise, shall *thou* — a handful of dust (v. 77: *"from a drop"*) — refuse prostration? The sun's *mustaqarr*-run shames prayerlessness: fire obeys, dust rebels. And the westward-rising prophecy (the sun's final *mustaqarr*-reversal) warns procrastinators: a dawn will come when *"belief will not benefit a soul that believed not before"* (6:158) — the door of repentance closes at the sun's reversal. Run (v. 20) while the sun runs eastward; when it reverses, running ends.

**Running Ordered Races Under the Decreed Sun**

The verse orders the believer's days by the sun's example. *Run appointed rounds*: the sun runs *to* (*li-*) — purposively; schedule thy days purposively — dawn-prayer at sunrise's edge, noon-prayer at zenith, afternoon at decline, sunset at flaying (v. 37), night at darkness — five *mustaqarrs* (appointed settlings) of worship punctuating labour's run. The sun never skips its station; skip not thine. *Prostrate beneath thy Throne-moments*: the sun prostrates under the Throne (ḥadīth) — prostrate thou under heaven's gaze in every prayer, seeking *permission* (success, *tawfīq*) for each day's rising. Begin tasks as the sun begins days: by leave sought (*bismillah*, *duʿāʾ*) and obedience rendered. *Measure by Might-and-Knowledge*: entrust thy *taqdīr* (decree) to *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* — thy lifespan's orbit, provision's stations, trials' solstices are *measured* as finely as the sun's. Anxiety is practical denial of *taqdīr*; trust (*tawakkul*) its affirmation. The Prophet ﷺ: *"Know that what missed thee was never to hit thee, and what hit thee was never to miss thee"* (Abū Dāwūd 4699; al-Tirmidhī 2516, from Ibn ʿAbbās). *Prepare for the western dawn*: repent before the sun's reversal (6:158) — each sunrise is respite, each sunset a warning. For the verse's final mercy is its *daily* witness: the sun *runs* — visibly, punctually, worshipfully — 365 annual sermons on appointed running. Watch it run — *and run like it*: purposively (*li-mustaqarr*), worshipfully (prostrating), and trustfully (decreed by Might-and-Knowledge). The sun runs to its dwelling; run thou to thine (vv. 55–58) — the Garden where suns need not run, for the Light (24:35) never sets.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:39

> **And for the moon, We have decreed mansions, till it returns like an old palm stalk.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qaddarnāhu Manāzil: The Moon's Decreed Mansions**

*"And for the moon, We have decreed mansions"* — *wa al-qamara qaddarnāhu manāzil* — *qaddarnā* (We decreed/measured — same root as v. 38's *taqdīr*): the moon's journey is *measured* by Might-and-Knowledge. *Manāzil* (mansions, stations, way-stops): the twenty-eight lunar stations (*manāzil al-qamar*) — nightly lodgings through which the moon travels across a lunar month, known to Arab astronomers (and all navigators) by name and star. Each night the moon *alights* at a new mansion — crescent-thin at one, half at another, full (*badr*) at midpoint, waning after — a celestial caravan-route as regular as the sun's (v. 38) yet *richer in phases*: the sun preaches *steadfastness* (same blaze daily), the moon *transformation* (waxing-waning yet unfailing). The Qur'an assigns the mansions a purpose:

> *"And determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning [of time]."* (Qur'an 10:5)

— and specifically worship's calendar:

> *"They ask thee about the new moons. Say, 'They are markers of time for mankind and for the ḥajj.'"* (Qur'an 2:189)

Ramadan's fast, Shawwāl's feast, ḥajj's standing, zakāh's year, waiting-periods' months — Islam's *sacred time* is moon-measured. The *manāzil* are thus not astronomy's trivia but devotion's timetable: God mansions the moon *that* men may worship on schedule. Lunar regularity is mercy's punctuality.

**Ḥattā ʿĀda ka-al-ʿUrjūn al-Qadīm: Thin as an Old Palm Stalk**

*"Till it returns like an old palm stalk"* — *ḥattā ʿāda ka-al-ʿurjūni al-qadīm* — *ʿurjūn* (date-cluster stalk: the curved stem from which dates hang), *qadīm* (old, withered, dried): after fullness, the moon thins nightly until it curves — yellowish, slender, bent — exactly like a dried palm-stalk after its fruit is stripped (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī: resembling the crescent "in shape, width, and color"). The simile's genius is *domestic*: Arabia's commonest sight (every palm-grove floor littered with *ʿarājīn*) illustrates heaven's subtlest phase. The Wise Qur'an (v. 2) teaches astronomy through agriculture — the farmer understands the moon by his compost, the astronomer by his tables; both by God's *qaddarnā* (decreeing). *ʿĀda* (returns) implies cyclicality: the moon *returns* to stalk-thinness monthly — death-like, yet unfailingly reborn as new crescent (*hilāl*). The monthly death-and-return is resurrection's *nearest* emblem after sleep (v. 37): the moon *dies* (invisible, *muḥāq*) and *rises* (crescent) twelve times yearly before deniers who doubt rising once (v. 78). Al-Rāzī notes the parallel's precision: stalk-thinness precedes disappearance as old age (v. 68: *"We cause him to regress in creation"*) precedes death — yet disappearance precedes reappearance in both (moon and man). Watch the month's end — and believe the world's.

**Sun and Moon: The Twin Witnesses Against Worshipping Either**

Verses 38–39 pair sun (blazing runner) with moon (phased mansions) — and the pairing is polemical: both were worshipped (Sabaeans, moon-cults, sun-cults; Quraysh's *al-Lāt* linked lunarly). The Qur'an forbids both cults in one stroke:

> *"Prostrate not to the sun nor to the moon, but prostrate to God Who created them, if it is Him that you worship."* (Qur'an 41:37)

The argument from *manāzil* (mansions) is devastating to moon-worship: a god does not *lodge* nightly at stations appointed by Another, thin to stalk-fragility, vanish utterly, and return by Another's *qaddarnā* (decreeing). Neediness (of stations, phases, permission) contradicts divinity; the moon's monthly humiliation (full → stalk → nothing → crescent) is its perpetual *shahādah* (testimony): *there is no god but the Measurer*. Likewise the sun's prostration (v. 38, ḥadīth). The two luminaries thus preach *tawḥīd* (Oneness) by *obedience*: their punctuality is their piety, their phases their profession. Modernity's subtler luminaries-worship (astrology's moon-anxieties, "solar energies," horoscope-guidance) falls under the same *manāzil*-refutation: the moon *mansions* by decree — it decrees nothing. Seekers of lunar guidance invert the verse: the moon is *qaddarnāhu* (decreed-for), not *qādir* (decreeing). Guidance comes from the Mansion-Maker (the Wise Qur'an, v. 2), never the mansion-keeper.

**The Moon's Phases as Parables of Faith's Seasons**

Beyond cosmology, the moon's mansions console spiritually: faith, like the moon, *phases*. The believer's heart waxes (Ramadan's full-moon blaze, ḥajj's radiance, new-knowledge crescents) and wanes (post-season dimming, trial-thinning, sin-eclipsing) — yet waning is *mansion*, not extinction: the moon at stalk-thinness is still *moon*, still *decreed*, still *returning*. Despair at spiritual thinness misunderstands *manāzil*: thinness is a *station*, appointed, temporary, followed by crescent-renewal. The Prophet ﷺ normalized the phasing:

> *"Faith wears out in one's heart as a garment wears out; so ask God to renew faith in your hearts."* (al-Ḥākim, al-Mustadrak 5; al-Ṭabarānī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Ḥākim, ḥasan by al-Albānī)

— renewal (*tajdīd*) is the crescent after *muḥāq* (disappearance). Practices of phasing: at fullness (spiritual *badr*), thank and share (teach, give, invite — full moons illuminate others); at thinning, persist in *manzil*-duties (obligatory prayers, minimal *adhkār* — the stalk-phase moon still *runs* its station); at darkness, await the crescent (repent, return, trust *qaddarnā* — disappearance precedes renewal by decree). Never mistake mansion for abandonment: the Mansion-Maker tracks every station (v. 76: *"We know what they hide and what they disclose"*). The moon has never once failed to return; nor has a returning believer (39:53: *"despair not"*). Phase — but never vanish by choice.

**Living by the Mansions: Calendar, Worship, and Wonder**

The verse orders lunar living in three rhythms. *Keep the calendar*: sight crescents (*hilāl*) with the Prophet's ﷺ supplication — *"O God, bring it upon us with security, faith, safety, and Islam"* (al-Tirmidhī 3451, from Ṭalḥah; graded ḥasan) — fast Ramadan by moon, feast by moon, perform ḥajj by moon (2:189), pay zakāh by lunar year. Lunar worship detaches devotion from seasons (Ramadan cycles through all) — universality by *manāzil*. *Worship the phases*: pray *kusūf* (eclipse-prayer) at eclipses — the Prophet ﷺ: *"The sun and moon are two signs among God's signs; they eclipse for no one's death or life. When you see them, pray"* (al-Bukhārī 1044; Muslim 901, from al-Mughīrah) — converting cosmic awe into prostration. Rise for night-prayer under the moon's mansion-light (73:6), reciting Yā Sīn's moon-verse *to* the moon. *Wonder monthly*: watch a full lunar cycle once yearly with *manāzil*-attention — crescent → half → full → half → stalk → absence → crescent — reading each phase as resurrection-catechism (death-and-return, v. 51) and faith-parable (waxing-waning, above). Show children the stalk-moon (*ʿurjūn*-night) and ask: *shall He who returns this return thee?* For the verse's final mercy is its *monthly* frequency: twelve annual death-and-returns witnessed gratis, each crying *qaddarnāhu* (We decreed it). The denier (v. 78) demands resurrection's proof; the moon performs it monthly. Look up — *and believe*.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:40

> **It befits not the sun to overtake the moon, nor the night to outstrip the day. Each glides in an orbit.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Lā al-Shamsu Yanbaghī Lahā: The Cosmic Propriety**

*"It befits not the sun"* — *lā al-shamsu yanbaghī lahā* — *yanbaghī* (befits, is proper, is given): the sun is *not given* overtaking — cosmic *propriety*, ordained etiquette. *An tudrika al-qamar* (to overtake/catch the moon): the sun, swifter-seeming, mightier-blazing, is *forbidden* the chase — it shall not catch, eclipse (by collision), or usurp the moon's mansion-time (v. 39). *"Nor the night to outstrip the day"* (*wa lā al-laylu sābiqu al-nahār*): night shall not *precede* (outrun, arrive before) day — succession stays sequenced: day, then night (v. 37's flaying), then day — no leapfrogging, no double-nights, no day's skipping. The verse legislates *cosmic manners*: luminaries queue, seasons wait turns, cycles hold order. The Qur'an's parallel confirms:

> *"He created the night and the day, the sun and the moon, each gliding in an orbit."* (Qur'an 21:33)

Violation would unhinge worship (prayer-times by sun, months by moon — overlap/confusion would chaos the calendar) and life (crops by seasons, travel by stars). *Yanbaghī* thus reveals creation as *polite*: powers restrained, jurisdictions honoured, rebellions unattempted. If fire (sun) and darkness (night) observe propriety toward fellows, shall *thou* — dust (v. 77) — overtake thy brother's right, outstrip thy sister's turn? Cosmic *adab* (etiquette) shames human rudeness: the heavens queue; men jostle. Learn manners from orbits.

**Kullun Fī Falakin Yasbaḥūn: Each Gliding in an Orbit**

*"Each glides in an orbit"* — *kullun fī falakin yasbaḥūn* — *falak* (orbit, sphere, celestial track): each luminary's *appointed lane*. *Yasbaḥūn* (glide, swim, float): motion as *swimming* — effortless, buoyant, graceful — the verb of fish in water, applied to suns in space. The plural (*yasbaḥūn*, masculine plural — though sun/moon/night/day include feminines) personifies: luminaries as *swimmers*, almost *worshippers* (22:18's prostration) — conscious obedience in cosmic lanes. *"Each"* (*kullun*): no drifter, no lane-changer, no collision — traffic-law sustancia universal. Modern astronomy deepens the wonder without disturbing the wording: sun's galactic swim (220 km/s), moon's terrestrial orbit (27.3 days), Earth's rotation (night-day succession) — nested *falaks* within *falaks*, each *yasbaḥ* (gliding) collisionlessly for eons. The verse's verbs (*yanbaghī*, befits; *yasbaḥūn*, glide) are *teleological-etiquetted*, not mechanically-exhaustive: revelation asserts *ordered obedience*, leaving mechanism's mathematics to inquiry. The order's *purpose* the Qur'an states: navigation and time (10:5: *"that you might know the number of years and the reckoning"*; 16:16: *"by the stars they guide themselves"*; 2:189: new moons for ḥajj) — orbits as *mercy*: without measurable lanes, men "would lose their bearings and not be able to continue their lives" (source commentary) — no prayer-times, no Ramadan, no ḥajj, no harvests, no voyages. *Falak* is guidance's infrastructure; *yasbaḥūn* its maintenance. Thank the Lane-Maker (v. 35: *a-fa-lā yashkurūn*).

**Cosmic Order as Resurrection's Argument and Judgment's Preview**

The verse argues doubly for the sūrah's themes. *For resurrection*: order's Maintainer can re-order — He who lanes suns collisionlessly for billions of circuits can re-lane souls to bodies in one Cry (v. 53). Precision's God is not perplexed by reassembly (v. 79: *"He knows every creation"*). The denier (v. 78: *"who revives bones, decayed?"*) is answered by orbits: *the Lane-Keeper*. *For judgment*: present order previews final *reordering* — for order *will* be suspended at the Hour: *"when the sun is folded, when the stars fall"* (81:1–2); *"the sun and the moon are brought together"* (75:9) — overtaking *then* permitted, outstripping *then* commanded. Today's *lā yanbaghī* (befits not) implies tomorrow's *qad jāʾa* (has come): propriety's suspension *is* the Hour's signature. Believers read orbits as *amānah* (trust): lanes hold *until* the Appointment (*ajal musammā*, 39:5: *"each running for a term appointed"*) — then lanes fold, swimmers strand, and *muḥḍarūn* (vv. 32, 53) assemble. Every punctual sunrise is thus a *postponement-notice* (mercy's extension, v. 44: *"enjoyment for a while"*) and a *punctuality-lesson* (judgment keeps appointments as surely as dawn). The cosmos runs on time; the Cry (v. 49) will too. Be ready as orbits are regular.

**The Ethics of Orbits: Jurisdiction, Pace, and Non-Encroachment**

*Yanbaghī* (befits) makes the verse an ethics-textbook in cosmic dress. *Jurisdiction*: sun-given-day, moon-given-night (v. 39's mansions), night-given-rest, day-given-livelihood (78:10–11) — each luminary *stays* in office. Human offices likewise: parent's, ruler's, scholar's, spouse's — *"each"* (*kullun*) gliding in *his* orbit (*falak*). Encroachment (sun *overtaking* moon = ruler usurping scholar, husband usurping wife, state usurping conscience) violates *yanbaghī* — cosmic impropriety in social form. The Prophet ﷺ orbited offices: *"Each of you is a shepherd, each responsible for his flock"* (al-Bukhārī 893; Muslim 1829, from Ibn ʿUmar) — shepherds in lanes, none overtaking. *Pace*: sun does not rush to catch moon, night does not hurry past day — creation *unhurried*, though *unpausing*. Human haste (overtaking competitors unethically, outstripping due process, rushing provisions anxiously) breaks orbit-grace. *Sabr* (patience) is orbital ethics: glide (*yasbaḥ*), do not grasp. *Non-collision*: eons without crash — the verse's silent miracle. Human orbits collide daily (traffic-rage, office-politics, marital crashes) for lack of *falak*-discipline. Keep lanes: rights (*ḥuqūq*) as orbit-walls, courtesy as spacing, forgiveness as collision-avoidance. The heavens' accident-free record shames earth's pileups — and offers the remedy: *befittingness* (*yanbaghī*) as life's traffic-code. Glide; do not grab.

**Swimming Appointed Lanes in Daily Life**

The verse assigns orbital practices. *Find thy falak*: discern thy appointed lanes — vocation (work-orbit), family (home-orbit), worship (mosque-orbit), community (service-orbit) — and *glide* (excellence with ease, *itqān* without anxiety), not drift (neglect) nor career (into others' lanes). The Prophet's ﷺ day orbited: dawn-mosque, forenoon-family/work, noon-mosque, afternoon-community, night-household/prayer — lanes kept, swum gracefully. *Keep celestial time*: order worship by sun-moon (five prayers by sun's stations, months by moon's — vv. 38–39) — punctuality as orbit-imitation. The dawn-prayer's regulars are dawn's fellows; Ramadan's fasters, the moon's companions. *Refuse cosmic sins*: overtaking (usurping others' rights/successes — backbiting to outshine, cheating to outrank) and outstripping (preempting God's timing — despairing of provision, rushing marriage/judgment). Trust *taqdīr* (v. 38: decree of Might-and-Knowledge): thy lane's length, thy swim's speed, thy term's appointment are *measured*. *Contemplate the night-sky monthly*: step out where stars show, recite vv. 37–40 to their subjects, watch moon-in-mansion (v. 39) and stars-in-lanes — *kullun fī falakin yasbaḥūn* performed live. Teach children orbit-ethics under orbits: *see — none collides; be like them*. For the verse's final mercy is its *nightly* visibility: the lanes glow overhead gratis, preaching propriety to all who look up. The denier demands signs (v. 46: yet *"turned away"*); the sky *is* the sign — gliding, queued, on time. Look up — *and glide like them*, toward thy *mustaqarr* (dwelling, v. 38) — the Garden where lanes need no lamps (24:35's Light suffices).

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:41

> **And a sign unto them is that We carried their progeny in the full-laden Ark.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyatun Lahum: The Ark as Ancestral Sign**

*"And a sign unto them"* — *wa āyatun lahum* — the signs-sequence continues (earth, vv. 33–35; night-sun-moon, vv. 37–40) with history's vessel: the Ark. *"That We carried their progeny"* (*annā ḥamalnā dhurriyyatahum*) — *ḥamalnā* (We carried): divine carriage — God *bore* them; *dhurriyyah* (progeny, offspring, descendants): their *seed*, their future. The majority (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī, al-Zamakhsharī) read *dhurriyyah* as forefathers-with-descendants: Noah's Ark carried the *ancestors* in whose loins all later generations (including *"their"* — the Makkans') lay — "by which God preserved one generation of humanity and thereby all of humanity." Every reader is thus Ark-cargo by ancestry: *thou* wast carried, in thy forefathers' loins, over drowning. *"In the full-laden Ark"* (*fī al-fulki al-mashḥūn*) — *fulk* (ark, ship — same word for Noah's vessel, 11:37; 23:27, and all ships, v. 42); *mashḥūn* (full-laden, freighted, crammed): pairs of every kind (11:40), provisions, believers — buoyancy at capacity, yet unsunk by God's *ḥamalnā* (carrying). The sign's logic: thou *existest* because God *carried* — existence itself is salvage. The resurrection-denier (v. 78) floats on his proof: the Carrier of arks carries graves' cargo to judgment (vv. 32, 53) with equal ease.

**Noah's Flood as the Sūrah's Second Parable-Town**

The Ark-verse replays the town-parable (vv. 13–30) in primeval key, and the parallels preach. *Warners rejected*: Noah warned 950 years (29:14) — the town's three messengers (v. 14) in marathon. *Mockery*: *"whenever chiefs passed, they mocked him"* (11:38) — verse 30's *yastahziʾūn* (mocking) verbatim. *A remnant believing*: Noah's few (11:40: *"none believed with him save a few"*) — the runner's one (v. 20). *Sudden destruction*: flood's fountains-and-doors (54:11–12) — the Cry's instant (v. 29: *ṣayḥah wāḥidah*). *The righteous extracted*: Ark's boarding (*"embark therein,"* 11:40) — the martyr's *udkhul* (v. 26: *enter*). The Makkans knew Noah's story (it opens the Qur'an's destroyer-catalogue, 7:59–64; 11:25–48; 71:1–28); this verse makes it *personal*: *thy* progeny (ancestors) rode; *thy* existence is flood-surplus. Al-Rāzī notes the verse's anti-exceptionalism (v. 31's *kam*, how many): the flood drowned *all* but cargo — universality of judgment previewing universality of arraignment (*jamīʿ... muḥḍarūn*, vv. 32, 53). Noah's flood is the town's Cry writ global; the Cry, the flood writ sudden; the Hour (v. 49), both writ final. Three destructions, one moral: *board* (believe, obey, embark) before waters/Cries rise. The Ark-then (wood, v. 41) and the ark-now (faith, v. 11's following) save alike; the flood-then (water) and the Fire-after (vv. 63–64) drown alike. *Embark* — the verse's silent imperative.

**"Their Progeny": The Womb-Reading and Its Tenderness**

A minority (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī) reads *dhurriyyah* (progeny) as *children* carried in mothers' wombs — wombs likened to *fulk mashḥūn* (full-laden arks): each pregnant mother an ark, each fetus flood-precious cargo, each birth a disembarkation (*"We settle in the wombs what We will,"* 22:5). The image's tenderness is exquisite: every man has *twice* been carried — ancestrally in Noah's Ark, personally in his mother's — and both carriages were God's (*ḥamalnā*, We carried). The womb-ark also answers resurrection-doubt (v. 78) intimately: He who *carried* thee (helpless cargo) through birth's flood (waters breaking, passage crushing) into life's shore *carries* thee through death's strait into eternity's shore. Birth is resurrection's first rehearsal; every birthday, flood-survival's anniversary. The two readings (Ark-ancestors / womb-children) harmonize as carriage-past and carriage-present: *then* forefathers in wood, *now* children in flesh — God's *ḥaml* (carrying) continuous across millennia. The ungrateful (v. 35: *a-fa-lā yashkurūn*) forget both carriages; the thankful remember conception-to-cradle as Ark-to-Ararat. Thou art carried still: by earth through space (v. 40's orbits), by body through years (v. 68's regress), by mercy through sins (v. 11's forgiveness) — cargo cherished, not freight forgotten.

**The Full-Laden Ark and the Full-Laden Earth**

*Mashḥūn* (full-laden) invites reflection on *capacity*: the Ark brimmed — yet floated. Its buoyancy despite burden images divine carrying's nature: God bears full loads lightly. Earth's ark likewise brims — eight billion passengers, plus beasts, plus provision — yet glides (*yasbaḥ*, v. 40) unsunk through space's flood. Thy life's ark brims — duties, debts, griefs, sins — yet floats by *ḥamalnā* (We carry). The Qur'an generalizes the buoyancy:

> *"Hast thou not considered that God has made whatsoever is on the earth subservient unto you, and the ships run upon the sea by His command? And He holds back the heaven from falling upon the earth save by His leave."* (Qur'an 22:65)

— holding-back (*yumsik*) as perpetual *ḥaml* (carrying). Despair whispers *overload* (too sinful to float, too burdened to continue); the Ark answers *mashḥūn-yet-afloat*: fullness never sank God's cargo — only *unboarding* drowned (Noah's son: *"I shall take refuge on a mountain,"* 11:43 — self-salvation's reef). Board (faith), stay boarded (perseverance), and trust buoyancy to the Carrier. The flood-then lifted the laden; the Fire-after (vv. 63–64) seeks the unboarded. *Mashḥūn* (full) is no barrier to *maḥmūl* (carried) — fullness *qualifies* for carrying, emptiness (of faith) disqualifies. Come laden — *"though thy sins reach the clouds"* (al-Tirmidhī 3540, ḥadīth qudsī) — and be carried.

**Boarding Arks: Practices of the Carried**

The verse commissions ark-practices. *Board the faith-ark*: testify (*āmantu*, v. 25), follow (v. 21), fear unseen (v. 11) — boarding's planks. Inspect boarding daily: am I *in* (obedient) or *on the mountain* (Noah's son's self-refuge — wealth? intellect? tribe?). *Honour carriers*: mothers (womb-arks — *"his mother bore him in weakness upon weakness,"* 31:14) — serve, thank, obey (in good), and pray for them; fathers, teachers, and all who *ḥamala* (carried) thee toward God. Ingratitude to carriers is ingratitude to the Carrier. *Carry others*: be ark for the drowning — rescue the sinning (counsel), the despairing (hope, 39:53), the ignorant (teach), the hungry (feed, v. 47) — *"whoever relieves a believer's distress, God relieves his on Resurrection Day"* (Muslim 2699, from Abū Hurayrah). Parenthood is ark-building: each child a *dhurriyyah* (progeny) entrusted for carrying to God's shore. *Travel thankfully*: every voyage (ship, plane, car — v. 42's *"the like thereof"*) rehearses the Ark — board with *bismillah* (*"in God's name be its course and its anchorage,"* 11:41 — Noah's boarding-duʿāʾ), ride thankfully, disembark humbly. For the verse's final mercy is its *ancestry*: thou desc Carr from *carried* ones — survival is thy heritage, carriage thy habit. The flood did not end thy line; the Cry (v. 49) need not end thy soul; the Fire (v. 64) need not board thee. *Boarded* (in faith) thou floatest — to Ararat-then, to Garden-after (vv. 55–58). *Ḥamalnā* (We carried) — past. *Naḥmilukum* (We carry you) — present. *Sa-naḥmilu* — to eternity's shore, God willing.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:42

> **And We created for them the like thereof upon which they ride.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa Khalaqnā Lahum Min Mithlihi: The Like Thereof**

*"And We created for them the like thereof"* — *wa khalaqnā lahum min mithlihi* — *We created* (*khalaqnā*): the Ark's successors are *creations*, not inventions — human shipwrights discover God's buoyancy-laws; camel-breeders receive God's desert-ships. *"The like thereof"* (*min mithlihi*): like the Ark (v. 41) — vessels that *carry* (v. 41's *ḥamalnā*) men over perils. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī, al-Zamakhsharī) unfold the like-ness in widening circles. Narrowest: *other ships* — every vessel after Noah's, from reed-rafts to galleons, Ark's offspring. Wider: *camels* — "ships of the desert" (*safīnat al-barr*) — Arabia's arks over sand-floods, carrying progeny (v. 41) through waterless wastes as the Ark through waterful. Widest: *all beasts of burden* (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī) — horses, mules, donkeys (16:8: *"horses, mules, and donkeys, that you may ride them"*) — and by extension (the verse's open wording invites it) *all riding-means*: carriages, trains, cars, planes — each a *mithl* (like) of the Ark in function (carrying over peril by God's leave). *"Upon which they ride"* (*mā yarkabūn* — *that which they ride*): riding (*rukūb*) as the human posture of trust — mounted, conveyed, dependent. The verse's *lahum* (for them): conveyances are *gifts*, tailored (*for* men) — the Carrier (v. 41) provides carriages. Every saddle is Ark-descended; every voyage, flood-echoed.

**Ships and Camels: Twin Arks of Sea and Sand**

The classical pairing (ships + camels) deserves savouring, for Arabia lived it. *Ships*: Quraysh were caravan-merchants, but Yemen's and Syria's ports ringed them; the Red Sea bore Abyssinian arks (the Muslims' first Hijrah rode ships to Abyssinia); the Prophet ﷺ likened his community's salvation to ships (the *fulk*-image recurs in supplications). Ships preach *tawakkul* (trust): planks between men and abyss, no shore in sight, storms unmasterable — sailors learn monotheism viscerally (*"when waves cover them like canopies, they call upon God,"* 31:32; cf. vv. 43's drowning-warning, 10:22–23; 17:66–67). *Camels*: the Bedouin's Ark — water-storing hull, sand-plowing keel, star-steered, weeks without port. The Qur'an singles the camel for contemplation:

> *"Have they not considered the camel, how it was created?"* (Qur'an 88:17)

— creation's contemplation-emblem. Camel-and-ship together testify *providence's tailoring*: sea-peril answered by floaters, sand-peril by plodders — God *matches* carriage to country. The Makkans, camel-masters, were meant to feel the verse personally: *thy* caravan's hundred kneeling arks (dawn-departures for Syria/Yemen) are *khalaqnā lahum* (We created for them) — not tribal heritage but heaven's loan. Modernity's arks (steel ships, jetliners) extend the tailoring: sky-peril answered by wings. Each age rides *mithlihi* (Ark's like); each age owes *shukr* (thanks, v. 35).

**"We Created": Technology as Divine Creation**

The verse's *khalaqnā* (We created) founds revelation's philosophy of technology: human artifacts are *divine creations through human hands*. Men *assemble* (planks, steel, circuits); God *creates* (buoyancy, combustion, lift — the laws assembled-things obey). The Qur'an states the metaphysics:

> *"And God created you and what you make."* (Qur'an 37:96)

— artisans and artifacts alike *makhlūq* (created). The implications humble and honour: *humble* — no inventor creates ex nihilo; patents are discoveries' certificates, not creations' titles. Boast (*"my* ship, *my* design, *my* genius") forgets *khalaqnā*. *Honour* — craftsmanship *participates* in creation: the shipwright continues Noah (11:37: *"construct the Ark under Our eyes"* — the first divinely-supervised shipyard), the engineer extends the Ark-line. Every halal conveyance-built is *ṣadaqah jāriyah*-potential (v. 12: ongoing charity — brucks, roads, vehicles conveying good). The verse also warns technologists (v. 43's sequel: *"if We will, We drown them"*): conveyances *obey* (float, fly) by leave — and *fail* by leave. Unsinkables sink (Titanic's hubris: "God Himself couldn't sink her" — the sea answered); uncrashables crash. *Khalaqnā* (We created) reserves *nuqdir* (We decree): ride thankfully (v. 35), not arrogantly. The Ark floated by *ḥamalnā* (v. 41: We carried), not carpentry — Noah the shipwright knew; modern shipwrights must relearn.

**Riding (Rukūb) as Worship's Posture**

*"Upon which they ride"* (*mā yarkabūn*) elevates *riding* to devotion's image: the rider — elevated, conveyed, trusting mount/vehicle/laws he did not make — postures the believer's relation to providence. Revelation ritualizes riding: the boarding-supplication — *"Glory be to Him who made this subservient to us, though we could not match it, and truly unto our Lord we are returning"* (43:13–14) — marries transport to eschatology (*returning*: every ride rehearses the Return, v. 83). The Prophet ﷺ rode thankfully (camel, horse, mule, donkey — all ridden humbly, often shared — Muʿādh behind him on a donkey, al-Bukhārī 5967), forbade animal-cruelty in riding (*"do not take animals' backs as pulpits"* — Abū Dāwūd 2567, from Abū Hurayrah; graded ṣaḥīḥ), and taught journey-mercies (shortened prayer, *jamʿ* — riding's *rukhaṣ*, concessions). *Rukūb* also images *trust's* psychology: the rider surrenders control (reins held loosely, pilot trusted, captain obeyed) — faith as *riding* providence: hold means (*asbāb*) lightly, trust the Carrier (v. 41) wholly. Anxiety is the passenger grabbing the wheel; *tawakkul*, the passenger praising the Driver. And eschatologically, riding previews mounting-toward-judgment: graves' disembarkation (v. 51: *rushing*), assemby's standing (vv. 32, 53: *muḥḍarūn*), Garden's conveyance (56:7–40's welcome). Ride now thankfully; be carried then gloriously.

**Boarding Gratefully in the Age of Wings**

The verse's *mithlihi* (like thereof) stretches to modernity's marvels — and its disciplines. *Board with remembrance*: every car-ignition, train-mounting, plane-boarding — 43:13–14's *duʿāʾ* (*subḥāna... wa innā ilā rabbinā la-munqalibūn*) — transforming commute into contemplation, flight into faith. Teach children: *this plane is Noah's Ark's grandchild*. *Ride mercifully*: maintain vehicles (trusts), drive gently (no road-*isrāf*, prodigality — v. 19's *musrifūn* includes reckless drivers), share rides (carrying others = *ḥaml*-charity), honour animals ridden (feed, rest, no overloading — the Prophet's ﷺ weeping-camel ḥadīth, Abū Dāwūd 2549). *Travel purposefully*: journey for good (kinship, knowledge, worship, livelihood-halal) — miles as *āthār* (traces, v. 12: footsteps recorded); avoid *mithlihi*-misuse (vehicles for sin, extravagance, showing-off — Ark's like debased). *Trust the Carrier over carriages*: inspect means (mechanic, weather, safety — *asbāb* commanded), then board with *tawakkul* (Carrier trusted) — fear neither turbulence (v. 43's drowning is *bi-idhnihi*, by His leave) nor delay (the Ark waited months; arrivals are *muqaddar*, decreed). For the verse's final mercy is its *lahum* (for them): conveyances are *for* thee — gifts, not entitlements; loans, not possessions. The Ark was *given*; ships are *given*; camels *given*; wings *given*. Ride — *and thank* (v. 35's *a-fa-lā yashkurūn*). The Carrier carried progeny (v. 41); He carries thee — over seas, sands, skies, and (God willing) over the Sirāṭ's abyss to the Garden's shore (vv. 55–58). *Board thankfully; arrive gloriously.*

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:43

> **And if We will, We drown them, such that they would have none to call upon and would not be saved,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa In Nashaʾ Nughriqhum: Drowning at Will**

*"And if We will, We drown them"* — *wa in nashaʾ nughriqhum* — *in nashaʾ* (if We will): drowning suspended on will — the sea's *permission* to kill, revocably granted. *Nughriqhum* (We drown them): God *drowns* — agency undisguised: waves are instruments, Will the agent. The verse follows carriage-gifts (vv. 41–42) with carriage's fragility: the Carrier (v. 41) can *un*-carry; the Creator of arks (v. 42) can recall buoyancy. *"This verse indicates that God could drown people at any moment"* (Ibn Kathīr): every voyage floats on *in nashaʾ* (if He wills otherwise) — hulls, skills, forecasts all *conditional*. The Qur'an exhibits the conditionality dramatically — sailors' cycle:

> *"When waves cover them like canopies, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion. But when He delivers them to land, some of them are moderate."* (Qur'an 31:32; cf. 10:22–23; 17:66–67 — remembering God at drowning's edge, forgetting at deliverance)

Storm-theology: peril *teaches* monotheism (all hands pray to One), safety *tempts* amnesia. The verse preempts amnesia: safety *is* suspended-drowning — each dry landing a *nughriqhum*-declined. The Ark floated (v. 41) by *nashāʾ* (He willed floating); Pharaoh sank (10:90) by *nashāʾ* (He willed sinking) — same sea, opposite wills. Riders (v. 42: *yarkabūn*) ride *will*, not wood. Remember — before waves canopy.

**Fa-Lā Ṣarīkha Lahum: "None to Call Upon" — The Cry Without Answer**

*"Such that they would have none to call upon"* — *fa-lā ṣarīkha lahum* — *ṣarīkh* (helper/cry: the one who answers screams, or the scream's answer): *lā ṣarīkha* — no responder, no rescue, no coastguard celestial. Mid-ocean, storm-risen, hull-breached: satellite-phones dead, distress-flares unseen — *lā ṣarīkha*. The alternative rendering ("none would call upon *them*" — they lie forgotten, unmourned) deepens dread: drowned *and* disregarded — supported by the forgotten-verses (7:51; 9:67; 20:126; 32:14; 45:34; 59:19 — *"they forgot God, so He forgot them"*). Both senses converge: drowning-by-will isolates — from rescue *and* remembrance. The idols (vv. 23, 74) are *ṣarīkh*-tested here: mid-storm, sailors jettison gods-first (Jonah's shipmates cast lots, then cargo, then called each his god — Jonah 1:5 — futilely) — false *ṣarīkhs* fail precisely when *ṣarīkh* is needed. Only the Drowner can un-drown: *"Is He who answers the distressed when he calls upon Him?"* (27:62) — *yes*, when called *sincerely* (31:32's *mukhliṣīn*). The verse thus evangelizes sailors (and all in peril): thy *ṣarīkh*-lessness proves gods'-worthlessness — call the Drowner; He alone lifeboats. Every *mayday* unanswered by men is heaven's invitation to the *Mayday*-Answerer.

**Wa Lā Hum Yunqadhūn: "And Would Not Be Saved"**

*"And would not be saved"* — *wa lā hum yunqadhūn* — *yunqadhūn* (rescued, delivered, extracted): passive — no rescuing *happens* to them. Double negation (*lā ṣarīkha... wa lā... yunqadhūn*) seals every exit: none answers, none extracts. The Qur'an's drowning-gallery confirms will's finality: Pharaoh's host — *"We drowned them all"* (8:54; 17:103); Noah's deniers — *"they were drowned"* (11:43–44; 71:25). Against these, deliverances shine as *exceptions-by-mercy*: Noah's cargo (v. 41), Moses' crossers (26:63–67), Jonah's ship (10:98's people spared by last-minute faith). The pattern: drowning is *default* for defiance (*nughriqhum* at will), deliverance *gift* for turning (*yunqadhūn* negated here, granted there). Theologically, the verse states *omnipotence's* corollary: creation's gifts (vv. 41–42) are *loans*, recallable — buoyancy, breath, wealth, health all *in-nashaʾ* (if-We-will) held. Practically, it states *gratitude's* ground (v. 35: *a-fa-lā yashkurūn*): every undrowned voyage is *yunqadh*-by-omission — salvation by *not*-sinking. Count deliverances: each flight landed, each illness survived, each night slept through (v. 37's lesser death unsealed) — a *lā nughriq* (We-did-not-drown) to thank. The unthankful (v. 35) mistake omission for entitlement; the thankful, for mercy. *Which art thou?*

**Life Always in God's Hands: The Verse's Pastoral Center**

The source commentary names the verse's heart: "a reminder that one's life is always in God's Hands." *Always*: not in storms only, but still waters (heart-attacks in calm cabins), not at sea only, but ashore (v. 49's Cry *"while they dispute"* — drowning on dry land, as it were). The *in nashaʾ* (if We will) universalizes: *every* breath is buoyancy — willed, loaned, recallable. The Prophet ﷺ lived the universal: boarding-supplications (43:13–14), journey-prayers, storm-duʿās (*"O God, around us, not upon us"* — al-Bukhārī 1014), yet complete *tawakkul* (trust) — fearing Will, trusting Mercy. The verse cures two opposite diseases: *recklessness* (voyaging/venturing/living as if unsinkable — the Titanic-syndrome, v. 42's warning) and *anxiety* (dreading every wave as willed-drowning — forgetting v. 44's *"mercy from Us"*). Recklessness denies *nughriqhum* (We drown); anxiety denies *nashaʾ* (Our *willing* — wise, merciful, measured). The believer's seamanship: prepare (means, skill, caution — *asbāb*), board (with *bismillah*, 11:41), trust (the Carrier, v. 41), and accept (wreck-or-landing as *khayr*, good — *"wondrous is the believer's affair,"* Muslim 2999). Seas obey; sailors trust; arrivals are decreed. *In nashaʾ* — and He wills wisely.

**Floating on Will: Practices of the Perpetually Rescued**

The verse ritualizes peril-awareness. *Before voyaging*: inspect means (roadworthy, seaworthy, airworthy — trusts), settle obligations (debts, wills, forgiveness — v. 29's no-bequest warning), supplicate boarding (11:41; 43:13–14), entrust family (to the Carrier). Travel *prepared-to-drown* (spiritually ready) yet *hoping-to-land* (optimism, *faʾl*). *In peril*: call *sincerely* (31:32's *mukhliṣīn* — One God, no partners, no pride) — storms are *tawḥīd*-tutors; pass their exam. The Prophet's ﷺ storm-counsel: behold thunder/lightning with *tasbīḥ* and *duʿāʾ*, not panic or photography. *After deliverance*: disembark thankfully — *land* as worship: *ḥamdalah* (praise), *ṣadaqah* (charity — deliverance's zakāh), vow-fulfillment (storm-promises kept ashore — 10:23 warns vow-breakers), and *memory* (forget not the Canopy-waves — 17:67's amnesiacs *"turned away"*). *Ashore, daily*: treat each day as voyage-delivered — morning's waking a *lā-nughriq* (un-drowned from night's sea, v. 37), evening's return a landing. The bed is *fulk* (ark), sleep the sea, dawn the shore — board nightly with *duʿāʾ*, land daily with *ḥamd*. For the verse's final mercy is its *conditional* (*in*, if): drowning is *if*-willed, not *always*-willed — and the Will (v. 44) *wills* mostly mercy (*"save as a mercy from Us"*). Thou floatest — by *if* un-triggered, by mercy extended. Float thankfully — *and rescue others* (the drowning in sin, despair, ignorance — throw faith-ropes). The Drowner-at-will is the Deliverer-by-mercy. *Call Him — before waves canopy, while shores await.*

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:44

> **Save as a mercy from Us and an enjoyment for a while.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Illā Raḥmatan Minnā: The Exception That Explains Survival**

*"Save as a mercy from Us"* — *illā raḥmatan minnā* — *illā* (save, except): the *only* barrier between sailors (v. 43) and drowning is *mercy* — not hulls, skills, or forecasts. *Raḥmah* (mercy): God's *withholding* of deserved sinking. *Minnā* (from Us): sourced exclusively heavenward — rescue's monopoly. The verse universalizes: *all* survival is *illā-raḥmah* (except-by-mercy) — the healthy undiseased by mercy, the wealthy unbankrupted by mercy, the sinful unseized by mercy (16:61: *"were God to take men for their wrongdoing, He would not leave upon it a single creature"*). The Qur'an's mercy-precedence stands behind: *"My mercy embraces all things"* (7:156); *"Thy Lord has prescribed mercy upon Himself"* (6:54). For the town's survivors (none — v. 29) mercy expired; for Makkah's hearers (still breathing) mercy extended — the verse *is* the extension, warning within respite. Al-Rāzī notes the *minnā* (from Us) excludes all co-saviours: idols (vv. 23, 74) rescue none; wealth ransoms none (v. 43: *lā ṣarīkha*, none answering); only *Us*. Every landing is *minnā*-signed; every dawn, *raḥmah*-stamped. The unthankful (v. 35) read landings as luck; the verse corrects the signature: *mercy, from Us*. Sign thy thanks accordingly.

**Wa Matāʿan Ilā Ḥīn: Enjoyment — For a While**

*"And an enjoyment for a while"* — *wa matāʿan ilā ḥīn* — *matāʿ* (enjoyment, provision, goods-for-use): life's pleasures as *travel-provisions*, not *home-furniture*. *Ilā ḥīn* (for a while, till a time): provision *expires* — at death (personal *ḥīn*), the Cry (cosmic *ḥīn*, v. 49), or judgment (final *ḥīn*, vv. 53–54). The Qur'an's *matāʿ*-theology is sustained: *"the enjoyment of this world is little"* (4:77); *"naught but the enjoyment of delusion"* (3:185; 57:20); contrasted with the Next (*khayr*, better — 3:14; 9:38; 13:26; 20:131; 28:60; 40:39; 42:36). For disbelievers, *ilā ḥīn* is *respite-for-increase* (of sin — 3:178: *"We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin, and theirs shall be a humiliating punishment"*) — mercy-as-test, not mercy-as-approval. For believers, *ilā ḥīn* is *respite-for-provisioning* (good deeds for the voyage-after) — mercy-as-opportunity. Same *matāʿ*, opposite uses: the wise pack (v. 12's *qaddamū*, sent-forth), the foolish picnic (v. 47's scoffing at spending). The verse's *ḥīn* (while) is pointedly vague — *how* long? Unknown (death's stealth, v. 49's suddenness) — vagueness as urgency: *a while* could end *while* reading this verse. *Enjoy* — but *provision* (pack!), for *ḥīn* expires, and *matāʿ* untransformed into *ajr* (reward, v. 11) evaporates. The Garden's *matāʿ* (v. 57: *"whatsoever they call for"*) never expires — trade expiry for eternity.

**Mercy and Enjoyment: The Two Faces of Respite**

The verse pairs *raḥmah* (mercy) with *matāʿ* (enjoyment) — respite's *cause* and *content*: *because* mercy, *therefore* enjoyment-continued. The pairing diagnoses every undrowned day: dawn (v. 37's raising) = *raḥmah*; breakfast (vv. 33–35's fruit) = *matāʿ*; voyage landed (vv. 41–43 un-triggered) = *raḥmah*; home reached = *matāʿ*. Life entire is *illā-raḥmatan-minnā-wa-matāʿan-ilā-ḥīn* (except-mercy-enjoyment-while) — existence as extended-clemency with catering. The Qur'an's respite-gallery confirms: Pharaoh respited till drowning (10:90–92 — respite for *proof*, then *seal*); Quraysh respited till Badr (8:32–33 — *"God would not punish them while thou art among them"* — the Prophet's ﷺ presence as *raḥmah*-umbrella); each soul respited till death (63:11: *"God never respites a soul when its term comes"*). Respite's *purpose* the sūrah states: repent-or-prove (v. 44's context — *"giving them time until death to repent or to prove their true nature"*). Every *ilā-ḥīn* (while) is examination-time: the paper (life) must be submitted at *ḥīn*'s end; blank papers (heedlessness, v. 6) and wrong answers (denial, v. 7) fail. Mercy grants *time*; enjoyment grants *means* (health, wealth, years); judgment grades *use*. *Use* the while — it is mercy, and it ends.

**Why the Merciful Drowns Some and Respites Others**

The verse (with v. 43) raises providence's hardest question: why do some drown (storms, quakes, Cries — v. 29's town) while others enjoy *ilā ḥīn*? Revelation's answer is multi-layered, never flippant. *Wisdom unshared*: *"He is not questioned concerning what He does, but they are questioned"* (21:23) — the Drowner owes no audit; faith trusts Wisdom beyond visibility. *Testing differentiated*: some tested by drowning (martyrs of sea/plague — the Prophet ﷺ: *"the drowned is a martyr,"* al-Bukhārī 2829; Muslim 1914 — drowning-as-honour for believers), others by respite (the respited tested *harder* — wealth, years, health as heavier trials than sudden death; 3:178's increase-in-sin). *Mercy disguised*: early death may *be* mercy (spared worse — 18:80–81's Khidr-youth), long enjoyment *trial* (more to answer — *"then you shall surely be questioned concerning bliss,"* 102:8). *Justice deferred*: drowning-then ≠ final-verdict — the drowned denier faces Fire-after (vv. 63–64), the drowned believer Garden-after (v. 55) — *ḥīn* (while) differs, *ḥisāb* (reckoning, v. 54) equalizes. The believer's response to disparity is neither envy (of the long-enjoying — their *ḥisāb* lengthens) nor despair (at the early-drowned — martyrdom may crown) but *use*: *my* while (whatever its length) spent packing (v. 12), thanking (v. 35), running (v. 20). *Ḥīn* is God's to set, mine to spend. Spend it — mercy-while lasts.

**Spending the While: Practices of the Respited**

The verse's *ilā ḥīn* (for a while) is a budget — spend it. *Budget time*: treat years/days/hours as *matāʿ* (provisions) with expiry — schedule eternity-yielding acts (prayer, Qur'an, charity, kinship, counsel) *before* expiry-consumables (entertainment, luxury, dispute — v. 49's *"while they dispute"*). The Prophet's ﷺ while-counsel: *"Take advantage of five before five: youth before old age, health before illness, wealth before poverty, free time before work, life before death"* (al-Ḥākim, al-Mustadrak 7846; graded ṣaḥīḥ). *Budget enjoyment*: enjoy *matāʿ* lawfully (food, family, rest, beauty — gifts, vv. 33–35) yet *lightly* (holding loosely — *"love what thou wilt, thou shalt part from it"* — al-Ḥākim, from Sahl; ḥasan) — enjoyment-as-provision (fuel for journey), not enjoyment-as-home (settling in transit-lounge). *Budget mercy*: since survival is *raḥmah* (mercy), *be* merciful — *"be merciful to earth's inhabitants"* (v. 27's ḥadīth) — mercy's receivers must be mercy's channels, or mercy may be recalled (the unmerciful servant, 39:53's context). Forgive debts, overlook slights, feed hungry (v. 47), visit sick — *raḥmah* reinvested multiplies. *Prepare for ḥīn's end*: will written (v. 29), debts cleared, repentance current (nightly *istighfār*), testimony fresh (v. 25's *āmantu*). For the verse's final mercy is its *illā* (except): drowning is *excepted* — *now*. The exception holds while this verse is read. Tomorrow's sea is unpromised; today's shore is mercy. *Enjoy* the shore — *provision* for the voyage — *thank* the Respiter (v. 35) — and *run* (v. 20) while *ḥīn* (while) remains. *Ilā ḥīn* — the clock ticks mercifully. Spend it — eternally.

---
## Sūrah Yā Sīn 36:45

> **And when it is said unto them, "Be mindful of that which is before you and of that which is behind you, that haply you may receive mercy."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wa Idhā Qīla Lahum: Counsel Offered, Response Omitted**

*"And when it is said unto them"* — *wa idhā qīla lahum* — *qīla* (it is said): the sayer unnamed (messengers, believers, the runner's heirs — every warner, v. 6); the *said-to* (*lahum*) named (the deniers). The verse reports counsel's *giving* but pointedly omits its *reception* — no "and they heeded" follows. The commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) supply the ellipsis: *"they turn away"* — the implied response, confirmed by the next verse (*"never did a sign come but they turned away,"* v. 46). The omission *is* the indictment: counsel falls into silence — no answer printable. Rhetorically, the gap invites the hearer to fill it — *what did they say?* — and the honest hearer, knowing Makkah (and himself), fills: *nothing; they walked away*. The Qur'an's counsel-formula (*idhā qīla* — "when it is said") recurs as heedlessness's litmus: *"when it is said to them, 'Believe as people believe,' they say, 'Shall we believe as fools believe?'"* (2:13); *"when it is said, 'Spend...,' they scoff"* (v. 47, next-but-one).
